Infinitely divisible priors for multivariate survival functions
Florian Brück

Abstract
This article introduces a novel framework for nonparametric priors on real-valued random vectors, which can be viewed as a multivariate generalization of neutral-to-the right priors. It is based on randomizing the exponent measure of a minimum-infinitely divisible random vector by an infinitely divisible random measure and naturally incorporates partially exchangeable data as well as exchangeable random vectors. We show how to construct hierarchical priors from simple building blocks and embed many models from Bayesian nonparametric survival analysis into our framework. The prior can concentrate on discrete or continuous distributions and other properties such as dependence, moments and moments of mean functionals are characterized. The posterior predictive distribution is derived in a general framework and is refined under some regularity conditions. In addition, a framework for the simulation from the posterior predictive distribution is provided, which is illustrated by an application to partially exchangeable data in a survival analysis context. As a byproduct, the construction of tractable infinitely divisible random measures is studied and the concept of subordination of homogeneous completely random measures by homogeneous completely random measures is extended to the subordination of homogeneous completely random measures by infinitely divisible random measures. This technique allows to create vectors of dependent infinitely divisible random measures with tractable Laplace transforms and serves as a general tool for the construction of tractable infinitely divisible random measures.



keywords
[class=MSC]
Primary 62G05
; secondary 60G57

keywords

Bayesian nonparametrics
, Infinitely divisible random measure
, Min-id distribution

1
Introduction

The Dirichlet process serves as the cornerstone of Bayesian nonparametrics. Since its introduction in
[
15
]
, significant efforts have been made to extend its underlying construction methods, leading to more flexible models for random probability measures and, consequently, more flexible frameworks for Bayesian analysis.
Essentially, these extensions can be categorized into three categories. The first type of extensions is based on the construction of the Dirichlet process as
\mu(\cdot)/\mu(\mathbb{Y})
, where
\mu
is a Gamma distributed completely random measure (CRM) on a Polish space
\mathbb{Y}
. Dropping the assumption of the Gamma distribution, the resulting random probability measures are known as normalized CRMs, which were introduced in
[
21
]
. This framework has been leveraged in many works due to its flexibility and analytical tractability of the posterior, see e.g.
[
8
,
3
,
26
,
1
]
for examples in clustering, topic modeling and density estimation.
The second type of extensions of the Dirichlet process are based on its stick-breaking representation
[
33
]
of the form
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{i}\delta_{Y_{i}}
, where
\pi_{i}=V_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}(1-V_{i})
with
(V_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}Beta(1,\alpha)
independent of an i.i.d. sequence
(Y_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. This construction principle is very popular due to its simplicity and flexibility and it has been generalized in many ways, see notably
[
24
,
30
,
20
]
, and applications are found in species sampling, clustering or hierarchical mixture models.

The third construction principle of the Dirichlet process is only valid in the univariate and real-valued case, in which the Dirichlet process can be viewed as a neutral-to-the-right (NTR) process
[
9
]
. An NTR process is a random probability measure constructed via a random survival function of the form
\exp\left(-\mu(-\infty,t])\right)
, where
\mu
is a CRM on
\mathbb{R}
. Extensions of this construction principle to the multivariate framework are less investigated than in the other two cases, since it is not obvious how a multivariate analog of
\exp\left(-\mu(-\infty,t])\right)
should be constructed to yield a valid multivariate survival function. Most works thus have focused on creating vectors of dependent univariate NTR processes, see e.g.
[
14
,
27
,
29
]
, with the exception of
[
22
]
who constructs a multivariate random probability measure with one specific margin being an NTR process.
The content of this paper can be viewed as the natural generalization of an NTR prior to the multivariate setting. Consider the random survival function of a real-valued random vector
{\bm{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
given by

\displaystyle S_{\mu}({\bm{x}})=P\left(X_{1}>x_{1},\ldots,X_{d}>x_{d}\mid\mu\right)=\exp\left(-\mu\left(({\bm{x}},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)\right),

(1)

where
\mu
denotes a random measure on
(-\infty,\infty]^{d}
which is finite on every set of the form
({\bm{x}},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}:=(-\infty,\infty]^{d}\setminus({\bm{x}},{\bm{\infty}}]
. The measure
\mu
is called exponent measure due to its appearance in the exponent of
S_{\mu}
and the corresponding random probability measure is denoted as
\minid(\mu)
. Since it is not trivial to see that
S_{\mu}
is a well-defined multivariate survival function for every
\mu
, we remark that survival functions of the form (
1
) naturally appear in the study of extremes of independent random vectors, where they are called minimum infinitely divisible (min-id) survival functions, explaining the notation
\minid(\mu)
and referring to
[
19
,
31
]
for more background information. It is easy to see that this construction principle is identical to the construction of an NTR prior when
\mu
is a univariate CRM. When
\mu
is a multivariate CRM each margin of
S_{\mu}
corresponds to an NTR process and it can be checked that
S_{\mu}({\bm{x}}_{1}),(S_{\mu}({\bm{x}}_{1})-S_{\mu}({\bm{x}}_{2}))/S_{\mu}({\bm{x}}_{1}),\ldots,(S_{\mu}({\bm{x}}_{K-1})-S_{\mu}({\bm{x}}_{K}))/S_{\mu}({\bm{x}}_{K-1})
is a collection of independent random variables for every
{\bm{x}}_{1}<\ldots<{\bm{x}}_{K}
, extending the classical neutrality concept for univariate distributions from
[
9
]
to the multivariate setting. Thus, random probability measures which are constructed by choosing a CRM
\mu
in (
1
) may be viewed as the natural multivariate generalizations of NTR processes.

Restricting
\mu
to the class of CRMs in (
1
) should however be seen as a limitation, since every realization of
\mu
is purely discrete and a corresponding random vector inherits this discrete structure. In this paper, we will go far beyond CRMs by allowing the random measure
\mu
to be infinitely divisible, i.e. for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exist i.i.d. random measures
\left(\mu^{(i,n)}\right)_{{1\leq i\leq n}}
such that
\mu\sim\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu^{(i,n)}
, see
[
23
, Chapter 3.3]
for an account on infinitely divisible random measures (IDRMs). The framework of IDRMs is much more flexible than that of CRMs, since IDRMs naturally include continuous as well as discrete random measures. Additionally, the class of IDRMs is closed under many natural operations on measures such as integration. For example, the univariate models of
[
13
,
28
]
for the cumulative hazard of a random variable are of the form
\int_{0}^{t}g(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)
for some diffuse CRM
\mu
and thus they are naturally included in the framework of IDRMs. Furthermore, many models for partially exchangeable (survival) data may also be embedded in this framework, such as
[
14
,
27
,
29
,
6
]
. In essence, this is always a consequence of these models being based on transformations of vectors of dependent CRMs, which often result in IDRMs.

At the heart of many multivariate Bayesian models is the construction of vectors of dependent CRMs, which are conditionally independent on a baseline CRM, see
[
7
]
for an overview of the topic. These vectors of dependent CRMs may or may not be smoothed at a higher level in the construction of the final model, but the discrete nature of the baseline CRM implies certain tie-like dependencies in the resulting vector of dependent random measures. Our framework allows to avoid these tie-like dependencies stemming from discrete baseline CRMs, as it allows for the constructions of vectors of dependent IDRMs, which are conditionally independent given a continuous baseline IDRM. This type of construction may be seen as a useful tool beyond the scope of this paper as it enables “smoother” dependence structures between dependent random measures.

Our specification of the prior can be summarized by the following hierarchical construction of the random probability distribution for a random vector
{\bm{X}}
:

\displaystyle{\bm{X}}\sim\minid(\mu),\ \mu\sim\IDRM.

(2)

The rest of this paper will be devoted to formalizing (
2
) in a sound mathematical framework for nonparametric Bayesian analysis. We already want to mention that the derivation of the posterior distribution will essentially be based on applications of de Finetti’s theorem, since we will show that the law of the exchangeable sequence
({\bm{X}}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\minid(\mu)
is again in the class of min-id distributions, which might be interpreted as a conjugacy property of our class of prior distributions. This conjugacy property will allow to represent our posterior without the need for the introduction of an auxiliary latent structure, since results about the conditional distributions of min-id sequences can be leveraged to identify the posterior of the random probability measure
\minid(\mu)
.

The main text is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces (exchangeable) sequences of min-id random vectors and IDRMs and derives a one-to-one correspondence of these two objects. Section
3
formally introduces our framework for prior distributions and discusses how to construct tractable models from simple building blocks. Further, several models from the literature are embedded into our framework. Section
4
derives the posterior distribution in the most general setting and Section
5
provides a framework to construct continuous priors with analytically tractable posterior distribution. Section
6
demonstrates the flexibility of our framework by introducing a tractable model for partially exchangeable data based on a continuous dependence structure and discusses and illustrates its simulation. Finally, Section
7
discusses potential implications and future research.

The paper is complemented by a supplementary material which contains a variety of addition results. First, Supplementary Material
8
provides an introduction to min-id distributions and their stochastic representations and conditional distributions. We strongly recommend all readers who are unfamiliar with the topic to first read this introductory material before proceeding with the main text of the paper. Section
9
provides a general framework to sample from the posterior predictive distribution of a min-id prior. Supplementary Material
10
exploits this general framework to derive a simulation algorithm for the posterior of the the model from Section
6
and also contains additional simulation results. Supplementary Material
11
contains additional properties of min-id priors. Supplementary Material
12
discusses the case of discrete priors and derives the posterior distribution of the multivariate version of the NTR prior. An additional example of a min-id prior is presented in Supplementary Material
13
. The proofs of the results from the supplementary material are collected in Appendix
14
whereas the proofs of the results from the main text are collected in Supplementary Material
15
. Technical details on random measure are deferred to Supplementary Material
16
. As the notation in the paper is rather heavy, an overview of the most important notation can be found in Supplementary Material
17
.

2
Exchangeable min-id sequences and infinitely divisible random measures

This section introduces exchangeable sequences of min-id random vectors, which naturally arise by imposing a prior on the exponent measure of a random vector according to (
2
). Further, we introduce infinitely divisible random measures on
(-\infty,\infty]^{d}
which will serve as the prior on the exponent measures in the construction of min-id random vectors. Finally, we prove that a sequence of min-id random vectors whose associated exponent measure is randomized by an infinitely divisible random measure stays in the class of exchangeable min-id sequences, which will be the essential technical ingredient to derive the posterior distribution.

In the following, will always assume that our random objects are defined on a common probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)
, if not explicitly mentioned otherwise. Moreover, to differentiate a sequence of random vectors, the first
m
random vectors in a sequence of random vectors and a random variable in the sequence we use the respective notation
{\underline{\bm{X}}}:=({\bm{X}}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}:=\left(\left(X_{i,j}\right)_{1\leq i\leq d}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
,
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{m}:=({\bm{X}}_{j})_{1\leq j\leq m}
and
X_{i,j}
. Similarly, a sequence of vectors, the first
m
vectors in a sequence of vectors and an entry in the sequence are denoted as
{\underline{\bm{x}}}:=({\bm{x}}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}:=\left(\left(x_{i,j}\right)_{{1\leq i\leq d}}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
,
{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m}:=({\bm{x}}_{j})_{1\leq j\leq m}
and
x_{i,j}
, respectively. Moreover, the operators on vectors/sequences
+,-,*,/,\min,\max,\sup,\inf
are always applied componentwise.

2.1
Min-id sequences

One of the central objects of this section are exchangeable sequences of random vectors. The following definition formalizes what we mean by exchangeable.

Definition 2.1
.

A sequence of random vectors
{\underline{\bm{X}}}:=\left({\bm{X}}_{1},{\bm{X}}_{2},\ldots\right)
is called exchangeable (exchangeable sequence) if for every deterministic permutation
\tau
of
\mathbb{N}
we have
{\underline{\bm{X}}}\sim\left({\bm{X}}_{\tau(1)},{\bm{X}}_{\tau(2)},\ldots\right)
.

This definition should not be confused with partial exchangeability, which often occurs in hierarchical models in Bayesian nonparametrics. Partial exchangeability allows different permutations in every margin of the sequence of random vectors and thus is a stronger condition.
We focus on sequences and random vectors on the space

E_{p}:=(-{\bm{\infty}},{\bm{\infty}}]=(-\infty,\infty]^{p},

where
p\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.
E_{p}
inherits the standard topology and corresponding Borel
\sigma
-algebra from
[-\infty,\infty]^{p}
. Frequently, the subspace
E^{\prime}_{p}:=E_{p}\setminus\{{\bm{\infty}}\}
, which inherits its topology and
\sigma
-algebra from
E_{p}
, will be important in theoretical considerations.

Min-id sequences are at the heart of this paper. Formally, they can be defined in various equivalent ways, which are presented jointly, because we will frequently use all of these definitions in our derivations. To ease the notation, denote the index set of a sequence of (random) vectors as
d\times\mathbb{N}:=\{(i,j)\mid 1\leq i\leq d,j\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Further, we will also use the notation
IJ:=\left((i_{k},j_{k})\right)_{1\leq k\leq p}
to refer to a
p
-dimensional margin of
d\times\mathbb{N}
and implicitly use the important convention
\min_{\emptyset}={\bm{\infty}}
. Finally, to emphasize that a random sequence (resp. vector)
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
(resp.
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
) takes values in a product space with index set
d\times\mathbb{N}
(resp.
IJ
), we denote
E_{IJ}=(-\infty,\infty]^{|IJ|}
for any
IJ\subset d\times\mathbb{N}
; and similarly for
E^{\prime}_{IJ}
.

Definition 2.2

(
[
35
,
31
]
)
.

A sequence of random vectors
{\underline{\bm{X}}}\in E_{d\times\mathbb{N}}
such that
P(X_{i,j}>x)>0
for all
(i,j)\in d\times\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
is called a min-id sequence if it satisfies one of the following equivalent conditions:

(i)

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exist i.i.d. sequences of random vectors
\left({\underline{\bm{X}}}^{(i,n)}\right)_{{1\leq i\leq n}}
such that
{\underline{\bm{X}}}\sim\min_{{1\leq i\leq n}}{\underline{\bm{X}}}^{(i,n)}
.

(ii)

There exists a Poisson random measure (PRM)
N=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{{\underline{\bm{x}}}^{(k)}}
on
E^{\prime}_{d\times\mathbb{N}}
with unique intensity measure
\lambda
, which satisfies
\lambda\left(\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}\in E^{\prime}_{d\times\mathbb{N}}\mid y_{i,j}\leq x\})\right)<\infty
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
{(i,j)\in d\times\mathbb{N}}
, such that

\displaystyle{\underline{\bm{X}}}\sim\min_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\underline{\bm{x}}}^{(k)}.

(3)

(iii)

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, all
p
-dimensional margins
IJ
of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
satisfy that
S_{IJ}\left({\bm{x}}_{IJ}\right)^{1/n}:=P\left({\bm{X}}_{IJ}>{\bm{x}}_{IJ}\right)^{1/n}
defines a proper survival function of an
E_{IJ}
-valued random vector.

The finite dimensional margins of a min-id sequence are called min-id (random) vectors. Further, a sequence
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
is called exchangeable min-id sequence if it is an exchangeable sequence which is also min-id.

Note that every measure
\lambda
on
E^{\prime}_{d\times\mathbb{N}}
which satisfies the conditions of Definition
2.2

(ii)
uniquely specifies the law of a min-id sequence. Since
\lambda
appears in the exponent of the survival function of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
, it is called the exponent measure of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
. Let us denote
\lambda^{(IJ)}
as the marginalization of
\lambda
to margin
IJ
, i.e. the intensity of a PRM on
E^{\prime}_{p}
such that the analog of (
3
) holds for
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
, which we also call exponent measure of
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
. One can easily check that when extending
\lambda
(
\lambda^{(IJ)}
) to
E_{d\times\mathbb{N}}
(
E_{IJ}
) the mass of
\lambda
(
\lambda^{(IJ)}
) on
{\bm{\infty}}
is irrelevant for the distribution of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
(
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
), which is why the point
{\bm{\infty}}
had to be disregarded to obtain uniqueness in Definition
2.2
(ii)
. Since it later will turn out to be convenient, we frequently view
\lambda^{(IJ)}
and
\lambda
as measures on
E_{IJ}
and
E_{d\times\mathbb{N}}
, where we employ the convention
\lambda^{(IJ)}(\{{\bm{\infty}}\})=\lambda(\{{\bm{\infty}}\})=\infty
. With this convention, the exponent measure
\lambda^{(IJ)}
of
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
can be viewed as the projection of the exponent measure
\lambda
of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
to the components
IJ
, i.e.
\lambda^{(IJ)}(A)=\lambda(\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}\mid{\bm{x}}_{IJ}\in A\})
, and for all
{\underline{\bm{x}}}\in[-\infty,\infty)^{d\times\mathbb{N}}
we can simply write

P({\underline{\bm{X}}}>{\underline{\bm{x}}})=\exp\Big(-\lambda\big(\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}\in E_{d\times\mathbb{N}}\mid y_{i,j}\leq x_{i,j}\text{ for some }{(i,j)\in d\times\mathbb{N}}\}\big)\Big),

while the analogous expression holds for
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
and
\lambda^{(IJ)}
. To simplify the notation we will denote
{\underline{\bm{X}}}\sim\minid(\lambda)
(
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}\sim\minid(\lambda^{(IJ)})
) to denote that
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
(
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
) is a min-id sequence (vector) with exponent measure
\lambda
(
\lambda^{(IJ)}
).

Example 1

(Min-id random vectors with independent components)
.

[
31
, 5.3.1. (i)]
shows that
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}\sim\minid(\lambda^{(IJ)})
has independent components if and only if
\lambda^{(IJ)}
concentrates on lines through
{\bm{\infty}}
, i.e.
\lambda^{(IJ)}
is supported on the set
\cup_{i=1}^{p}\mathcal{E}_{i,j}
, where

\mathcal{E}_{i,j}:=\big\{{\bm{x}}\in E_{p}^{\prime}\mid x_{i,j}<\infty,\ x_{k,l}=\infty\ \forall(k,l)\in IJ\text{ s.t. }(i,j)\not=(k,l)\big\}.

To illustrate this, note that when
\lambda^{(IJ)}
is concentrated on
\cup_{i=1}^{p}\mathcal{E}_{i,j}
we have that

P({\bm{X}}_{IJ}>{\bm{x}})=\exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda^{(IJ)}\big(\{y_{i,j}\in\mathcal{E}_{i,j}\mid y_{i,j}\leq x_{i,j}\}\big)\right).

Thus, the survival function of
{\bm{X}_{IJ}}
is the product of the terms
\exp\big(-\lambda^{(IJ)}\big(\{y_{i,j}\in\mathcal{E}_{i,j}\mid

y_{i,j}\leq x_{i,j}\}\big)\big)
, which are the marginal survival functions of the
X_{i,j}
.

The example emphasizes the importance of allowing for mass of
\lambda
(resp.
\lambda^{(IJ)}
) on
E_{d\times\mathbb{N}}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d\times\mathbb{N}}
(resp.
E_{IJ}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{|IJ|}
), since this is needed to be allow for independent components in min-sequences (resp. random vectors). Moreover, as we will see later in Examples
5
and
6
, many Bayesian models from the literature use priors which concentrate on min-id random vectors with (conditionally) independent components and thus such random vectors will naturally also play an important role in our framework.

Remark 2.3

(The role of
{\bm{\infty}}
)
.

As mentioned above, the mass of an exponent measure on
{\bm{\infty}}
is not relevant for the distribution of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}\sim\minid(\lambda)
. However, a careful treatment of the point
{\bm{\infty}}
is necessary when embedding
{\bm{X}_{IJ}}
in
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
. To illustrate this, assume that
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
has independent entries and recall that the exponent measure of
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
is given by
\lambda^{(IJ)}=\lambda\left(\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}\in E_{d\times\mathbb{N}}\mid{\bm{x}}_{IJ}\in\cdot\cap E_{p}^{\prime}\}\right)
, which is the projection of
\lambda
to the components
IJ
. Thus, the mass of
\lambda
on
\{{\bm{x}}_{IJ}=\infty\}
, which corresponds to the mass of the marginalized components
d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ
, is disregarded in
\lambda^{(IJ)}
as it is fully projected to
{\bm{\infty}}
when considering the exponent measure of
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
. However, when trying to reverse-engineer
\lambda
from
\lambda^{(IJ)}
(resp.
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
from
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
) it is now crucial to take into account the mass that was projected to
{\bm{\infty}}
. Thus, for reconstructing
\lambda
from
\lambda^{(IJ)}
, one should view
\lambda^{(IJ)}
as a measure on
E_{p}
, since there is potentially mass of
\lambda
on
\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}\in E_{d\times\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}\mid{\bm{x}}_{IJ}={\bm{\infty}}\}
, which is relevant to determine
\lambda
and the distribution of
{\bm{X}}_{(d\times\mathbb{N})\setminus IJ}
. This peculiarity will turn out to be important for many derivations and calculations in the paper, as we frequently have to embed
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
in a sequence, which is why this point is emphasized already at this stage.

Remark 2.4
.

We remark that
P(X_{i,j}>x)>0
for all
(i,j)\in d\times\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
should be viewed as a standardization of our framework, since, in general, min-id distributions can have an arbitrary upper endpoint of their support. It can be easily checked that
\left((f_{i,j}(X_{i,j})\right)_{(i,j)\in d\times\mathbb{N}}
remains a min id sequence in the sense of Definition
2.2
(i)
for every collection of non-decreasing transformations
(f_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in d\times\mathbb{N}}
from
[-\infty,\infty]\to[-\infty,\infty]
. Thus w.l.o.g. we can conduct our analysis for min-id sequences which satisfy
P(X_{i,j}>x)>0
for all
(i,j)\in d\times\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, whereas results about min-id sequences with finite upper endpoint of their support may be deduced from a suitable non-decreasing marginal transformation of a min-id sequence with
\infty
as upper endpoint of their support.

Definition
2.2
(i)
entails the name-giving stochastic representation of a min-id sequence, since it shows that every min-id sequence can be represented as the componentwise minimum of arbitrary many i.i.d. sequences. On the other hand, the stochastic representation (
3
) is the most useful representation for our purposes, since it nicely illustrates that every
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
can simply be constructed as the componentwise minimum of countably many sequences
{\underline{\bm{x}}}^{(k)}
, which are the atoms of a PRM. A natural question that arises from this representation is whether for every
(i,j)\in d\times\mathbb{N}
there is exactly one
k(i,j)
such that
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{i,j}=x^{(k(i,j))}_{i,j}
, i.e. whether the value
X_{i,j}
can be uniquely associated to a single atom
{\underline{\bm{x}}}^{(k)}
of the PRM. In general, the answer to this is negative, since it is rather easy to see that when the intensity
\lambda
has atoms there can be several
x^{(k)}_{i,j}
that take the same value. However, when the marginal distributions of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
are continuous, the answer is positive.

Lemma 2.5

(
[
12
, Proposition 2.5]
)
.

Let
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
denote a min-id sequence with stochastic representation (
3
) such that for every
(i,j)\in d\times\mathbb{N}
the random variable
X_{i,j}
has a continuous distribution function. Then, with probability
1
, there exists exactly one
k(i,j)
such that
X_{i,j}=x^{(k(i,j))}_{i,j}
for all
(i,j)\in d\times\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma
2.5
allows to define random equivalence classes for the index set
d\times\mathbb{N}
(
IJ
) of a min-id sequence
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
(
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
) with continuous margins by collecting all indices
(i,j)
which have the same minimizing sequence
{\underline{\bm{x}}}^{(k)}
, i.e. by

\displaystyle(i,j)\sim(\tilde{i},\tilde{j})\Leftrightarrow X_{i,j}=x^{(k)}_{i,j}\text{ and }X_{\tilde{i},\tilde{j}}=x^{(k)}_{\tilde{i},\tilde{j}}.

(4)

This equivalence relation is the basis of the so-called hitting scenario of a min-id sequence, describing the latent structure of the atoms of the PRM which are responsible for seeing the realization
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
(
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
). It was first introduced in
[
12
]
and will play an important role in the representation of the posterior in later sections.

Definition 2.6
.

Consider a margin
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
of a min-id sequence
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
. A hitting scenario
\Theta(IJ)=(\Theta_{1},\ldots,\Theta_{L})
is a random partition of
IJ
, where the partition is generated according to the equivalence relation (
4
) and
L:=L(\Theta(IJ))
denotes the length of the partition.

2.2
Infinitely divisible exponent measures

In this paper, we focus on random measures
\mu
on
E_{d}
that can be interpreted as random exponent measures.
To define random measures on
E_{d}
, we follow the technical elaborations in
[
23
, Section 1+2]
and consider only
d<\infty
, while more details concerning the well-definedness of the following definitions can be found in Supplementary Material
16
. We have to take extra care in the definition of
\sigma
-finite random measures on
E_{d}
since we later want to allow for measures that explode towards
{\bm{\infty}}
.
First, we fix a measurable partition
(U_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
E_{d}^{\prime}
given by

\displaystyle U_{i}:=\left(E_{d}\setminus\left((i,\infty]^{d}\right)\right)\setminus\cup_{j=1}^{i-1}U_{j}

(5)

and let

M_{d}:=\big\{\eta\mid\eta\text{ is a measure on }E_{d}\text{ such that }\eta(U_{i})<\infty\text{ and }\eta({\bm{\infty}})=\infty\big\}

denote the subset of
\sigma
-finite measures on
E_{d}
which are finite on
(U_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. Note that
\cup_{j=1}^{i-1}U_{j}=\left((i-1,\infty]^{d}\right)^{\complement}
. Therefore, every measure
\eta\in M_{d}
is an exponent measure of a min-id random vector on
E_{d}
as
\eta\left(({\bm{x}},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)<\infty
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and we can equivalently write
M_{d}=\{\eta\mid\eta\text{ is the exponent measure of a random vector on }E_{d}\}
.
Next, we equip
M_{d}
with the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{G}
that is generated by the evaluations of the measure on finite collections of measurable subsets of
E_{d}
, which allows us to define a random measure.

Definition 2.7
.

A random measure is a random element
\mu\in M_{d}
, i.e. a measurable map from
\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P\right)\to(M_{d},\mathcal{G})
.

Thus, a random measure
\mu
is the sum of countably many almost-surely finite random measures
(\mu(\cdot\cap U_{i}))_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and an infinite atom at
{\bm{\infty}}
.
The introduction of the “localizing sets”
(U_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is necessary, since the definition of a random measure on the space of
\sigma
-finite random measures on
E_{d}
additionally requires to determine where the measure is almost surely finite, see the technical elaborations in
[
23
, Section 1+2]
. In our case, the definition ensures that a random measure is a
\sigma
-finite exponent measure for the same exhausting sequence
(U_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, i.e. the sets on which a random measure may explode are non-random.

Next, we restrict ourselves to random measures that can be decomposed into sums of arbitrary many i.i.d. random measures, called infinitely divisible random measures. The formal definition is as follows.

Definition 2.8
.

A random measure
\mu
is called infinitely divisible random measure (IDRM) if for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists i.i.d. random measures
\mu^{(i,n)}
such that
\mu\sim\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu^{(i,n)}
.

Every IDRM can be characterized in terms of its Laplace transform, which follows a specific representation, called the Lévy-Khintchine representation. We recall this result here, since it will be important for the further developments in this paper.

Theorem 2.9

(
[
23
, Theorem 3.20]
)
.

Every IDRM
\mu
on
E_{d}
has the representation

\displaystyle\mu\sim\alpha+\int_{M^{0}_{d}}\eta N(\mathrm{d}\eta)=\alpha+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\eta_{j},

(6)

where
\alpha\in M_{d}
is a unique deterministic exponent measure and
N=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{\eta_{j}}
is a PRM on
M^{0}_{d}:=\{\eta\in M_{d}\mid\eta(E_{d}^{\prime})\not=0\}
with unique intensity measure
\nu
such that
\int_{M^{0}_{d}}\min\{\eta(U_{i});1\}

\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)<\infty
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
The Laplace transform of
\mu
has the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\int_{E_{d}}f({\bm{x}})\mu(d{\bm{x}})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(-\int_{E_{d}}f({\bm{x}})\alpha(d{\bm{x}})-\int_{M^{0}_{d}}1-\exp\left(-\int_{E_{d}}f({\bm{x}})\eta(d{\bm{x}})\right)\nu(d\eta)\bigg),

for every measurable function
f\geq 0
on
E_{d}
.
Conversely, every tuple
(\alpha,\nu)
satisfying the stated conditions specifies the law of an (unique in law) IDRM on
E_{d}
.

The measure
\alpha
will be called base measure, the measure
\nu
will be called Lévy measure and the tuple
(\alpha,\nu)
will be called Lévy-Khintchine characteristics of an IDRM
\mu
.
For our choice of localizing sets
(U_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
we have that every IDRM is actually an infinitely divisible random exponent measure (IDEM) on
E_{d}
with
\alpha({\bm{\infty}})=\infty
.

According to the definition of an IDEM
\mu
there exist i.i.d. random measures
\mu^{(i,n)}
such that
\mu\sim\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu^{(i,n)}
. An immediate consequence is that each
\mu^{(i,n)}
must also have localizing sets
(U_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. Thus, each
\mu^{(i,n)}
may be identified with a corresponding random exponent measure as well to obtain a decomposition of
\mu
into sums of i.i.d. random exponent measures. Further, one can show that each
\left(\mu^{(i,n)}\right)_{{1\leq i\leq n}}
is infinitely divisible. The results are summarized in the following corollary.

Corollary 2.10
.

For every IDEM
\mu
there exist i.i.d. IDEMs
\left(\mu^{(i,n)}\right)_{1\leq i\leq n}
such that
\mu\sim\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu^{(i,n)}
. Moreover,
\mu\sim\alpha+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\eta_{j}
, where
\alpha
is a deterministic exponent measure on
E_{d}
and
\left(\eta_{j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
are random exponent measures on
E_{d}
, which constitute the atoms of a PRM on
M_{d}^{0}
with unique intensity
\nu
.

Example 2

(Completely random measure)
.

Let us illustrate the simplest choice of IDEMs. Consider a completely random measure (CRM)
\mu
on
E^{\prime}_{d}
without fixed atoms, i.e. a random measure with the representation
\alpha+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}a_{k}\delta_{\bm{b}_{k}}
, where
\left((a_{k},\bm{b}_{k})\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are the atoms of a PRM on
(0,\infty)\times E_{d}^{\prime}
with intensity
\upsilon(\mathrm{d}(a,\bm{b}))
such that
\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{({\bm{x}},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}}\min\{1,a\}\upsilon(\mathrm{d}(a,\bm{b}))

<\infty
for every
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then
\mu(A_{1}),\ldots,\mu(A_{n})
are independent random variables for every collection of disjoint measurable sets
(A_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}\subset E_{d}
, which justifies the term “completely random”. It is easy to see that
\mu
is an IDEM with base measure
\alpha+\infty\delta_{\infty}
and Lévy measure
\nu(A)=\int_{(0,\infty)\times E_{d}^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{\{a\delta_{\bm{b}}+\infty\delta_{\bm{\infty}}\in A\}}\upsilon(\mathrm{d}(a,\bm{b}))
. Thus, the Lévy measure of a completely random measure concentrates on weighted Dirac measures.
The Laplace transform of a completely random measure
\mu
takes the well-known form

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\int_{E_{d}}f({\bm{x}})\mu(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\int_{E_{d}}f({\bm{x}})\alpha(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})-\int_{(0,\infty)\times E_{d}}1-\exp\left(-af(\bm{b})\right)\upsilon(\mathrm{d}(a,\bm{b}))\right).

2.3
One-to-one correspondence of exchangeable min-id sequences and infinitely divisible exponent measures

Let us end this section by providing the connection of IDEMs and exchangeable min-id sequences. First, we formulate a condition which ensures that
{\bm{X}}\sim\minid(\mu)
is real-valued.

Condition 1
.

The IDEM
\mu
on
E_{d}
satisfies
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu\left(\{{\bm{x}}\in E_{d}\mid x_{i}\leq n\}\right)=\infty
almost surely for all
1\leq i\leq d
.

The following theorem is one of the main technical ingredients for the derivation of the posterior distribution. It shows that randomizing the exponent measure of a min-id random vector by an IDEM yields an exchangeable min-id sequence, conditionally on
\mu
as well as unconditionally (i.e. integrating out the randomness of
\mu
).

Theorem 2.11
.

Let
\mu
denote an IDEM with Lévy-Khintchine characteristics
(\alpha,\nu)
that satisfies Condition
1
. Then, we can define a sequence of conditionally i.i.d. random vectors
{\underline{\bm{X}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times\mathbb{N}}
via

\displaystyle({\bm{X}}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\minid(\mu).

(7)

Further,
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
is an exchangeable min-id sequence, whose law is uniquely determined by
(\alpha,\nu)
.
The exponent measure
\lambda
of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
is determined by the Levy-Khintchine characteristics of
\mu
via

\displaystyle\lambda(A)=\lambda_{\alpha}(A)+\lambda_{\nu}(A),

(8)

where
\lambda_{\alpha}
is a measure concentrated on
\mathcal{W}:=\cup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}\in E_{d\times\mathbb{N}}\mid{\bm{x}}_{j}={\bm{\infty}}\text{ for all }j\not=i\in\mathbb{N}\}
given by

\lambda_{\alpha}(A):=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\alpha\left(\big\{{\bm{x}}\in E_{d}\mid\times_{k=1}^{j-1}\{{\bm{\infty}}\}\times\{{\bm{x}}\}\times_{k=j+1}^{\infty}\{{\bm{\infty}}\}\in A\big\}\right)

and

\lambda_{\nu}(A):=\int_{M^{0}_{d}}\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid(\eta)\left(\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}\in A\}\right)\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta),

where
\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid(\eta)
denotes the law of an i.i.d. sequence of min-id random vectors on
E_{d}
with exponent measure
\eta
.

Conversely, for every exchangeable min-id sequence whose exponent measure is of the form
\lambda_{\alpha}+\lambda_{\nu}
for some Lévy characteristics
(\alpha,\nu)
there exists a unique in law IDEM satisfying Condition
1
which generates the exchangeable min-id sequence according to (
7
).

The theorem could be called de Finetti’s theorem for exponent measures of exchangeable min-id sequences generated by IDEMs, since it shows that the exponent measure associated to these sequences is a (possibly infinite) mixture of the law of i.i.d. min-id sequences plus the exponent measure of a min-id sequence with i.i.d. components. Notice that our requirement of
\mu
taking values in
M_{d}
allows to define
{\bm{X}}_{j}
as a min-id random vector conditionally on every realization of
\mu
.
It should be emphasized that, even though
({\bm{X}}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is real-valued,
\eta
might be the exponent measure of a min-id random vector whose components can take the value
\infty
, i.e.
\minid(\eta)
is the distribution of a random vector on
E_{d}
and
\eta
might not satisfy Condition
1
.
An immediate consequence of Theorem
2.11
is the following stochastic decomposition of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
.

Corollary 2.12
.

An exchangeable min-id sequence
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
satisfying Condition
1
has the following stochastic decomposition:

{\underline{\bm{X}}}\sim\min\big\{{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{1},{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{2}\big\},

where
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{1}
is a sequence of i.i.d. min-id random vectors with exponent measure
\alpha
and
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{2}
is an exchangeable min-id sequence with exponent measure
\lambda_{\nu}
. Moreover, there is no i.i.d. sequence of random vectors
\underline{{\bm{Y}}}_{1}
independent of a min-id sequence
\underline{{\bm{Y}}}_{2}
such that
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{2}\sim\min\{\underline{{\bm{Y}}}_{1},\underline{{\bm{Y}}}_{2}\}
.

Thus, intuitively, an exchangeable min-id sequence can be divided into a trivial i.i.d. part
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{1}
and a non-trivial part
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{2}
, which is responsible for the dependence of the sequence. For our purposes of imposing a prior on
{\bm{X}}
this result says that
\alpha
drives an i.i.d. ”censoring-like” part of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
and
\nu
is the quantity that purely governs the dependence between the
{\bm{X}}_{j}
and allows to learn about the distribution of
{\bm{X}}
.

3
IDEM priors

Having collected all auxiliary results, we are ready to formally introduce our prior distribution on the space of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Definition 3.1

(IDEM prior)
.

Let
\mu
denote an IDEM on
E_{d}
satisfying Condition
1
. Then, the random probability measure
\minid(\mu)
is called IDEM prior.

In abuse of notation we will also call an IDEM
\mu
a prior in the following, which is justified by the fact that every IDEM prior has a unique associated IDEM due to Theorem
2.11
.

Example 3

(Partial exchangeability and independent components)
.

It is easy to see that
{\bm{X}}\sim\minid(\mu)
having independent components almost surely implies partial exchangeability of the associated exchangeable sequence
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
. Thus, we have that
\mu
is an IDEM for partially exchangeable data whenever
\mu
almost surely concentrates on
\cup_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{E}_{i}
, where

\mathcal{E}_{i}:=\{{\bm{x}}\in E_{d}\mid x_{i}<\infty,x_{j}={\bm{\infty}}\ \forall\ 1\leq j\not=i\leq d\}.

3.1
Building tractable IDEM priors from simple building blocks

Since IDRMs can be quite complicated objects, it is natural to ask whether one can construct such random measures based on simpler building blocks. Theoretically, the answer is simple. Due to Theorem
2.9
we simply need to look at PRMs on
M_{d}^{0}
to construct all IDRMs. However, in practice, PRMs on
M_{d}^{0}
are not very convenient to model and one might ask the question whether one can obtain IDRMs by simple transformations of tractable subclasses of IDRMs, e.g. such as CRMs.

It is not hard to see that the property of infinite divisibility is preserved under many natural operations on measures, such as integration. The difficult part is to retain tractable Lévy characteristics, which is especially important, since we will later see that tractable Lévy characteristics are the central ingredient for obtaining tractable formulas for the posterior. Here, we extend results on transformations of univariate infinitely divisible processes from
[
5
,
4
]
to multivariate IDRMs to retain infinite divisibility together with tractable Lévy characteristics. In practice one might concatenate several of these operations to obtain a suitable IDEM prior with tractable Lévy characteristics.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\mu
denote an IDEM on
E_{d}
with Lévy characteristics
(\alpha,\nu)
and assume that
\mu
satisfies Condition
1
.

(i)

Let
g:\ E_{d}\to E_{d}
be measurable and monotone increasing in each component with
g({\bm{\infty}})={\bm{\infty}}
. Define
\mu_{g}(A):=\int_{\{g({\bm{x}})\in A\}}\mathrm{d}\mu
. Then,
\mu_{g}
is an IDEM with Lévy characteristics
(\alpha_{g},\nu_{g})
, where
\alpha_{g}(A):=\int_{\{g({\bm{x}})\in A\}}\mathrm{d}\alpha
and

\nu_{g}(A):=\nu\big(\{\eta\in M_{d}^{0}\mid\eta_{g}\in A,\ \eta_{g}(E_{d}^{\prime})

\not=0\}\big)
.

(ii)

Let
g:E_{d}\to[0,\infty)
be measurable and bounded on
({\bm{y}},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}
for every
{\bm{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
\mu^{(g)}(A):=\int_{A}g({\bm{x}})\mu(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})
is an IDEM with Lévy characteristics
(\alpha^{(g)},\nu^{(g)})
, where
\nu^{(g)}(A)=\nu\left(\{\eta\in M_{d}^{0}\mid\eta^{(g)}\in A,\eta^{(g)}(E_{d}^{\prime})\not=0\}\right)

(iii)

Let
\beta
denote a measure on
E_{d}
such that
\beta\left(({\bm{y}},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)<\infty
for all
{\bm{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, the measure
\mu^{(\beta)}\left(A\right):=\int_{A}\mu\left((-{\bm{\infty}},{\bm{x}}]\right)\beta(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})
is an IDEM with Lévy characteristics
(\alpha^{(\beta)},\nu^{(\beta)})
, where
\nu^{(\beta)}(A)=\nu\left(\{\eta\in M_{d}^{0}\mid\eta^{(\beta)}\in A,\eta^{(\beta)}(E_{d}^{\prime})\not=0\}\right)
.

The transformation in Proposition
3.2
(i)
is useful to adjust the margins of a min-id prior to some predefined margins. For
g({\bm{x}})=(g_{1}(x_{1}),\ldots,g_{d}(x_{d}))
the distribution of the
i
-th margin of
\minid(\mu_{g})
only depends on
g_{i}
and thus it may be chosen such that
\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\mu_{g}\left(\{{\bm{x}}\in E^{\prime}_{d}\mid x_{i}\leq\cdot\}\right)\right)\right]
follows an arbitrary prespecified survival function.
The intuition behind the definition of
\mu^{(\beta)}
in Proposition
3.2
(iii)
is that it can be seen as a natural generalization of the popular smoothing of a univariate CRM given by
\int_{A}\mu\left((-\infty,s]\right)\mathrm{d}s
.

Additional properties of min-id priors such as prior moments, moments of mean functional and their induced dependence structure can be found in Supplementary Material
11
.

3.1.1
Subordination of independent CRMs by IDRM

A popular construction scheme for multivariate models in Bayesian nonparametrics is to build vectors of dependent random measures via a hierarchical construction, see e.g.
[
7
]
for an overview. Often, these constructions are based on simple transformations of (vectors of) CRMs. For example, in the context of survival analysis,
[
6
]
consider a diffuse homogeneous CRM
\mu_{0}
on
\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\min\{1,a\}\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)<\infty\text{ for every }t\in\mathbb{R}

(9)

and define
d
conditionally independent homogeneous completely random measures
\left(\mu_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq d}
with respective base measures
\alpha_{i}
and Lévy measures
\nu_{i}
given as the image measure of
(a,y)\mapsto a\delta_{y}
under
\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)
, i.e.
\nu_{i}(A)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\{a\delta_{y}\in A\}}\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)
.
The resulting
\left(\mu_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq d}
are dependent random measures, which are conditionally independent CRMs given
\mu_{0}
.
[
6
]
define a model for partially exchangeable data by setting the marginal random survival function of margin
i
to
\exp(-\mu_{i}^{(\kappa_{i})}\left((-\infty,t]\right))
, where for every measurable function
\kappa:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
and every measure
\eta
on
\mathbb{R}
we define

\eta^{(\kappa)}\left((-\infty,t]\right):=\int_{-\infty}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\kappa(s,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s\ \forall\ t\in\mathbb{R}

and
\eta^{(\kappa)}\left(\infty\right)=\infty
. Now, their model can be embedded into our framework by defining an exponent measure
\mu_{\mu_{1}^{(\kappa_{1})},\ldots,\mu_{d}^{(\kappa_{d})}}
on
E_{d}
via

\displaystyle\mu_{\mu_{1}^{(\kappa_{1})},\ldots,\mu_{d}^{(\kappa_{d})}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{i}(t)\right):=\mu_{i}^{(\kappa_{i})}\left((-\infty,t]\right)\ \forall\ t\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }1\leq i\leq d

(10)

where
\mathcal{E}_{i}(t):=\big\{{\bm{x}}\in E_{d}\mid x_{i}\leq t,x_{j}=\infty\ \forall\ 1\leq i\not=j\leq d\big\}
and
\mu_{\mu_{1}^{(\kappa_{1})},\ldots,\mu_{d}^{(\kappa_{d})}}({\bm{\infty}})=\infty
. It was already implicitly derived in
[
6
]
that
\mu_{\mu_{1}^{(\kappa_{1})},\ldots,\mu_{d}^{(\kappa_{d})}}
is infinitely divisible, however its Lévy characteristics were not tractable. Here, we show that the vector of random measures
\left(\mu_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq d}
is actually not only a vector of CRMs conditionally on
\mu_{0}
, but also unconditionally. Further, we extend the framework to the case where
\mu_{0}
can be an IDRM instead of a homogeneous CRM, which allows to replace the discrete structure of
\mu_{0}
with a continuous structure. To the best of the authors knowledge, such continuous structures at the root could not be considered to date since their Laplace transform has not been tractable. The following result provides the infinite divisibility and Lévy characteristics of such “subordinated” CRMs and is also of independent interest beyond the scope of this paper. To ease the notation in the following let
\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\crm_{\eta_{i}(\mathrm{d}(a,\bm{b}))}
denote the law of
d
independent CRMs with base measure
\infty\delta_{\bm{\infty}}
and Lévy measures given as the image measure of
(a,\bm{b})\mapsto a\delta_{\bm{b}}
under
\eta_{i}
, respectively, dropping
\otimes_{i=1}^{d}
when
d=1
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Assume that
\mu_{0}
is an IDRM on
\mathbb{R}
with Lévy characteristics
(\alpha_{0},\nu_{0})
such that
\mu_{0}\left((-\infty,t]\right)<\infty
almost surely for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, conditionally on
\mu_{0}
, let
\left(\mu_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq d}
denote
d
independent homogeneous CRMs on
\mathbb{R}
with base measures
\alpha_{i}
and Lévy measures
\nu_{i}
given as the image measures of
(a,b)\mapsto a\delta_{b}
under
\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}b)
, respectively. The following is true:

(i)

\mu_{i}((-\infty,t])<\infty
almost surely for all
{1\leq i\leq d}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,
\left(\mu_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq d}
is a vector of IDRMs which is jointly infinitely divisible, i.e. for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
one can find i.i.d. vectors of random measures
\left(\left(\mu_{i}^{(j,n)}\right)_{1\leq i\leq d}\right)_{1\leq j\leq n}
s.t.
\left(\mu_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq d}\sim\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{i}^{(j,n)}\right)_{1\leq i\leq d}
. The Lévy characteristics of
\mu_{i}
are given by
(\alpha_{i},\nu_{1,i}+\nu_{2,i})
where

\nu_{1,i}(A)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\{a\delta_{b}+\infty\delta_{\bm{\infty}}\in A\}}\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\alpha_{0}(\mathrm{d}b)

and

\nu_{2,i}(A)=\int_{M^{0}_{d}}\int_{M_{d}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\infty\delta_{\infty}\neq\eta_{i}\in A\}}CRM_{\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\eta(\mathrm{d}b)}(\eta_{i})\nu_{0}(\mathrm{d}\eta).

(ii)

When
\mu^{(\kappa_{i})}_{i}\left((-\infty,t]\right)<\infty
almost surely for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq i\leq d
then
\mu_{\mu_{1}^{(\kappa_{1})},\ldots,\mu_{d}^{(\kappa_{d})}}
as defined in (
10
) is an IDEM with base measure
\mu_{\alpha_{1}^{(\kappa_{1})},\ldots,\alpha_{d}^{(\kappa_{d})}}
and Lévy measure
\nu=\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}
, where

\nu_{1}(A)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\{\infty\delta_{\infty}\neq\otimes_{j=1}^{i-1}\delta_{\infty}\times(a\delta_{b})^{(\kappa_{i})}\otimes_{j=i+1}^{d}\delta_{\infty}\in A\}}\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\alpha_{0}(\mathrm{d}b)

and

\nu_{2}(A)=\int_{M^{0}_{d}}\int_{\times_{i=1}^{d}M_{d}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\infty\delta_{\infty}\neq\mu_{\eta^{(\kappa_{1})}_{1},\ldots,\eta^{(\kappa_{d})}_{d}}\in A\}}\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\crm_{\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\eta(\mathrm{d}b)}(\mathrm{d}\eta_{i})\nu_{0}(\mathrm{d}\eta).

Remark 3.4
.

The assumption that the
(\mu_{i})_{0\leq i\leq d}
in Proposition
3.3
are random measures on
\mathbb{R}
may be relaxed to allow the
\mu_{i}
to be random measures on an arbitrary Polish space
\mathbb{Y}
. The results of Proposition
3.3
remain valid, where
\kappa_{i}(s,y)
needs to be defined on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Y}
, implicitly assuming that the
(\mu_{i})_{0\leq i\leq d}
are well-defined. Similarly, Proposition
3.2
(
i
)-(
ii
) may be straightforwardly extended to IDRMs on
\mathbb{Y}
. To avoid excessive notation and technical regularity conditions we have focused on the most important case
\mathbb{Y}=\mathbb{R}
and leave spelling out the details to the reader.

3.2
Examples of IDEM priors from the literature

Before proceeding with the posterior distribution of an IDEM prior, we embed several well-known models into our framework to illustrate how IDEM priors are constructed in the literature.

Example 4

(Neutral-to-the-right-priors
[
16
,
9
,
18
]
)
.

Continuing Example
2
in the case
d=1
, assume we are given a diffuse CRM
\mu
on
(0,\infty)
. In the spirit of Theorem
2.11
we can then define a prior in terms of
X\sim\minid(\mu)
. The corresponding prior is well known in Bayesian nonparametrics as an NTR prior
[
16
,
9
,
18
]
.
We obtain that
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
is a min-id sequence with exponent measure
\lambda(A)=\lambda_{\alpha}(A)+\lambda_{\nu}(A)
, where
\lambda_{\nu}(A)=\int_{(0,\infty)^{2}}\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid(a\delta_{b})\upsilon(\mathrm{d}(a,b)).

Thus,
\lambda_{\nu}
is the mixture of the law of i.i.d. sequences, each of them being concentrated on the two points
\{b,\infty\}
. Further, by Corollary
2.12
it is easy to see that
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
has the stochastic representation

\displaystyle{\underline{\bm{X}}}\sim\min\Big\{\underline{{\bm{X}}}_{1};{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{2}\Big\},

(11)

where
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{2}:=\min_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\underline{\bm{x}}}^{(k)}
is an exchangeable mid-id sequence such that
{\underline{\bm{x}}}^{(k)}\in\{b_{k},\infty\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is an exchangeable sequence with marginal distribution
(1-\exp(-a_{k}))\delta_{b_{k}}+\exp(-a_{k})\delta_{\infty}
and
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{1}=(y_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is an i.i.d. sequence with
y_{k}\sim\minid(\alpha)
. Analogous statements hold when
\mu
is a multivariate CRM, generalizing NTR priors to the multivariate setting. A detailed treatment of multivariate NTR priors can be found in Supplementary Material
12
.

Example 5

(Hierarchically dependent mixture hazards
[
6
]
)
.

As already explained at the beginning of Section
3.1.1

[
6
]
construct vectors of dependent CRMs
(\mu_{i})_{1\leq i\leq d}
on a Polish space
\mathbb{Y}
, which are conditionally i.i.d. given a CRM
\mu_{0}
. Then, they define the respective survival functions of
d
groups of observation by
S_{i}(t):=\exp\left(-\mu_{i}^{(\kappa_{i})}\left((-\infty,t]\right)\right)
, which is equivalent to assuming
{\bm{X}}\sim\minid\left(\mu_{\mu_{1}^{(\kappa_{1})},\ldots,\mu_{d}^{(\kappa_{d})}}\right)
. Thus, the model concentrates on exponent measures of random vectors with independent components, implying partial exchangeability of the resulting sequence
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
. By Proposition
3.3
,
\mu_{\mu_{1}^{(\kappa)},\ldots,\mu_{d}^{(\kappa)}}
is an IDEM prior with tractable Lévy characteristics.

In the case
d=1
, which also includes the models of
[
13
,
28
]
as special cases, we argue that there is actually one canonical choice of
\mathbb{Y}
which includes all construction possibilities of
\mu_{1}
. Recall that every univariate measure can be uniquely associated to a non-negative and non-decreasing cumulative distribution function. Thus, every univariate IDRM can be uniquely associated to a non-negative and non-decreasing infinitely divisible càdlàg process
\left(H(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}:=\left(\mu_{1}^{(\kappa_{1})}([0,t])\right)_{t\geq 0}
. In
[
4
, Proposition 2.16]
it is shown that every non-negative and non-decreasing infinitely divisible càdlàg process can be represented as
\left(\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
, where
\left(f_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are the atoms of a PRM on the (Polish) space
D^{0}_{\geq 0,\nearrow}([0,\infty))
of non-negative and non-decreasing càdlàg functions indexed by
[0,\infty)
that satisfy
f(0)=0
, which is equipped with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra generated by the Skorohod topology. Thus,
H(t)\sim\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}(t)
, where
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are the atoms of a PRM N on
\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}:=D^{0}_{\geq 0,\nearrow}([0,\infty))\cap\{f\text{ absolutely continuous}\}
and choosing
\kappa(s,f)=f^{\prime}(s)
yields
\mu_{1}^{(\kappa_{1})}([0,t])\sim H(t)\sim\int_{\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}\kappa(s,f)\mathrm{d}sN(\mathrm{d}f)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}(t)
. Thus, it suffices to look at PRMs on
\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}
to represent all univariate models of
[
6
]
.

Example 6

(Hazard rate mixtures
[
27
]
)
.

Two completely random measures
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
on a Polish space
\mathbb{Y}
are coupled by introducing a latent variable
Z\in[0,1]
such that, conditionally on
Z
, the CRMs can be represented as
\mu_{1}(A)=\tilde{\mu}_{1}(A)+\tilde{\mu}_{0}(A)
and
\mu_{2}(A)=\tilde{\mu}_{2}(A)+\tilde{\mu}_{0}(A)
, where
\left(\tilde{\mu}_{i}\right)_{0\leq i\leq 2}
are independent CRMs on
\mathbb{Y}
with base measure
\infty\delta_{{\bm{\infty}}}
and intensities
Z\tilde{\nu}
,
(1-Z)\tilde{\nu}
and
(1-Z)\tilde{\nu}
for some Lévy measure
\tilde{\nu}
. Thus,
Z
steers the dependence of
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
by governing the magnitude of
\tilde{\mu}_{0}
relative to
\tilde{\mu}_{1}
and
\tilde{\mu}_{2}
. The authors define a corresponding random vector
{\bm{X}}\in(0,\infty)^{2}
via the survival function
S(t_{1},t_{2})=\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\kappa(s,y)\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\kappa(s,y)\mu_{2}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s\right)
, where
\kappa:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Y}\to[0,\infty)
. Conditionally on
Z
and
(\mu_{i})_{i=1,2}
,
{\bm{X}}
is min-id with independent components and the associated exponent measure is given by
\mu_{\mu_{1}^{(\kappa)},\mu_{2}^{(\kappa)}}
, which is an IDEM conditionally on
Z
. We obtain that the Lévy measure of
\mu
is given by
\nu(A)=\nu_{0}(A)+\nu_{1}(A)+\nu_{2}(A)
, where
\nu_{0}(A)=Z\tilde{\nu}\left(\{(a,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{Y}\mid\mu_{(a\delta_{y})^{(\kappa)},(a\delta_{y})^{(\kappa)}}\in A\}\right)
,

\nu_{1}(A)=(1-Z)\tilde{\nu}\left(\{(a,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{Y}\mid\mu_{(a\delta_{y})^{(\kappa)},\delta_{\infty}}\in A\}\right)
and similarly for
\nu_{2}
.
Again, for
d=1
, one can choose
\mathbb{Y}=\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}
with
k(s,f)=f^{\prime}(s)
to basically obtain the same priors as in
[
6
]
.

An additional example embedding the frameworks of
[
14
,
29
]
into the framework of IDEM priors can be found in Supplementary Material
13
.

4
Posterior distribution of IDEM priors

In this section, we derive the posterior distribution of an IDEM prior by deriving the conditional distribution of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
given
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
and applying de Finetti’s theorem to
({\bm{X}}_{n+1},{\bm{X}}_{n+2},\ldots)
to identify the posterior of an IDEM
\mu
.

4.1
Conditional distribution of min-id sequences

A central ingredient to derive the posterior distribution of an IDEM
\mu
is the conditional distribution of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
given
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
. In this subsection, we exploit the results of
[
12
]
to describe these conditional distributions.
Before formally introducing the conditional distribution of an exchangeable min-id sequence, we must introduce some further terminology. Recall the hitting scenario from Definition
2.6
, which determines how the minimum over all atoms of the PRM in (
3
) is attained. We will see that the conditional hitting scenario, i.e. the conditional distribution of a hitting scenario given
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
, will play a major role in the representation of the posterior. Formally, the definition of a conditional hitting scenario is as follows.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
be an exchangeable min-id sequence. The conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}=\left(\tilde{\Theta}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\Theta}_{L}\right)
of the
IJ
-margin
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
is the random partition of
IJ
which follows the distribution of the hitting scenario
\Theta(IJ)
conditionally on
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
, where
L=L(\tilde{\Theta})
denotes the length of the partition. Its probability distribution is denoted as
\tau({\bm{X}}_{IJ},\theta):=P(\tilde{\Theta}=\theta\mid{\bm{X}}_{IJ})
.

Example 7

(Conditional hitting scenarios of NTR process)
.

Surprisingly, the conditional hitting scenarios of NTR processes without fixed jumps are very simple. There is only one conditional hitting scenario with positive probability, which can be directly read off from the sample
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
. Simply create a partition of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
by assigning
i
and
j
to the same subset of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
if
X_{i}=X_{j}
, i.e. if random variable
i
and
j
form a tie in the data.
This can be verified using the stochastic representation of a min-id sequence associated to an NTR process given by (
11
). First, recall that the associated non-negative and non-decreasing additive process
H(t)=\alpha((-\infty,t])+\sum_{b_{k}\leq t}a_{k}\delta_{b_{k}}
cannot have any fixed points of discontinuity, which implies that the
(b_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(y_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are almost surely distinct. Due to (
11
), we have
{\underline{\bm{X}}}\sim\min\{\min_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\underline{\bm{x}}}^{(k)},(y_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\}
, where
{\underline{\bm{x}}}^{(k)}\in\{b_{k},\infty\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Thus we can only have that
X_{i}=X_{j}
if and only if there exists a
k
such that
x^{(k)}_{i}=X_{i}=b_{k}=X_{j}=x^{(k)}_{j}
, which proves the claim.

The second ingredient that will be needed to formulate the posterior distribution of an IDEM prior is the conditional probability distribution of the exponent measure
\lambda
.
Recall that
\lambda
is generally an infinite measure, which is why the definition of a conditional probability distribution of
\lambda
requires some care. In analogy to the definition of a regular conditional probability we define the conditional probability distribution of the measure
\lambda
given
{\bm{x}}_{IJ}:=(x_{i_{k},j_{k}})_{1\leq k\leq p}
via the integral equation

\displaystyle\int_{E_{d\times\mathbb{N}}}\mathsf{G}\big({\bm{x}}_{IJ},{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{\setminus IJ}\big)\lambda(\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{x}}})

\displaystyle=\int_{E^{\prime}_{p}}\int_{(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}}G({\bm{x}}_{IJ},{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{\setminus IJ}\big)K_{IJ}\big({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{\setminus IJ}\big)\lambda^{(IJ)}\big(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}_{IJ}\big),

(12)

where
{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{\setminus IJ}:=(x_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}
,
\mathsf{G}(\cdot,\cdot)
is a function that vanishes on
\{{\bm{\infty}}\}\times(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}
and
K_{IJ}({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\cdot)
denotes a probability kernel on
(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}
for every
{\bm{x}}_{IJ}\in E^{\prime}_{p}
. Formally, the existence of such probability kernels
K_{IJ}
has been verified for min-id sequences
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
with continuous marginal distribution and compact index set in
[
12
, Appendix A.2]
, but it can be checked that their definition only relies on the assumption of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
being an element of a Polish space, which is trivially satisfied in our setting. Further,
[
12
]
provide formulas for the conditional distribution of continuous min-stable processes with continuous marginal distributions, which allows to derive the distribution of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{m}
given
{\bm{X}_{IJ}}
under the following strengthening of Condition
1
which implies continuous marginal distributions of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
and well-definedness of the conditional hitting scenario.

Condition 2
.

For all
1\leq i\leq d
the IDEM
\mu
is such that the random functions
h_{i}(t):=\mu\left(\{{\bm{x}}\in E_{d}^{\prime}\mid x_{i}\leq t\}\right)
are stochastically continuous with
\lim_{t\to\infty}h_{i}(t)=\infty
almost surely.

Note that the condition does not exclude that a realization of
\mu
can be a discrete measure, it just excludes that a margin can have a fixed point of discontinuity with positive probability. A proof of the fact that Condition
2
is equivalent to continuous margins of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
is given in
[
4
, Corollary 2.3]
. Under Condition
2
, an application of
[
12
, Theorem 3.3]
yields the posterior predictive distributions, i.e. the conditional distribution of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
given
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
denote an exchangeable min-id sequence. Under Condition
2
, the regular conditional distribution of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
given
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
is given by

\displaystyle P\left({\bm{X}}_{n+1}>{\bm{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{X}}_{n+k}>{\bm{x}}_{k}\ \big|\ {\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-\lambda\left(\Big\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}\ \big|\ \left({\bm{y}}_{n+j}\right)_{1\leq j\leq k}\in\big({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k},{\bm{\infty}}\big]^{\complement}\text{ , }{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n}>{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}\Big\}\Big)\right)

(13)

\displaystyle\sum_{\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\tau({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\theta})\prod_{l=1}^{L(\tilde{\theta})}\frac{K_{\tilde{\theta}_{l}}\left({\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\theta}_{l}},\big\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{\setminus\tilde{\theta}_{l}}\mid{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\theta}_{l}}>{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\theta}_{l}},\left({\bm{y}}_{n+j}\right)_{1\leq j\leq k}>{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k}\big\}\right)}{K_{\tilde{\theta}_{l}}\left({\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\theta}_{l}},\big\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}\mid{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\theta}_{l}}>{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\theta}_{l}}\big\}\right)},

(14)

where
\mathcal{P}_{n}
denotes the set of all possible partitions of
d\times n
.

4.2
A general representation of the posterior

The formula from Theorem
4.2
splits into the product of two terms: the term (
13
) and the remaining term (
14
) that involves a sum over all partitions of
d\times n
. This implies that, conditionally on
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\left({\bm{X}}_{n+j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\sim\min\big\{\underline{\bm{Y}}^{(n)},\underline{\bm{Z}}^{(n)}\big\},

(15)

where
\underline{\bm{Y}}^{(n)}
is distributed according (
13
) and independent of
\underline{\bm{Z}}^{(n)}
, which is distributed according to (
14
).
The term (
13
) is easily identified as the restriction of the exponent measure
\lambda
to the set
A({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}):=\big\{\underline{{\bm{y}}}\in E_{d\times\mathbb{N}}\mid{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n}>{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}\big\}
,
which due to Theorem
2.11
can be represented as

\displaystyle\lambda(\cdot\cap A({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}))

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\alpha}(\cdot\cap A({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}))+\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid(\eta)\left(\cdot\cap A({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n})\right)\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta).

Therefore, for every measurable set of
B\subset E^{\prime}_{d\times\mathbb{N}}
we have

\displaystyle\lambda(\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}\in E_{d\times\mathbb{N}}\mid{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{\setminus d\times n}\in B,{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n}\in A({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n})\})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\alpha}(B)+\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid(\eta)\left(\{\left({\bm{y}}_{n+j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in B\}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\exp\left(-\eta\left(({\bm{X}}_{j},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)\right)\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)

Thus, conditionally on
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
,
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}
is distributed as an exchangeable min-id sequence with exponent measure given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha}(\cdot)+\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid(\eta)\left(\{\left({\bm{y}}_{n+j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in\cdot\}\right)\overline{\nu}_{n}(\mathrm{d}\eta),

(16)

where

\displaystyle\overline{\nu}_{n}(\mathrm{d}\eta):=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\exp\left(-\eta\left(({\bm{X}}_{j},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)\right)\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)

(17)

defines a valid Lévy measure of an IDEM, since it satisfies the conditions from Theorem
2.9
. Thus,
\underline{\bm{Y}}^{(n)}
is
an exchangeable min-id sequence associated to the IDEM
\overline{\mu}_{n}
with base measure
\alpha
and Lévy measure
\overline{\nu}_{n}
.

The remaining term (
14
) is more complicated to identify, but it is easily seen that
\underline{\bm{Z}}^{(n)}
is generated by an hierarchical structure:

1.

Draw a conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
according to
\tau({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\cdot)
.

2.

Conditionally on
\tilde{\Theta}=(\tilde{\Theta}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\Theta}_{L})
, draw
L
independent sequences
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(\tilde{\Theta}_{l})}
with distribution
K_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}({\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}},\cdot)
conditionally on
\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}>{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}\}
and set

\displaystyle\underline{\bm{Z}}^{(n,l)}:=\left({\bm{Z}}_{n+j}^{(\tilde{\Theta}_{l})}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

(18)

.

3.

Define
\underline{\bm{Z}}^{(n)}=\min_{1\leq l\leq L}\underline{\bm{Z}}^{(n,l)}
.

It should be mentioned that, even though
\left({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n+j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is real-valued,
\underline{{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}
may also assume the value
\infty
in its entries as its distribution is determined by
K_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}({\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}},\cdot)
, which are probability distributions on
E_{\infty}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Under Condition
2
and conditionally on the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}=(\tilde{\Theta}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\Theta}_{L})
, the sequences
\left(\left({\bm{Z}}^{(n,l)}_{j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\right)_{1\leq l\leq L}
from (
18
) are exchangeable.

A consequence of Lemma
4.3
is that, by de Finetti’s theorem, each sequence of random vectors
\underline{{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}
can be associated to a random multivariate survival function
S^{(n,l)}
such that
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}
is conditionally i.i.d. given
S^{(n,l)}
. Thus, we can define the random survival function associated to
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n)}
as

S_{n}({\bm{x}}):=\prod_{l=1}^{L}S^{(n,l)}({\bm{x}}),

which immediately implies that
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n)}
is exchangeable as well.

Theorem 4.4

(Posterior of IDEM prior)
.

Let
\mu
be an IDEM and let
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
denote the corresponding exchangeable min-id sequence. Under Condition
2
, the posterior predictive distribution of an IDEM prior can be characterized in terms of de Finetti’s theorem as follows. Conditionally on
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
, the random survival function associated to the exchangeable sequence
({\bm{X}}_{n+j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is given by

\exp\left(-\overline{\mu}_{n}\left(({\bm{x}},\infty]^{\complement}\right)\right)S_{n}({\bm{x}}),

where
\overline{\mu}_{n}
denotes an IDEM with Lévy-Khintchine characteristics
(\alpha,\overline{\nu}_{n})
independent of
S_{n}=\prod_{1\leq l\leq L}S^{(n,l)}
, where the
S^{(n,l)}
are the random survival functions associated to
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}
.

It should be mentioned that we had to formulate Theorem
4.4
in terms of de Finetti’s theorem, since the author does not know whether or not
S^{(n,l)}
is always the survival function of a min-id distribution. When imposing an additional regularity condition, one can show that each
S^{(n,l)}
may be identified with a random exponent measure
\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}
and one can phrase Theorem
4.4
directly in terms of the posterior of
\mu
, see Theorem
5.5
below.
In the univariate case
\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}\left((x,\infty]^{\complement}\right):=-\log(S^{(n,l)}(x))
always defines a measure and one can always express the posterior of
\left({\bm{X}}_{n+j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
directly in terms of the posterior of
\mu
.

Corollary 4.5

(Univariate case)
.

In the setting of Theorem
4.4
with
d=1
the posterior distribution of an IDEM prior
\mu
is given by

\mu\sim\overline{\mu}_{n}+\sum_{1\leq l\leq L}\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)},

where
\overline{\mu}_{n}
denotes an IDEM with Lévy-Khintchine characteristics
(\alpha,\overline{\nu}_{n})
independent of the random exponent measures
\left(\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}\right)_{1\leq l\leq L}
defined via
\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}((-\infty,x]):=-\log(S^{(n,l)}(x))
.

Remark 4.6

(Partial exchangeability and partial observations)
.

In the case of partial exchangeability, i.e.
\mu
concentrating on
\cup_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{E}_{i}
, it is often the case that one observes different numbers of observations per component of
{\bm{X}}
, i.e. one observes
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
instead of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
. Theorem
4.4
can easily be adapted to partial observations of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
by considering the conditional hitting scenario of
IJ
and the respective restriction of
\lambda
. A thorough formulation of Theorem
4.4
under this more general framework would require additional notation and interpretation, which is why we have refrained from spelling out the details and leave this exercise to the reader. A concrete example where we have derived the posterior for partial observations is the case of the multivariate NTR prior, see Supplementary Material
12
.

Clearly, the most complex quantities appearing in the posterior are the conditional distributions
K_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}
and the distribution of the conditional hitting scenarios
\tau({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\cdot)
. Providing universal tractable formulas for these quantities seems rather difficult as they are dependent on the specific choice of
\mu
. Section
5
below shows that, under some simple regularity condition, one can obtain tractable formulas for a large class of IDEM priors which concentrate on continuous distributions, which also allow to represent
S^{(n,l)}
via a min-id distribution. On the other hand, IDEM priors which concentrate on discrete distributions usually cannot satisfy the regularity conditions of Section
5
. Nevertheless, we have derived a closed-form analytical representation of the posterior of a multivariate NTR prior in Supplementary Material
12
, which can serve as a template for deriving the posterior of other discrete IDEM priors.

5
A large class of tractable models for continuous distributions

Since the results of Section
4
are rather theoretical and difficult to grasp, we here provide closed form representations of the posterior distribution of continuous IDEMs which follow some regularity condition that is often met in practice. The strategy is to impose a regularity condition on
\mu
via
(\alpha,\nu)
such that the resulting exponent measure
\lambda
retains this regularity condition. This is also convenient in practice since an IDEM prior will usually be specified in terms of its corresponding Lévy characteristics
(\alpha,\nu)
.

Our idea can be viewed as a generalization of the ideas of
[
12
, Section 4.3]
to the Bayesian framework, as they provide formulas for
K_{IJ}({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\cdot)
and
\tau({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\cdot)
when
\lambda^{(IJ)}
has a density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure. However, conditions on the level of
\lambda^{(IJ)}
are not suitable in our framework, since the existence of a density of
\lambda^{(IJ)}
w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure is usually not realistic and difficult to check when imposing a prior on
\mu
. For example, when
\mu
concentrates on
\cup_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
\lambda^{(IJ)}
might or might not have a Lebesgue density, depending on the specific choice of
\mu
. Therefore, a condition on the level of
\lambda^{(IJ)}
does not seem natural in a Bayesian setting and we take a different approach by imposing a regularity condition on the level of
(\alpha,\nu)
. To be specific, we assume that for
\nu
-almost every
\eta

\minid(\eta)
has a density which can be represented as a sum of lower-dimensional Lebesgue densities w.r.t. the disjoint sets

\mathcal{E}_{D}=\{{\bm{x}}\in E_{d}\mid x_{i}<\infty\text{ for all }i\in D\text{ and }x_{j}=\infty\text{ for all }1\leq j\not\in D\leq d\},

where
D\subseteq\{1,\ldots,d\}
.
Denoting
\Lambda
as the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
we define a measure on
(-\infty,\infty]
by
\Lambda^{(\infty)}:=\delta_{\infty}+\Lambda
with the corresponding measure on
E_{p}
defined as

\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{p}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}):=\otimes_{i=1}^{p}\Lambda^{(\infty)}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}),

which will act as a dominating measure for
\minid(\eta)
.
The definition implies that
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{d}(\cdot\cap\mathcal{E}_{D})
corresponds to the
D
-dimensional Lebesgue measure on
\mathcal{E}_{D}
, i.e.
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{d}
is the sum of Lebesgue measures on the sets
\mathcal{E}_{D}
and a point mass on
{\bm{\infty}}
. Now, the idea is to deduce that when
\nu
-almost every
\minid(\eta)
is absolutely continuous w.r.t.
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{d}
this implies that
\lambda^{(IJ)}
is absolutely continuous w.r.t.
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{|IJ|}
as well. Formally, this translates to the following simplifying assumption, which is a strengthened version of Condition
2
.

Condition 3
.

The Lévy measure
\nu
of the IDEM
\mu
satisfies
\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\minid(\eta)\big(\{{\bm{x}}\in E_{d}\mid

x_{i}\leq t\}\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)=\infty
for all
1\leq i\leq d
. Moreover, for
\nu
-almost every
\eta
, the probability measure
\minid(\eta)
and the measure
\alpha
admit densities
f_{\eta}(\cdot)
and
g_{\alpha}(\cdot)
w.r.t.
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{d}
.

It is worth mentioning that
f_{\eta}
is a probability density whereas
g_{\alpha}
is generally not, and
g_{\alpha}({\bm{\infty}})=\infty
. Further,
f_{\eta}({\bm{\infty}})>0
is possible, since
\minid(\eta)
is a probability distribution on
E_{d}
even though
\minid(\mu)
concentrates on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The following example illustrates Condition
3
.

Example 8
.

Let us illustrate the bivariate case by a continuation of Example
6
, assuming
\lim_{s\to\infty}\kappa(s,y)>0
for all
y\in\mathbb{Y}
. We know that
g_{\alpha}=\infty\mathbf{1}_{\{{\bm{x}}={\bm{\infty}}\}}
and that
\nu
concentrates on measures of the form
\mu_{(a\delta_{y})^{(\kappa)},\delta_{\infty}}
,
\mu_{\delta_{\infty},(a\delta_{y})^{(\kappa)}}
,
\mu_{(a\delta_{y})^{(\kappa)},(a\delta_{y})^{(\kappa)}}
for some
a\in(0,\infty)
and
y\in\mathbb{Y}
. Thus,
\minid(\eta)
has corresponding densities w.r.t.
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{2}
of the form
f_{\mu_{(a\delta_{y})^{(\kappa)},(a\delta_{y})^{(\kappa)}}}({\bm{x}})=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2}a\kappa(x_{i},y)\right)\exp\left(-a\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{x_{i}}\kappa(s,y)\mathrm{d}s\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\}}
,
f_{\mu_{(a\delta_{y})^{(\kappa)},\delta_{\infty}}}({\bm{x}})=a\kappa(x_{1},y)\exp\left(-a\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\kappa(s,y)\mathrm{d}s\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\}}
and similarly for
f_{\mu_{\delta_{\infty},(a\delta_{y})^{(\kappa)}}}({\bm{x}})
.

Remark 5.1
.

In general, it is hard to check that a given multivariate probability density is the density of a min-id distribution, see
[
31
, Exercise 5.1.4]
. Therefore it is difficult to directly specify a multivariate model in terms
f_{\eta}
and it is advisable to first specify
\eta
and then to derive the corresponding density of
\minid(\eta)
from its survival function. For example, one may specify
\eta=\sum_{D\subseteq\{1,\ldots,d\}}\eta_{D}
, where
\eta_{D}
has a Lebesgue density on
\mathcal{E}_{D}
. Then, the corresponding density of
\minid(\eta_{D})
might be derived by applying standard density formulas, accounting for the potential mass on
\infty
of other coordinates. Note that non-zero
\eta_{D}
implies that
(X_{i})_{i\in D}
are dependent for
{\bm{X}}\sim\minid(\eta)
. When
\eta=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\eta_{i}
for
\nu
-almost every
\eta
and
\alpha
also concentrates on
\cup_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{E}_{i}
then
\minid(\mu)
is a model for partially exchangeable data.

According to Condition
3
, for every set
A\in\mathcal{B}\left(E_{d\times m}\right)
we get

\displaystyle\lambda_{\nu}^{(d\times m)}(A)

\displaystyle=\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\otimes_{i=1}^{m}\minid(\eta)(A)\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)=\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\int_{A}\prod_{i=1}^{m}f_{\eta}({\bm{x}}_{i})\Lambda_{dm}^{(\infty)}(\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m})\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)

\displaystyle=\int_{A_{i}}\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}f_{\eta}({\bm{x}}_{i})\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)\Lambda_{md}^{(\infty)}(\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m}),

where we can apply Fubini because
\nu
and
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{dm}
are both
\sigma
-finite measures, due to Lemma
16.4
in Supplementary Material
16
. This implies that the
md
-dimensional margin of
\lambda_{\nu}
corresponding to
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{m}
has the density

\displaystyle f^{(d\times m)}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m}):=\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}f_{\eta}({\bm{x}}_{i})\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)

(19)

w.r.t.
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{md}
. Note that
f^{(d\times m)}
is not a probability density anymore, but
f^{(d\times m)}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m})
is finite for
\lambda_{\nu}^{(d\times m)}
-almost every
{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m}\in E_{md}^{\prime}
, since
\lambda^{(d\times m)}
is finite in every neighborhood of
{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m}\in E_{md}^{\prime}
. Moreover, it can easily be observed that for every
IJ\subset d\times m
the
IJ
-margin of
f^{(d\times m)}
is given by

\displaystyle f^{(IJ)}\left({\bm{x}}_{IJ}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{E_{md-|IJ|}}f^{(d\times m)}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m})\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{md-|IJ|}\left(\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m\setminus IJ}\right).

(20)

which is the density of
\lambda_{\nu}^{(IJ)}
w.r.t.
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{|IJ|}
.
By similar arguments, the density of
\lambda_{\alpha}
w.r.t.
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{md}
corresponding to
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{m}
is given by

\displaystyle g^{(d\times m)}\left({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m}\right):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}g_{\alpha}({\bm{x}}_{j})\mathbf{1}_{\{{\bm{x}}_{k}={\bm{\infty}}\text{ for all }k\not=j,1\leq k\leq m\}}

(21)

and the density corresponding to an
IJ\subset d\times m
margin is therefore given by

\displaystyle g^{(IJ)}\left({\bm{x}}_{IJ}\right)

\displaystyle:=\int_{E_{md-|IJ|}}g^{(d\times m)}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m})\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{md-|IJ|}\left(\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m\setminus IJ}\right).

Now, we are able to derive the regular conditional distributions of
\lambda^{(d\times m)}
, which are defined analogously to (
12
).

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\mu
be an IDEM which satisfies Condition
3
with corresponding densities
f^{(d\times m)}
and
g^{(d\times m)}
as defined in (
19
) and (
21
). Further, let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and let
IJ\subset d\times m
. Then, for every
{\bm{x}}_{IJ}\in E^{\prime}_{|IJ|}
, the conditional probability distribution of
\lambda^{(d\times m)}
is given by

K_{IJ}\left({\bm{x}}_{IJ},d{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m\setminus IJ}\right)=\frac{f^{(d\times m)}\left({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m}\right)+g^{(d\times m)}\left({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m}\right)}{f^{(IJ)}\left({\bm{x}}_{IJ}\right)+g^{(IJ)}\left({\bm{x}}_{IJ}\right)}\left(\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{md-|IJ|}\right)(\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m\setminus IJ})\text{ a.s.}.

A consequence of Lemma
5.2
is that we can obtain a simpler representation of
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}
in the posterior representation of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Under the same conditions as in Lemma
5.2
and conditionally on the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
and
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
, the survival function of
\left({\bm{Z}}^{(n,l)}_{j}\right)_{1\leq j\leq k}
from (
18
) takes the form

\displaystyle P\left(\bm{Z}^{(n,l)}_{j}>{\bm{x}}_{j}\text{ for all }1\leq j\leq k\mid\tilde{\Theta},{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{K({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})+\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\underset{1\leq j\leq k}{\otimes}\minid(\eta)\left(\big\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k}\mid{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k}>{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k}\big\}\right)h(\eta,{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)}{C({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})},

where
C({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})
denotes the corresponding normalizing constant and
