h(\eta,{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l}):=\int_{\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}>{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}\}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{\eta}\left(({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\underline{{\bm{y}}}_{n})(\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\right)\left(\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{nd-|\tilde{\Theta}_{l}|}\right)\left(\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}\right),

K({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l}):=\int_{\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}>{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}\}}g^{(d\times n)}\left(({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n})(\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\right)\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{nd-|\tilde{\Theta}_{l}|}\left(\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}\right)

with
({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\underline{{\bm{y}}}_{n})(\tilde{\Theta}_{l}):=\left(\mathbf{1}_{\{(i,j)\in\tilde{\Theta}_{l}\}}X_{i,j}+y_{i,j}\mathbf{1}_{\{(i,j)\not\in\tilde{\Theta}_{l}\}}\right)_{(i,j)\in d\times n}.

Thus, the distribution of
\left({\bm{Z}}^{(n,l)}_{j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is the mixture of min-id distributions and it can be generated as follows. First, draw a Bernoulli random variable
B:=B({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})
with success probability
1-K({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})/C({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})
. If
B=0
, set
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{j}={\bm{\infty}}
for all
1\leq j\leq k
, which corresponds to
{\bm{Z}}^{(n,l)}_{j}\sim\minid(\overline{\mu}^{(l)}_{n})
with
\overline{\mu}^{(l)}_{n}=\infty\delta_{{\bm{\infty}}}
. Otherwise, simulate a measure
\overline{\mu}^{(l)}_{n}
from the appropriately normalized version of
h(\eta,{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)
and then simulate sequences of i.i.d. random vectors
\left({\bm{Z}}^{(n,l)}_{j}\right)_{1\leq j\leq k}
with distribution
\minid\big(\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}\big)
. It should be mentioned that
K({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})=0
whenever
\tilde{\Theta}_{l}
contains more than one distinct
j
index or
\alpha=\infty\delta_{\bm{\infty}}
, which implies
B=1
almost surely.
For a full representation of the posterior it remains to derive the distribution of the conditional hitting scenario.

Lemma 5.4
.

Under the same conditions as in Lemma
5.2
, the distribution of the conditional hitting scenario is given by

\displaystyle\tau({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta})=

(22)

\displaystyle\frac{\prod_{l=1}^{L(\tilde{\Theta})}\int_{\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}>{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}\}}\left(f^{(d\times n)}+g^{(d\times n)}\right)\left(({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\underline{{\bm{y}}}_{n})(\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\right)\left(\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{nd-|\tilde{\Theta}_{l}|}\right)\mathrm{d}\left({\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}\right)}{\sum_{\hat{\Theta}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{l=1}^{L(\hat{\Theta})}\int_{\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\hat{\Theta}_{l}}>{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n\setminus\hat{\Theta}_{l}}\}}\left(f^{(d\times n)}+g^{(d\times n)}\right)\left(({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\underline{{\bm{y}}}_{n})(\hat{\Theta}_{l})\right)\left(\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{nd-|\hat{\Theta}_{l}|}\right)\mathrm{d}\left({\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\hat{\Theta}_{l}}\right)}.

The results of this section are summarized in the following theorem, which provides a tractable formula for the posterior of an IDEM prior under Condition
3
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
\mu
denote and IDEM which satisfies Condition
3
and assume that
\alpha=\infty\delta_{{\bm{\infty}}}
. Then, the posterior of the corresponding exchangeable min-id sequence
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
and
\mu
is given by

\left({\bm{X}}_{n+j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mid{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}\sim\minid\left(\overline{\mu}\right),\ \ \ \mu\mid{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}\sim\overline{\mu}=\overline{\mu}_{n}+\sum_{l=1}^{L(\tilde{\Theta})}\overline{\mu}^{(l)}_{n}.

where
\overline{\mu}_{n}
is an IDEM with Lévy characteristics
\left(\alpha,\overline{\nu}_{n}\right)
,
\left(\tilde{\Theta},\left(\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}\right)_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}\right)
are independent of
\overline{\mu}_{n}
, the distribution of
\tilde{\Theta}
is determined by Lemma
5.4
and, conditionally on
\tilde{\Theta}
, the
\left(\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}\right)_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}
are independent random measures with distribution
C({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})^{-1}

\left(h(\eta,{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)+K({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\delta_{(\infty\delta_{{\bm{\infty}}})}\right)
as defined in Lemma
5.3
.

6
A multivariate model for partially exchangeable data with continuous dependence at the root

In this section, we illustrate the flexibility of our framework by constructing a model for multivariate survival analysis. The setting is as follows. Assume that one observes survival data from different groups and that the goal is to learn the survival function of individuals in the different groups. We assume that the observations are homogeneous within a group and heterogeneous between the groups, while assuming that the groups share some common, but unknown, structure. In the Bayesian setting such data is commonly modeled by a partially exchangeable framework, where the dimension
d
represents the number of groups. The shared structure between the groups then allows for the so-called “borrowing of information”, i.e. to learn about the survival function in one group from observations in other groups.

Mathematically, the assumption of partial exchangeability translates to the prior distribution concentrating on the space of product probability measures, see e.g.
[
14
,
27
,
29
,
6
]
for examples of such frameworks. All the latter models have in common that their prior is constructed in such a way that the shared structure between the groups is identical, i.e. if the prior has a (smoothed) atom at a certain location then its occurrence is either present in all groups or its occurrence is completely independent from the other groups. The technical reason for this is that the shared structure is usually discrete, since this is technically more convenient to handle. Let us exemplarily explain this for the framework of
[
6
]
, which was already discussed in detail in Example
6
, while similar comments apply to the frameworks of
[
14
,
27
,
29
]
. The completely random measure
\mu_{0}
at the root, which represents the shared structure, is a discrete measure. This implies that the hazard rates of the individual groups are sums of terms of the form
a_{i}\kappa(s,y_{i})
, where the
(y_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are the same for all groups. The standard choice
\mathbb{Y}=[0,\infty)
and
\kappa(s,y)=a\mathbf{1}_{\{s\geq y\}}
implies that the prior assumes that each group has a jump in the hazard rate starting at the same locations
y_{i}
. This assumption might be seen as restrictive for survival data, since, even though the hazard rates should be “similar”, there is no particular reason why the locations
y_{i}
should be identical across groups.

Here, we propose a model which solely assumes that an increase in the hazard rate at a certain location in a certain group implies that the probability of an increase in the hazard rate in the other groups close to this location is higher, which reflects the intuition that increases in hazard rates should be “similar” but not identical. On a technical level, this will be achieved by choosing a continuous IDEM at the root
\mu_{0}
in the construction of the subordinated CRMs in Proposition
3.3
as follows. Choose a CRM
\mu_{0}
on
[0,\infty)
with base measure
\alpha_{0}
and Lévy measure
\nu_{0}
given by the image measure of
(a,b)\mapsto a\delta_{b}
under
l(a,b)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}b
. Further, choose a kernel
\kappa_{0}
and define
\mu^{(\kappa_{0})}_{0}([0,t]):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa_{0}(s,y)\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s
. Now,
\mu^{(\kappa_{0})}
is used as the common intensity measure for the construction of
d
conditionally independent CRMs without base measure, i.e. we use the continuous
\mu^{(\kappa_{0})}
as the shared structure for the
d
different groups. Choosing
d
kernels
(\kappa_{i})_{{1\leq i\leq d}}
and marginal intensities
\left(\rho_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq d}
we can define the corresponding IDEM
\mu_{\mu^{(\kappa_{1})}_{1},\ldots,\mu^{(\kappa_{d})}_{d}}
as in (
10
).
Intuitively speaking, the construction implies that the
\mu_{i}
will be dependent but with distinct atoms almost surely. Consequently,
\minid\left(\mu_{\mu^{(\kappa_{1})}_{1},\ldots,\mu^{(\kappa_{d})}_{d}}\right)
is a model for partially exchangeable data with marginal survival function
\exp\left(-\mu^{(\kappa_{i})}_{i}\big((-\infty,x_{i}]\big)\right)
, i.e. for
{\bm{X}}\sim\minid(\mu)

\mathbb{P}\left({\bm{X}}>{\bm{x}}\mid\mu\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\exp\left(-\mu_{i}^{(\kappa_{i})}\big((-\infty,x_{i}]\big)\right).

We need to impose some regularity conditions on the
\kappa_{i}
and
\mu_{i}
such that the proposed model is well-defined.

Condition 4
.

The kernels
(\kappa_{i})_{0\leq i\leq d}
satisfy

(K1)

There exists a
C>0
such that
\kappa_{i}(s,b)\leq C
for all
s,b>0
. Moreover, there exists a
\delta,\tilde{C}>0
such that for every
b>0
and all
\tilde{s}\in(b,b+\delta)
we have
\kappa_{i}(\tilde{s},b)>\tilde{C}
.

(K2)

For every
t>0
there exists a
C>0
such that
\kappa_{i}(s,b)=0
for all
s\leq t
and
b>C
.

In addition, we require

(C1)

l(a,b)>0
for all
a,b>0
,
\int_{0}^{\infty}l(a,b)\mathrm{d}a=\infty
for every
b>0
and
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\min\{a;1\}

l(a,b)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}b<\infty
for all
t>0
. Moreover, for every
1\leq i\leq d
we have
\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a=\infty
.

(C2)

Either
\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa_{i}(s,b)\mathrm{d}s=\infty
for all
1\leq i\leq d
and
b>0
or
\mu_{0}(\mathbb{R})=\infty
almost surely.

Examples of kernels satisfying Condition
4
(K1)-(K2) are e.g. the Dykstra-Laudt kernel
\kappa(s,b)=\tau\mathbf{1}_{\{s\geq b\}}
, the rectangular kernel
\kappa(s,b)=a\mathbf{1}_{\{|s-b|\leq\tau\}}
and the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck kernel
\kappa(s,b)=\sqrt{2\tau}\exp\left(-\tau(s-b)\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{s\geq b\}}
, where
a,\tau>0
. The conditions on the
\mu_{i}
are very mild and most likely satisfied for all models that are used in practice.
Under Condition
4
one can verify that
\mu_{\mu^{(\kappa_{1})}_{1},\ldots,\mu^{(\kappa_{d})}_{d}}
is a well-defined IDEM and that Condition
3
is satisfied, which is summarized in the following Lemma. To simplify the notation in the following we denote for

e_{\eta,\kappa}(x)=\left(\mathbf{1}_{\{x<\infty\}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa(x,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)+\mathbf{1}_{\{x=\infty\}}\right)\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa(s,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s\right)

any exponent measure
\eta
and kernel
\kappa
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\mu_{\mu^{(\kappa_{1})}_{1},\ldots,\mu^{(\kappa_{d})}_{d}}
be constructed as in Proposition
3.3
, where the
\mu_{i}
are CRMs without base measure and intensities
\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\mu_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)
, and
\mu_{0}
is a CRM with base measure
\alpha_{0}
and intensity
l(a,b)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}b
. Additionally assuming that Condition
4
is satisfied, we have that
\mu_{i}^{(\kappa_{i})}([0,t])\in(0,\infty)
for all
t>0
,
\mu_{\mu^{(\kappa_{1})}_{1},\ldots,\mu^{(\kappa_{d})}_{d}}
is an IDEM without base measure and Lévy measure
\nu=\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}
where

\nu_{1}(A)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\{\infty\delta_{\bm{\infty}}\neq\otimes_{j=1}^{i-1}\delta_{\infty}\times(a\delta_{b})^{(\kappa_{i})}\otimes_{j=i+1}^{d}\delta_{\infty}\in A\}}\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\alpha^{(\kappa_{0})}_{0}(\mathrm{d}b)

and

\displaystyle\nu_{2}(A)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\times_{i=1}^{d}M_{d}}\mathbf{1}_{\big\{\mu_{\eta^{(\kappa_{1})}_{1},\ldots,\eta^{(\kappa_{d})}_{d}}\in A\big\}}\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\crm_{\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}(\mathrm{d}\eta_{i})l(a_{0},b_{0})\mathrm{d}a_{0}\mathrm{d}b_{0}.

Moreover,
\mu_{\mu^{(\kappa_{1})}_{1},\ldots,\mu^{(\kappa_{d})}_{d}}
satisfies Condition
3
and the corresponding densities w.r.t.
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{d}
are given by
g_{\alpha}=\infty\mathbf{1}_{\{{\bm{\infty}}\}}
,

f_{\otimes_{j=1}^{i-1}\delta_{\infty}\times(a\delta_{b})^{(\kappa_{i})}\otimes_{j=i+1}^{d}\delta_{\infty}}({\bm{x}})=e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa_{i}}(x_{i})\mathbf{1}_{\{{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{E}_{i}\cup\{{\bm{\infty}}\}\}}

and

f_{\mu_{\eta^{(\kappa_{1})}_{1},\ldots,\eta^{(\kappa_{d})}_{d}}}({\bm{x}})=\prod_{i=1}^{d}e_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}(x_{i}).

Theorem
5.5
now directly provides the representation of the posterior of
\mu_{\mu^{(\kappa_{1})}_{1},\ldots,\mu^{(\kappa_{d})}_{d}}
. We remark that the model is structurally conjugate, as every random measure
\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}
appearing in the posterior in Theorem
5.5
is constructed as in (
10
). To see this, observe that
\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}
is again constructed via a vector of conditionally independent random measures as follows, assuming
\alpha_{0}=\infty\delta_{\infty}
for simplicity. Draw a baseline random measure

(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}
, where
(a_{0},b_{0})\sim K(a_{0},b_{0},{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta})C({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta})^{-1}l(a_{0},b_{0})\mathrm{d}a_{0}\mathrm{d}b_{0}
and

K(a_{0},b_{0},{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}):=\mathbb{E}_{\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\crm_{\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}}\left[h\left(\mu_{\eta_{1}^{(\kappa_{1})},\ldots,\eta_{d}^{(\kappa_{d})}},{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}\right)\right].

Construct a vector of univariate random measures
\eta_{i}
whose law is given by

P\left((\eta_{i})_{{1\leq i\leq d}}\in A\right)=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\crm_{\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{(\eta_{i})_{{1\leq i\leq d}}\in A\}}h\left(\mu_{\eta_{1}^{(\kappa_{1})},\ldots,\eta_{d}^{(\kappa_{d})}},{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}\right)\right]}{K(a_{0},b_{0},{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta})^{-1}}.

Then, set
\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}=\mu_{\eta_{1}^{(\kappa_{1})},\ldots,\eta_{d}^{(\kappa_{d})}}
according to (
10
).

It remains to find tractable formulas for the conditional hitting scenarios, which by Lemma
5.4
is the case if we can express
f^{(IJ)}
from (
20
) in a tractable way.

Lemma 6.2
.

In the setting of Lemma
6.1
, the density
f^{(IJ)}
w.r.t.
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{|IJ|}
associated to
\mu_{\mu^{(\kappa_{1})}_{1},\ldots,\mu^{(\kappa_{d})}_{d}}
is given by

\displaystyle f^{(IJ)}\left({\bm{x}}_{IJ}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{1}_{\{x_{k,j}=\infty\forall(k,j)\in IJ,k\not=i\}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\prod_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa_{i}}(x_{i,j})\right)\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{\eta_{i}\sim CRM_{\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}}\Bigg[\prod_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}e_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}(x_{i,j})\Bigg]l(a_{0},b_{0})\mathrm{d}a_{0}\mathrm{d}b_{0}

(23)

Finally, let us verify that our model allows a group-dependent increase in the hazard rate around a shared center
b_{0}
. Assume for simplicity that
\alpha_{0}=\infty\delta_{\infty}
,
d=2
,
\kappa_{0}(s,y)=\mathbf{1}_{\{|x-y|\leq\tau\}}
and
\kappa_{1}(s,y):=\kappa_{2}(s,y):=\mathbf{1}_{\{s\geq y\}}
.
Then, the hazard rate corresponding to
e_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}
is simply
\eta_{i}([0,s])
, which increases in the interval
[b_{0}-\tau,b_{0}+\tau]
and is constant thereafter, while the increase is determined by the realization of
\eta_{i}
. If we would have chosen to use
\mu_{0}
instead of
\mu_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}
as our shared structure between the groups
\eta_{i}
would have only had one atom at
b_{0}
and the hazard rate corresponding to
e_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}
would jump at
b_{0}
and be constant thereafter. Thus, combining this with the discussion above Lemma
6.2
, the
\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}
induce similar, but not identical, increases in the hazard rates in the
d
groups, which reflects the intuition we have announced for the motivation of this model.

6.1
Simulation from the posterior

To complement the theoretical derivations in this section, let us illustrate the results in a simulation study. As we have access to the posterior predictive distribution, we develop a simulation algorithm that allows us to draw random vectors which, conditionally on
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
and
\overline{\mu}
, follow the distribution

\left({\bm{X}}_{n+j}\right)_{1\leq j\leq k}\mid{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}\sim\minid\left(\overline{\mu}\right),\ \ \ \mu\mid{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}\sim\overline{\mu}=\overline{\mu}_{n}+\sum_{l=1}^{L(\tilde{\Theta})}\overline{\mu}^{(l)}_{n},

where
k\in\mathbb{N}
and the distributions of
\bar{\mu}_{n}
and
\left(\bar{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}\right)_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}
are described in Theorem
5.5
and further specified in Lemmas
6.1
and
6.2
above. Since the simulation from this model is rather complex we will only give a rough summary of the main ideas here and refer to
Appendices
8.3
and
9
for a detailed discussion of a general framework for the simulation from the posterior of a min-id prior. Moreover, the simulation algorithm for the simulation from the posterior of the specific model from this section is discussed in Supplementary Material
10
, where we also provide practical comments on the implementation. The code to reproduce the simulation study can be found at
https://github.com/florianbrueck/IDEM
.

In contrast to the first intuition, we generally do not suggest to try to first simulate
\bar{\mu}_{n}
and
\left(\bar{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}\right)_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}
and then to simulate from the corresponding min-id distribution, but to exploit known simulation approaches from extreme value theory as introduced in
[
10
]
and
[
5
]
. At the core, the reason for this suggestion is that simulation from a min-id distribution is a non-trivial task. In our setting, the exponent measure
\bar{\mu}
is random and every draw of
\bar{\mu}
provides a new exponent measure with possibly different characteristics. Thus, it seems rather challenging to develop a general simulation algorithm that can easily deal with changing realizations of the exponent measure. On the other hand. the advantage of relying on simulation algorithms from extreme value theory is that they essentially allow to focus on the simulation from three fixed objects. As already described in Section
4
, the posterior distribution of
\left({\bm{X}}_{n+j}\right)_{1\leq j\leq k}
can be represented as

\left({\bm{X}}_{n+j}\right)_{1\leq j\leq k}\sim\big\{{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k},{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n)}_{k}\big\},

where, by (
16
) and (
17
),
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k}
is distributed as a min-id random vector with exponent measure

\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\otimes_{1\leq j\leq k}\minid(\eta)(\cdot)\overline{\nu}_{n}(\mathrm{d}\eta)=:\bar{\lambda}^{(n)}_{k}

and
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n)}_{k}
is distributed as the first
k
vectors in the exchangeable sequence
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n)}
from (
18
). From (
18
), we recall that
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n)}_{k}
can be represented as
\min_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
where each
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
is distributed as the first
k
vectors in an exchangeable sequence of random vectors whose distribution is determined by the conditional distribution of the exponent measure of the prior
\lambda_{\nu}
and the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
, already invoking that
\lambda_{\alpha}
vanishes in our current setting.
The key point to observe is that this representation of
\left({\bm{X}}_{n+j}\right)_{1\leq j\leq k}
allows simulation from a min-id distribution with fixed exponent measure
\lambda_{k}^{(n)}
and, as we show in Supplementary Material
10
, the simulation of each
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}_{k}^{(n,l)}
essentially requires the same “ingredients” as the simulation of
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k}
.
Therefore, the suggested simulation procedure for the posterior is to simulate
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k}
and
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n)}_{k}
separately and to take the componentwise minimum of the two resulting vectors to obtain
\left({\bm{X}}_{n+j}\right)_{1\leq j\leq k}
.

In total, this allows to break down the procedure into three main steps. First, one has to simulate from a fixed min-id distribution with exponent measure
\bar{\lambda}^{(n)}_{k}
, second one needs to simulate from the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
and, third, one has to simulate random vectors
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
whose distribution is determined by the conditional distribution of the exponent measure
\lambda_{\nu}
. Nevertheless, all three steps require simulation from complicated objects, which are also far more complex than those objects that routinely appear in extreme value theory. Thus, the already developed procedures in extreme value theory are not easily applicable here and, even though the general framework follows the same logical steps, an application of this simulation strategy in a Bayesian framework still requires significant theoretical developments and practical considerations. Again, we refer to Appendices
8.3
,
9
and
10
for more details. The only thing that we want to mention here is that the developed simulation algorithm is based on an MCMC scheme whose underlying densities are evaluated via automatic differentiation of closed-form representable Laplace transforms, which is believed to be a novel concept that has applications beyond the scope of this paper.

The main aim of this section is to provide a proof-of-concept of the practical feasibility of this simulation strategy by considering a toy example with
d=2
groups and
6
observations per group, which are given by
\{0.47,0.16,3.01,1.32,0.91,0.17\}
and
\{0.24,1.42,0.96,1.11,0.14,1.87\}
, respectively. We have chosen the same hyperparameters for each group of observations such that the two groups share an a-priori identical survival function, while allowing for group-specific deviations in the posterior. For example, this type of data could represent survival times in two related patient populations or two slightly different treatment variants. We fix
k=1
and simulate
1000
samples from the posterior predictive distribution of
{\bm{X}}_{n+1}
. Due to space constraints, we only present here only the plot of the posterior predictive survival function in Figure
1
, which is obtained as the empirical survival function of the samples from posterior. Figure
1
clearly shows that the posterior shrinks the prior towards the mostly smaller observed event times. Therefore, it showcases that the posterior is able to learn from the data, even for a small sample size. Additional explanations of the setup, simulation results and discussions can be found in Supplementary Material
10.3
.

Fig 1:
Prior (dotted) and posterior predictive (solid) survival functions. Opaque vertical lines mark the observed event times within the groups (blue,red). The dashed, dashed-dotted and dotted vertical lines represent the empirical mean of the observations within the group, the posterior mean and the prior mean, respectively. The yellow shaded regions are the Dvoretzky-Kiefer-Wolfowitz confidence bands.

7
Discussion and Outlook

This paper introduces a large class of prior distributions based on IDEMs, which can be seen as a generalization of NTR priors to the multivariate and continuous setting. The framework is shown to be general as it embeds the frameworks of
[
16
,
13
,
28
,
18
,
14
,
27
,
29
,
6
]
. Further, it is shown how to construct tractable IDEM priors from simple building blocks. As a by-product, the paper also extends the concept of subordination of CRMs by CRMs to subordination of CRMs by IDRMs, which allows to construct vectors of dependent IDRMs with tractable Lévy characteristics and is of independent interest beyond the scope of this paper. In contrast to most of the previous literature, the derivation of the posterior is not based on conditioning on a latent structure, but rather on a de-Finetti type argument, which allows for a closed-form representation of the posterior in terms of the posterior predictive distribution and, under some regularity conditions, also of the IDEM.

A reader who is familiar with extreme value theory may have already noticed that the whole framework may be re-interpreted in the viewpoint of this field. Essentially, the results of this paper say that it is possible to conduct nonparametric Bayesian inference on the exponent measure of a min-id random vector. Thus, this work can be seen as the natural framework for nonparametric Bayesian inference for min-id distributions in the context of extreme value theory as well and an investigation of this topic is deferred to future research.

Finally, an extension of the current framework to observations from continuous real-valued stochastic processes seems to be in reach, since the results of
[
12
]
apply to observations from stochastic processes as well. However, there a some technical subtleties that deserve a careful treatment. For example, it remains to find a suitable extensions of hitting scenarios, since these are inherently defined for observations at finitely many locations and an analog on the continuous process level has to be found. Moreover, it is not straightforward to formulate a suitable analog of classical finite-dimensional IDRMs in the infinite-dimensional setting, e.g. such as infinite-dimensional NTR priors. We leave these issues as on open problem for future investigation.

[Acknowledgments]
I would like to thank Matthias Scherer and Jan-Frederik Mai who have hinted me towards this topic and encouraged me to explore this line of research while doing my PhD at the Technical University Munich.

†
funding:
This work was supported by the Swiss National Science Foundation under Grant 186858.

Supplementary Material

Supplementary material for Infinitely divisible priors for multivariate survival functions

This document contains the supplementary material for Infinitely divisible priors for multivariate survival functions. It contains additional theoretical results, examples and the proofs for the main paper.

Contents

8
Introduction to min-id processes

This section provides a more thorough introduction to min-id processes
\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\in T}
, where
T
is an arbitrary index set. The purpose of this section is to foster the understanding of their stochastic representation and the concepts of extremal functions, hitting scenarios and their conditional distributions. We present these concepts for min-id processes indexed by an arbitrary index sets
T
as the author firmly believes that the process-view simplifies the understanding of the key concepts as their full beauty and elegancy is only unveiled on this level. Nevertheless, sequences and finite-dimensional min-id vectors are also treated as the index sets
T=d\times\mathbb{N}
and
T=\{1,\ldots,d\}
represent these cases. This section is heavily based on
[
5
,
12
]
and the author refers the interested reader to those publications for the technical details. As a general remark, we want to mention that most of the results in the literature are expressed for max-id processes, the negative of min-id processes, as this is more convenient in the field of extreme value theory. Therefore, many of the results presented below cannot be found in the exact same form elsewhere, as we had to translate the results for max-id processes to min-id processes, which is however the only discrepancy to be found in the following.

8.1
Stochastic representation: The extremal functions and the hitting scenario

Minimum-infinitely divisible (min-id) random vectors
{\bm{X}}
naturally arise as weak limits of scaled (componentwise) minima of independent random vectors of the form

\bm{a}_{n}\left(\min_{{1\leq i\leq n}}{\bm{Y}}^{(i)}-\bm{b}_{n}\right)\to{\bm{X}},

where
\bm{a}_{n}\in(0,\infty)^{d}
,
\bm{b}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\left({\bm{Y}}^{(i)}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are independent random vectors, see
[
19
]
for the details. The most important subclass of min-id distribution is the subclass of min-stable distributions, which comprises all possible limit laws of i.i.d. sequences
\left({\bm{Y}}^{(i)}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. Min-stable distributions are well-studied as they constitute the main objects of interest in extreme-value theory, see
[
32
]
for a thorough textbook account.

Min-id processes
{\underline{\bm{X}}}=(X_{t})_{t\in T}
are the continuous time analog of min-id random vectors and arise as the possible limits of scaled minima of independent stochastic processes of the form

\underline{\bm{a}}_{n}\left(\min_{{1\leq i\leq n}}{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(i)}-\underline{\bm{b}}_{n}\right)\to{\underline{\bm{X}}},

where
\underline{\bm{a}}_{n}\in(0,\infty)^{T}
,
\underline{\bm{b}}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}
are deterministic sequences and
\left({\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(i)}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are independent stochastic processes, see

[
17
,
2
]
for details.
Equivalently, a stochastic processes
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
is minimum-infinitely divisible (min-id) if for every given
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exist i.i.d. stochastic processes
\left({\underline{\bm{X}}}^{(i,n)}\right)_{1\leq i\leq n}
such that
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
is distributed as their componentwise minima, i.e.

\displaystyle{\underline{\bm{X}}}=\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\in T}\sim\left(\min_{1\leq i\leq n}X^{(i,n)}_{t}\right)_{t\in T}=:\min_{{1\leq i\leq n}}{\underline{\bm{X}}}^{(i,n)},

(24)

explaining the nomenclature minimum-infinitely divisible.

Under the assumption that
t\mapsto X_{t}
is continuous, real-valued and that
\sup\{x\in\mathbb{R}\mid\mathbb{P}\left(X_{t}\leq x\right)=1\}=\infty
for all
t\in T
, which we also assume for the remainder of this section,
[
17
,
2
]
show that
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
can be represented as the componentwise minimum of the atoms of a (usually infinite) PRM
N=\sum_{{i\in\mathbb{N}}}\delta_{f_{i}}
on the space of continuous functions

C(T):=\{f\mid f:T\to(-\infty,\infty]\text{ is continuous}\}

i.e.,

\displaystyle{\underline{\bm{X}}}\sim\min_{{i\in\mathbb{N}}}f_{i}.

(25)

The intensity measure
\lambda(\cdot):=\mathbb{E}\left[N(\cdot)\right]
of the PRM
N
is also called the exponent measure of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
and it uniquely characterizes its distribution via

P\left(X_{t_{i}}>x_{i}\text{ for all }{1\leq i\leq n}\right)=\exp\left(-\lambda\left(\{f\mid f(t_{i})\leq x_{i}\text{ for some }{1\leq i\leq n}\}\right)\right)

Conversely, for every PRM
N=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{f_{i}}
on
C(T)
such that
\lambda\left(\{f\mid f(t)<\infty\}\right)=\infty
and
\lambda\left(\{f\mid f(t)\leq a\}\right)<\infty
for all
t\in T
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
one can define a corresponding continuous min-id process via

{\underline{\bm{X}}}:=\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\in T}:=\left(\min_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}(t)\right)_{t\in T}.

One should observe that for
T=d\times\mathbb{N}
or
T=\{1,\ldots,d\}
every min-id process is continuous and thus the stochastic representation above holds for all min-id processes with those index sets.

When working with min-id processes it is very useful to introduce the concept of extremal functions and hitting scenarios. To be able to understand these concepts it is crucial to observe that for
{\bm{t}}:=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
the value of
{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}:=(X_{t_{1}},\ldots,X_{t_{k}})
is fully determined by the atoms of the so-called extremal point measure at
{\bm{t}}
given by

\displaystyle N^{+}_{\bm{t}}:=\sum_{f\in N}\delta_{f}\mathbf{1}_{\big\{f(t_{i})=X_{t_{i}}\text{ for some }1\leq i\leq k\big\}}.

(26)

Thus, all atoms of the so-called subextremal point measure at
{\bm{t}}
given by

\displaystyle N^{-}_{\bm{t}}:=\sum_{f\in N}\delta_{f}\mathbf{1}_{\big\{f(t_{i})<X_{t_{i}}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq k\big\}}

(27)

are irrelevant when we are solely interested in
{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}
. The atoms of
N^{+}_{\bm{t}}
, resp.
N^{-}_{\bm{t}}
, are called the extremal, resp. subextremal, functions at
{\bm{t}}
.
[
12
, Section 2]
formally analyzes the extremal and subextremal point measures and show that they are indeed well-defined random measures. Moreover, they show that
N_{\bm{t}}^{+}
has finitely many atoms almost surely and that there is almost surely one extremal function per location
t_{i}
.

Collecting those indices of
{\bm{t}}
for which the same atom of
N^{+}_{\bm{t}}
is responsible for seeing the observation
{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}
one obtains a partition
\Theta=\left(\Theta_{i}\right)_{1\leq l\leq L(\Theta)}
of
{\bm{t}}
of length
L(\Theta)
, which is called the hitting scenario of
{\bm{t}}
. More precisely, we can write

N^{+}_{\bm{t}}=\sum_{l=1}^{L(\Theta)}\delta_{e_{l}}

where
e_{l}
are the extremal functions at locations
\Theta_{l}
, i.e. those functions for which

X_{t}=e_{l}(t)\text{ for all }t\in\Theta_{l}.

Thus, the hitting scenario encodes the locations at which an extremal function determines the observation
{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}
.

Fig.
2
illustrates the extremal and subextremal functions of a continuous max-id process on
\mathbb{R}
with
{\bm{t}}=(0,1,\ldots,5)
and hitting scenario
\Theta(\{0,\ldots,5\})=\big\{\{0,1,2,5\},\{3,4\}\big\}
.

Fig 2:
Illustration from
[
5
]
of extremal and subextremal functions of a PRM
N
on
C(\mathbb{R})
. Functions in solid-blue belong to
N^{+}_{(0,\ldots,5)}
, functions in dashed-red belong to
N^{-}_{(0,\ldots,5)}
. The hitting scenario is given by
\Theta(\{0,\ldots,5\})=\big\{\{0,1,2,5\},\{3,4\}\big\}

8.2
Conditional distribution of min-id processes

It turns out that the concept of extremal functions and hitting scenarios is crucial to understand the conditional distribution of min-id processes.
The core idea to derive their conditional distributions is to split the conditional distribution of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
given
{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}
into two parts:

1.

The conditional distribution of the extremal point measure
N^{+}_{\bm{t}}
, encoded via the conditional hitting scenario with the corresponding extremal functions given by
\left(\tilde{\Theta},(e_{l})_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}\right)
.

2.

The conditional distribution of the subextremal point measure
N_{\bm{t}}^{-}
.

From the discussion above it is obvious that the conditional hitting scenario with the corresponding extremal functions determines the conditional distribution of the extremal point measure and together with the conditional distribution of the subextremal point measure one can fully describe the conditional distribution of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
. Intuitively, one first reconstructs the random vector
{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}
via the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
and the extremal functions
(e_{l})_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}
and then reconstructs the remaining part of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
via the conditional distribution of the subextremal point measure. The corresponding stochastic representation of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
given
{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}
is then given by

{\underline{\bm{X}}}\mid{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}\sim\min\big\{{\underline{\bm{Z}}},{\underline{\bm{Y}}}\big\}

where

\displaystyle{\underline{\bm{Y}}}\sim\min_{f\in N^{-}_{\bm{t}}}f\text{ and }{\underline{\bm{Z}}}\sim\min_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta}({\bm{t}}))}e_{l},

(28)

and we use the notation
f\in N
to say that
N
has an atom at
f
.

Let us start with the conditional distribution of
N^{-}_{\bm{t}}
given
N^{+}_{\bm{t}}
, which is derived in
[
11
, Lemma 3.2]
. The authors show that the conditional distribution of
N^{-}_{\bm{t}}
given
N^{+}_{\bm{t}}
is equal to the distribution of a PRM with intensity

\mathbf{1}_{\{f(t_{i})>X_{t_{i}},1\leq i\leq k\}}\mathrm{d}\lambda(f).

Thus, the conditional distribution of the subextremal point measure
N^{-}_{\bm{t}}
given
N^{+}_{\bm{t}}
actually solely depends on
{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}
and thus it is also the conditional distribution of
N^{-}_{\bm{t}}
given
{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}
. In other words, the conditional distribution of
N^{-}_{\bm{t}}
given
{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}
is equal to the distribution of the PRM underlying the stochastic representation of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
subject to the restriction that neither of its atoms changes the value
{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}
, which is encoded by
\mathbf{1}_{\{f(t_{i})>X_{t_{i}},1\leq i\leq k\}}
. This directly implies that
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}
is again a min-id process whose exponent measure is given by
\mathbf{1}_{\{f(t_{i})>X_{t_{i}},1\leq i\leq k\}}\lambda(\mathrm{d}f)
.

It remains to describe the conditional distribution of
N^{+}_{\bm{t}}
in terms of the conditional hitting scenario and the conditional distribution of the extremal functions. We start with the conditional distribution of the extremal functions given the conditional hitting scenario, since the distribution of the conditional hitting scenario is more involved. Intuitively, conditionally on the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
, one needs to find functions
e_{l}
such that

e_{l}(t)=X_{t}\text{ for all }t\in\tilde{\Theta}_{l}\text{ and that }e_{l}(t)>X(t)\text{ for all }t\in\cup_{1\leq l\neq\underline{l}\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}\tilde{\Theta}_{\underline{l}},.

It turns out that the distribution of those functions can be described by the conditional distribution of the exponent measure
\lambda
. As
\lambda
is usually an infinite measure, let us describe how we define the conditional distribution of an infinite measure, reyling on the description on
[
12
, Appendix A2]
. Formally, the conditional distribution of
\lambda
is defined as follows. For every
{\bm{t}}\subset T
and function
G:(-\infty,\infty]^{k}\times C(T)\to[0,\infty)
such that
G({\bm{\infty}},C(T))=0
there is a disintegration formula for
\lambda
given by

\int G\left((f(t))_{t\in{\bm{t}}},(f(t))_{t\in T\setminus{\bm{t}}}\right)\lambda(\mathrm{d}f)=\int G\left({\bm{x}},(f(t))_{t\in T\setminus{\bm{t}}}\right)K_{\bm{t}}\left({\bm{x}},\mathrm{d}(f(t))_{t\in T\setminus{\bm{t}}}\right)\lambda^{({\bm{t}})}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}),

where
K_{\bm{t}}({\bm{x}},\cdot)
is a probability kernel on
C(T\setminus{\bm{t}})
for every
{\bm{x}}\in(-\infty,\infty]^{k}\setminus{\bm{\infty}}
and
\lambda^{({\bm{t}})}
denotes the
{\bm{t}}
-margin of
\lambda
given by

\lambda^{({\bm{t}})}(A)=\lambda\left(\{f\mid(f(t))_{t\in{\bm{t}}}\in A\right)\}).

The existence and well-definedness of
K_{\bm{t}}({\bm{x}},\cdot)
is provided in
[
12
]
. Crucially, they also show that

\displaystyle P\left(e_{l}\in A_{l},1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})\mid\tilde{\Theta},{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{l=1}^{L(\tilde{\Theta})}K_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}\left({\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}},\{f_{l}\in A_{l};\ (f(t))_{t\in{\bm{t}}\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}>{\bm{X}}_{{\bm{t}}\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}})\}\right),

(29)

i.e., conditionally on the hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
and
{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}
, the extremal functions
(e_{l})_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}
are independent with distribution determined by the conditional distributions of
\lambda
.

To fully describe the conditional distribution of a min-id process it solely remains to describe the distribution of the conditional hitting scenario. The crucial ingredient is a family of finite measures

\left(\beta_{\theta}\right)_{\theta\in\mathcal{P}_{{\bm{t}}}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
which are defined via

\beta_{\theta}(A):=P\left({\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}\in A,\Theta=\theta\right),

where
\mathcal{P}_{{\bm{t}}}
denotes the set of all possible partitions of
{\bm{t}}
. Intuitively,
\beta_{\theta}
can be viewed as the (non-normalized) distribution of
{\bm{X}}_{{\bm{t}}}
when the hitting scenario
\Theta
is fixed to
\theta
. Therefore, we also have that
\beta:=\sum_{\theta\in\mathcal{P}_{{\bm{t}}}}\beta_{\theta}
is equal to the distribution of
{\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}
since

\beta(A):=\sum_{\theta\in\mathcal{P}_{\bm{t}}}\beta_{\theta}(A)=P({\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}\in A)

Trivially, each
\beta_{\theta}
is absolutely continuous w.r.t.
\beta
with a density
d\beta_{\theta}/d\beta
on
\mathbb{R}^{|IJ|}
.
[
12
, Theorem 3.2.1]
provides that the distribution of the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
is given by

\tau({\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}},\theta):=P\left(\tilde{\Theta}=\theta\right)=P\left(\Theta=\theta\ \big|\ {\bm{X}}_{{\bm{t}}}\right)=\frac{d\beta_{\theta}}{d\beta}({\bm{X}}_{\bm{t}}).

Thus, one “solely” has to find the Radon-Nikodym derivative
d\beta_{\theta}/d\beta
to determine the distribution of the conditional hitting scenario. However, since the relation of the
\beta_{\theta}
is generally rather complex, providing more intuitive and explicit expressions of
d\beta_{\theta}/d\beta
is difficult. A starting point can be that, also due to
[
12
]
,
\beta_{\theta}
has the representation

\displaystyle\beta_{\theta}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})=\exp\left(-\lambda^{({\bm{t}})}\left(({\bm{x}},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)\right)\prod_{l=1}^{L(\theta)}K_{\theta_{l}}\left({\bm{x}}_{\theta_{l}},\big\{(f(t))_{t\in{\bm{t}}\setminus\theta_{l}}>{\bm{x}}_{{\bm{t}}\setminus\theta_{l}}\big\}\right)\otimes_{l=1}^{L(\theta)}\lambda^{(\theta_{l})}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}_{\theta_{l}}).

(30)

For example, when each
\lambda^{(\theta_{l})}
has a
|\theta_{l}|
-dimensional Lebesgue density, it is relatively easy to see that standard conditional density arguments can be employed to find
d\beta_{\theta}/d\beta
.

8.3
A high level overview of the simulation of the conditional distribution of a min-id process

Lastly, we want to provide some intuition about the simulation from the conditional distribution of a min-id process, which is one potential approach to simulate from the posterior of an exchangeable min-id process. However, we take a rather high-level approach as the details strongly depend on the specific model at hand and a schematic overview will help the reader to better grasp the general ideas. From the previous subsection it is clear that a simulation from the conditional distribution of a min-id process requires the simulation from the two stochastic processes
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}
and
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}
which were defined in (
28
).

We already know that
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}
is a min-id process and therefore simulation algorithms for min-id processes can be employed, such as the one proposed in
[
5
]
. The general idea of the simulation algorithm is simple. One starts with an initialization of the extremal function
e_{1}
corresponding to
Y_{t}
, where the location
t
is arbitrary. Then one exploits that, conditionally on
Y_{t}
,
N_{t}^{-}
is again distributed as a PRM with intensity measure
\mathbf{1}_{\{f(t)>e_{1}(t))\}}\lambda(\mathrm{d}f)
to obtain
Y_{s}
at another location
s
. This is possible since the only atoms of
N_{t}^{-}
that are relevant to determine
Y_{s}
are those atoms
f
which satisfy
f(s)<e_{1}(s)
, as the remaining atoms are subextremal at location
s
. Due to the fact that
\lambda\left(\{f\mid f(s)<a\}\right)<\infty
for all
a\in\mathbb{R}
, this only requires the simulation of finitely many atoms of the corresponding PRM and thus is feasible in practice. Iterating this procedure for the remaining locations of interest allows to obtain an exact simulation of
{\bm{Y}}_{\bm{t}}
for arbitrary many locations
{\bm{t}}
. The possibility of obtaining an initialization of the extremal function
e_{1}
that we implicitly assumed above is not a complication. The results of the previous subsection show that one only needs to be able to simulate from the marginal distribution of
Y_{t}
and then to simulate from
K_{t}(Y_{t},\cdot)
to obtain them. For the technical details of this algorithm we refer to
[
5
]
.

Assuming that the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}_{l}
has already been simulated, the simulation from
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}\sim\min_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}
boils down to simulating each
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}
. Their distribution is given by
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}\sim K_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}({\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}},\cdot)
and they are independent of each other, as follows from (
29
). The form of
K_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}
is highly model dependent and the feasibility of simulation from
K_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}({\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}},\cdot)
must be assessed case-by-case. The same is true for simulation from the conditional hitting scenario. In general, it is not easy to derive its distribution and, even if one is able to do so, it is still highly challenging to provide exact simulation algorithms as the size of its state space is the
|{\bm{t}}|
-th Bell number, which grows superexponentially. Thus, one usually must resort to an MCMC scheme to simulate from the conditional hitting scenario, as is described in Section
9.2
below.

9
A general recipe for simulation from the posterior of min-id prior

In this section, we want to provide a discussion of the potential simulation approaches from the posterior of a min-id prior. Even though the posterior distribution of a min-id prior is known analytically, certain statistical functionals of interest are usually only accessible by simulating from the posterior, e.g. credible intervals of
S_{\mu}(\bm{t})
, which is why access to a posterior simulation algorithm is usually essential in applications. Here, we focus the discussion on the simulation from the posterior predictive distribution, i.e. on the simulation of the conditional distribution of an exchangeable min-id sequence.
As a consequence, in contrast to the standard approach in Bayesian nonparametrics, this means that we suggest to not try to simulate from
\overline{\mu}\sim\mu\mid{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
, but to simulate from
\left({\bm{X}}_{n+i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq k}\mid{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
to derive such quantities. The reason that we follow this approach is that direct simulation from the random measure
\overline{\mu}
can be quite involved, since it usually does not have a simple generating process. Further, even when
\overline{\mu}
could be simulated, one still needs to simulate from
{\bm{X}}_{n+i}\sim\minid(\overline{\mu})
in many cases, which is a non-trivial problem itself as simulation from a min-id distribution corresponding to a given exponent measure is non-trivial. It is to be expected that for each realization of
\overline{\mu}
one needs to adapt the simulation scheme of
\minid(\overline{\mu})
, which seems unrealistic in practice.

On the contrary, a simulation algorithm for
\left({\bm{X}}_{n+i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq k}\mid{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
can be based on two different approaches. First, one can extend existing simulation algorithms for conditional distributions of min-id processes. This approach will, in theory, always work and we present it as a general recipe. Second, when Condition
3
is satisfied, one can resort to more classical MCMC algorithms to simulate the conditional distributions of min-id processes.

First, let us discuss a general simulation algorithm, which only requires Condition
2
to be satisfied. To simplify the notation, we define
m=n+k
and note that we need to simulate from the conditional distribution of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{m}
given
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
. Then, recalling the stochastic representation (
15
) of
\left({\bm{X}}_{n+j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mid{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}\sim\min\{{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)},{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n)}\}
, the simulation from
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{m}
boils down to simulating from three objects. First, one needs to simulate the first
k
components from the exchangeable min-id sequence
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}
, denoted as
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k}
. Second, one needs to simulate a conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
and, third, conditionally on the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
, one needs to simulate the first
k
components from the
L(\tilde{\Theta})
random sequences
\left(\left({\bm{Z}}_{j}^{(n,l)}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\right)_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}
, denoted as
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
, respectively. We discuss the simulation of each of these objects separately in the following paragraphs.

(I)
Simulation of
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k}
:
To simulate
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k}
, one might think of first simulating
\overline{\mu}_{n}
and then, conditionally on
\overline{\mu}_{n}
, simulating a random vector from
\minid(\overline{\mu}_{n})
. However, as explained above, this is quite challenging since simulating the IDEM
\overline{\mu}_{n}
is non-trivial and simulation from
\minid(\overline{\mu}_{n})
for changing realizations of
\overline{\mu}_{n}
is also difficult. Therefore, it is generally quite difficult to use this procedure to generate samples from
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k}
. Fortunately, exact simulation schemes for min-id processes have been developed in
[
5
]
. The main advantage of this simulation algorithm is that it does not require to simulate from
\overline{\mu}_{n}
to obtain
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k}
. Instead, it uses concepts from extreme value theory to iteratively simulate
{\bm{Y}}^{(n)}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{Y}}^{(n)}_{k}
. Essentially, the simulation algorithm only requires that one is able to simulate from the probability measure corresponding to the appropriately normalized version of
\otimes_{i=1}^{k}\minid(\eta)\left(\big\{\cdot\cap\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k}\mid x_{i,j}<a\}\big\}\right)\overline{\nu}_{n}(\mathrm{d}\eta)=:Q^{(i,j)}_{k,a}(\cdot)
for every
(i,j)\in d\times k
and
a<\infty
. Note that this still involves sampling a random exponent measure
\eta
, but this is usually simpler than sampling
\overline{\mu}_{n}
, since
Q_{k,a}^{(i,j)}
is basically a mixture of min-id distributions with mixing measure of the form
\overline{\nu}_{n}(\mathrm{d}\eta)
. For example, when simulating from the posterior of a univariate NTR prior, simulation from
Q_{k,a}^{(i,j)}
boils down to simulating
2
-dimensional random vectors instead of a CRM
\overline{\mu}_{n}
with infinitely many atoms, see the discussion in
[
5
, Remark 7]
. Intuitively, the simulation algorithm only requires to simulate the few relevant atoms
{\underline{\bm{y}}}^{(e)}
of the PRM
\sum_{e\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{{\underline{\bm{y}}}^{(e)}}
with intensity
\lambda_{\alpha}+\lambda_{\overline{\nu}_{n}}
such that there is some
(i,j)\in d\times k
such that
Y^{(n)}_{i,j}=y^{(e)}_{i,j}
. Thus, one can use the simulation algorithm of
[
5
]
to simulate from
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k}
and we refer the reader directly to the paper for the details.

(II)
Simulation of
\tilde{\Theta}\sim\Theta\mid{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
:
Simulating from the conditional hitting scenario is quite involved, since there is a very large number of states that the random partition might assume and there is no simple formula for its distribution. For exponent measures of max-stable distributions which have a density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure an approximate simulation algorithm based on a Gibbs sampling procedure has already been proposed in
[
10
]
. Investigating their algorithm in detail reveals that, even though it is explicitly formulated for max-stable distributions, it essentially does not rely on the max-stability assumption. Further, the condition of assuming a density of the exponent measure w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure can also be relaxed to exponent measures having a density w.r.t.
\Lambda_{dn}^{(\infty)}
, including those min-id distributions arising from IDEMs satisfying Condition
3
. Essentially, the approach constructs a Gibbs sampler of the conditional hitting scenario, exploiting the conditional distribution of the conditional hitting scenario when one index
(i,j)
is deleted from the partition. The precise mathematical derivations and a more detailed explanation is deferred to Section
9.2
.

(III)
Simulation of
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
:
Provided a realization of the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
, it remains to simulate from the
L(\tilde{\Theta})
random vectors
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
, whose distribution is determined via the conditional distributions
K_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}({\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}},\cdot)
. Those distributions are certainly highly model dependent and it is difficult to try to find a general recipe of simulating from
K_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}
, since
\lambda
, and thus
K_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}
, could be discrete, continuous or a mixture of both types of distributions.
When the exponent measure
\lambda^{(IJ)}
has a density w.r.t.
\Lambda_{|IJ|}^{(\infty)}
, in particular exponent measures arising from IDEMs satisfying Condition
3
, the derivations in Section
5
show that a general recipe for the the simulation of
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
can be described as follows. Essentially, one has to simulate a
\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}
from a probability measure of the form
h(\eta,{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)
and then, conditionally on
\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}
, one has to simulate i.i.d. random vectors with distribution
\minid\left(\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}\right)
. Again, it is to be expected that simulating
\overline{\mu}_{n}^{(l)}
is simpler than directly simulating
\overline{\mu}
.

To summarize, in this general simulation framework the simulation of
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k}
and
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
requires the application of the algorithm of
[
5
]
to simulate from several (simpler) min-id random vectors. In both cases, an application of
[
5
]
essentially requires to first simulate an
\tilde{\eta}\sim\tilde{h}(\eta)\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)
for some density
\tilde{h}
and then to simulate i.i.d. random vectors with distribution
\minid(\tilde{\eta})
. It is expected that simulation from
\tilde{h}(\eta)\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)
can still be non-trivial and it has to be checked case by case if simulation from these random measures is feasible. Furthermore, the simulation from the conditional hitting scenario is difficult and must be treated case-by-case, since there is generally no closed form representation of its probability distribution available.

9.1
An MCMC scheme under Condition
3

Under Condition
3
, the density of
\lambda^{(IJ)}
w.r.t.
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{|IJ|}
is available in closed form and one can resort to a fully MCMC-driven approach.
First, Lemma
5.4
provides a closed form representation of the conditional distribution of the conditional hitting scenario and therefore the Gibbs sampler from Supplementary Material
9.2
below can be used for its simulation in step
(II)
. Moreover, Lemma
5.3
applies and the density of each
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
is known in closed-form. Thus, step
(III)
above can be replaced by an MCMC scheme whose density is proportional to
\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}f_{\eta}({\bm{x}}_{j})h(\eta,{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)+K({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})
. Moreover, step
(I)
essentially requires to simulate from
Q_{k,a}^{(i,j)}
whose density is proportional to
\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}f_{\eta}({\bm{x}}_{j})\mathbf{1}_{\{x_{i,j}<a\}}\overline{\nu}_{n}(\mathrm{d}\eta)+g^{(d\times k)}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k})\mathbf{1}_{\{x_{i,j}<a\}}
. Thus, steps
(I)
,
(II)
and
(III)
can be fully conducted via a (nested) MCMC scheme.
The bottleneck of this simulation approach is that the MCMC scheme usually involves the evaluation of high dimensional integrals, since
h(\eta,{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})
is defined in terms of high-dimensional integrals. In practice, evaluation of these integrals is usually quite difficult and it has to be assessed case-by-case if such evaluations can be conducted with reasonable accuracy. For example, for our model for partially exchangeable data from Section
6
a similar reasoning as for the proof of Lemma
6.2
yields that
h(\eta,{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})
is available in closed form and
\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}f_{\eta}({\bm{x}}_{j})h(\eta,{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)
can be represented in terms of a
2
-dimensional Lebesgue integral and an expectation w.r.t to the law of a CRM. Supplementary Material
10
shows how to practically apply the suggested MCMC scheme for steps
(I)
,
(II)
and
(III)
to our model for partially exchangeable data from Section
6
.

9.2
Gibbs sampler for conditional hitting scenarios

In this subsection we extend the Gibbs sampling algorithm of
[
10
, Section 3]
, which provides a simulation algorithm for the conditional hitting scenarios of max-stable processes whose exponent measure has a Lebesgue density. We mimic their arguments to show that the algorithm can be extended to yield an algorithm for the simulation of the conditional hitting scenario of min-id distributions whose exponent measure has a density w.r.t.
\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{nd}
. In essence, the goal is to construct a Gibbs sampler on the space of partitions of
d\times n
for the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}\sim\Theta\mid{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
based on

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{\Theta}\in\cdot\mid\tilde{\Theta}_{\setminus(i,j)}=\theta_{\setminus(i,j)}\right),

(31)

where
\tilde{\Theta}_{\setminus(i,j)}
(resp.
\theta_{\setminus(i,j)}
) denotes the partition of
d\times n\setminus\{(i,j)\}
resulting from deleting the index
(i,j)
from the partition
\tilde{\Theta}
(resp.
\theta
).

The idea is to use (
31
) to consecutively update the states, i.e. the subset of the partition that
(i,j)
belongs to, in a classical Gibbs sampling scheme. The conditional distribution in (
31
) is convenient in a Gibbs sampling scheme, since there are at most
L(\theta_{\setminus(i,j)})+1\leq nd
partitions
\tilde{\theta}
such that
\tilde{\theta}_{\setminus(i,j)}=\theta_{\setminus(i,j)}
.

To describe
(\ref{eqngibbshitscen})
in closed-from let us assume that Condition
3
is satisfied. Then, Lemma
5.4
states that the distribution of the conditional hitting scenario
\tau({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\cdot)
is given by

\displaystyle\tau({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\theta)=C({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n})^{-1}\prod_{l=1}^{L(\theta)}v(\theta_{l},{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n})

where

v(\theta_{l},{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n})=\int_{\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\theta_{l}}>{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n\setminus\theta_{l}}\}}\left(f^{(d\times n)}+g^{(d\times n)}\right)\left(({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\underline{{\bm{y}}}_{n})(\theta_{l})\right)\left(\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{nd-|\theta_{l}|}\right)\mathrm{d}\left({\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\theta_{l}}\right)

and
C({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n})
is a constant that only depend on
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
,
recalling the notation
({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\underline{{\bm{y}}}_{n})(\theta_{l}):=\left(\mathbf{1}_{\{(i,j)\in\theta_{l}\}}X_{i,j}+y_{i,j}\mathbf{1}_{\{(i,j)\not\in\theta_{l}\}}\right)_{(i,j)\in d\times n}
.
Therefore, (
31
) can be written as

\displaystyle P\left(\tilde{\Theta}=\theta^{\star}\mid\tilde{\Theta}_{\setminus(i,j)}=\theta_{\setminus(i,j)}\right)=\frac{\tau({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\theta^{\star})\mathbf{1}_{\{\theta^{\star}_{\setminus(i,j)}=\theta_{\setminus(i,j)}\}}}{\sum_{\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\tau({\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n},\tilde{\theta})\mathbf{1}_{\{\tilde{\theta}_{\setminus(i,j)}=\theta_{\setminus(i,j)}\}}}

\displaystyle\propto\mathbf{1}_{\{\theta^{\star}_{\setminus(i,j)}=\theta_{\setminus(i,j)}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{L(\theta^{\star})}v(\theta^{\star}_{l},{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n})\propto\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\{\theta^{\star}_{\setminus(i,j)}=\theta_{\setminus(i,j)}\}}\prod_{l=1}^{L(\theta^{\star})}v(\theta^{\star}_{l},{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n})}{\prod_{l=1}^{L(\theta)}v(\theta_{l},{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n})}

(32)

A simple combinatorial argument shows that on the right-hand side of (
32
) at most
4
factors remain, since there are at most two subsets in the partition
\theta^{\star}
that are not in
\theta
and vice versa. Thus, since we have at most
L(\theta_{\setminus(i,j)})+1
possible partitions
\theta^{\star}
with positive probability, we have at most
4dn
factors to compute in each step of the Gibbs sampling scheme, which yields a practically feasible was to simulate the conditional hitting scenario. One should note that the estimate of
4dn
states with positive probability is a worst-case estimate and that the number of partitions with positive probability is usually much smaller than
4dn
since usually
L(\theta_{\setminus(i,j)})+1<<dn
.

10
Simulation of a model for partially exchangeable data

We want to simulate from the posterior of our model for partially exchangeable data with prior

\left(\mu^{(\kappa_{i})}_{i}\right)_{{1\leq i\leq d}},\ \left(\mu_{i}\right)_{{1\leq i\leq d}}\ \overset{indep}{\sim}\crm_{\rho_{i}(a)\mu_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)},\ \mu_{0}\sim\crm_{l(a,b)\mathrm{d}(a,b)},

which was introduced in Section
6
, assuming that
\mu_{0}
has continuous base measure
\alpha_{0}
while the
\left(\mu_{i}\right)_{{1\leq i\leq d}}
do not have a base measure. Further, we assume that we are given observations
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}=\left(X_{i,j_{i}}\right)_{1\leq i\leq j_{i},1\leq i\leq d}
from a partially exchangeable array. To avoid extra notation, we still use
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n)}
,
\left({\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}\right)_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}
and
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}
to denote the sequences appearing in the stochastic representation of the posterior of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}\mid{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
in (
15
), even though we condition on
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
instead of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
. Moreover, to define a proper survival model, we will assume throughout this section that the CRMs
(\eta_{i})_{0\leq i\leq d}
and kernels
(\kappa^{(i)})_{0\leq i\leq d}
are concentrated on
(0,\infty)
, which ensures that
{\underline{\bm{X}}}\in(0,\infty)^{d\times\mathbb{N}}
.

We want to follow the simulation approach suggested in Section
9.1
. For the simulation from the posterior we need to simulate three types of objects: the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}=(\tilde{\Theta}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\Theta}_{L})
with the associated
\left({\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}\right)_{1\leq l\leq L}
as well as the min-id random vector
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}
. Section
5
provides closed-from representations of their distribution in terms of the posterior density. However, in practice, these formulas are difficult to evaluate numerically and the bottleneck for simulation from the posterior via MCMC methods is the speed of the evaluation of these densities. Our strategy to tackle this issue is to first identify that all the involved densities share the same ‘‘structure’’ and that their fast and accurate numerical approximation boils down to the fast evaluation of the same type of object. This object will be represented in terms of partial derivatives of the Laplace transform of the underlying IDRMs and we will heavily rely on the automatic differentiation libraries of JAX to enable fast numerical evaluation of the partial derivatives and associated integrals.
1
1

1

The author has also tried to evaluate the densities with classical numerical integration methods combined with a Monte-Carlo scheme, but this approach turned out to be way too slow in practice.

Let
\eta
denote a CRM without base measure and intensity
\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)
,
J_{1},J_{2}\subset\mathbb{N}
some disjoint index sets and let
{\bm{x}}_{J_{1}}\in[0,\infty)^{|J_{1}|}
as well as
{\bm{x}}_{J_{2}}\in[0,\infty]^{|J_{2}|}
. The key observation for the following derivations is that the common “structure” appearing in all of the densities below will be terms of the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in J_{1}}e_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\right],

(33)

where we define

e_{\eta,\kappa}(x):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa(x,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa(s,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s\right)

and

E_{\eta,\kappa}(x):=\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa(s,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s\right).

There is no simple way to compute these expectations for a given CRM
\eta
, but recognizing that
e_{\eta,\kappa}(x)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial x}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x)
we can rewrite

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in J_{1}}e_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(y_{j})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in J_{1}}\frac{-\partial}{\partial x_{j}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\right].

Now, if we were allowed to interchange expectation and the derivatives, we would obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in J_{1}}\frac{-\partial}{\partial x_{j}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{\eta}(x_{j})\right]=\left(\prod_{j\in J_{1}}\frac{-\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in J_{1}\cup J_{2}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\right],

with

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in J_{1}\cup J_{2}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{i,j})\right]=:L_{\kappa,\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)}({\bm{x}}_{J_{1}},{\bm{x}}_{J_{2}})

denoting the Laplace transform of the random vector

\left(\left(\int_{0}^{x_{j}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa(s,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s\right)_{j\in J_{1}},\left(\int_{0}^{x_{j}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa(s,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s\right)_{j\in J_{2}}\right)

evaluated at
\bm{1}=(1)_{j\in J_{1}\cup J_{2}}
.
By Proposition
3.2
,
L_{\kappa,\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)}
is available in closed form and thus an evaluation of (
33
) boils down to calculating its derivatives.

In the argument above, we have assumed that we are allowed to interchange expectation and derivation. This is however non-trivial as
x\mapsto E_{\eta}(x)
is only differentiable Lebesgue-almost everywhere since
x\mapsto\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa(x,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)
is, in general, not continuous. For example, when
\kappa(s,y)=\mathbf{1}_{\{s\geq y\}}
is the Dykstra-Laudt kernel then
\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa(x,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)=\eta([0,x])
, which is cádlág but not continuous. Therefore, an application of Leibniz’ rule is precluded to interchange the expectation and differentiation. Nevertheless, the following lemma shows that we can interchange expectation and differentiation under general regularity conditions.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
\eta
denote a CRM on
[0,\infty)
with intensity
\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)
and let
\kappa
denote a kernel which satisfies Condition
4
. Let
J_{1},J_{2}\subset\mathbb{N}
denote two disjoint finite index sets and let
{\bm{x}}_{J_{1}}=\left(x_{j}\right)_{j\in J_{1}}\in[0,\infty)^{|J_{1}|}
and
{\bm{x}}_{J_{2}}:=\left(x_{j}\right)_{j\in J_{2}}\in[0,\infty]^{|J_{2}|}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in J_{1}}e_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\right]=\left(\prod_{j\in J_{1}}\frac{-\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)L_{\kappa,\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)}({\bm{x}}_{J_{1}},{\bm{x}}_{J_{2}})

and for
|J_{1}|>1
we have

\displaystyle(-1)^{|J_{1}|}\left(\prod_{j\in J_{1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\log\left(L_{\kappa,\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)}\left({\bm{x}}_{J_{1}},{\bm{x}}_{J_{2}})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{j\in J_{1}}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(x_{j})\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(x_{j})\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b).

\left(\otimes_{k=1}^{|J_{1}|}\Lambda\right)\otimes\left(\otimes_{k=1}^{|J_{2}|}\Lambda^{(\infty)}\right)
-almost everywhere.

Lemma
10.1
will be the central ingredient in developing a fast simulation routine for the posterior as it will allow us to calculate complex expectations via automatic differentiation of the Laplace transforms of univariate IDEMs, which are usually available in closed form due to Proposition
3.2
.

10.1
A convenient representation of the posterior densities

Let us now use the results of Lemma
10.1
to represent the posterior densities in a convenient form. To simplify the exposition we assume that for
1\leq i\leq d
we have
\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa_{i}(s,y)\eta_{i}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s=\infty
almost surely and we let
(\eta_{i})_{{1\leq i\leq d}}\overset{\text{indep}}{\sim}CRM_{\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}\mathrm{d}b}
be fixed.
First, we obtain from (
20
) that the density of
\lambda^{(IJ)}
is given by

\displaystyle f^{(IJ)}\left({\bm{x}}_{IJ}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{1}_{\{x_{k,j}=\infty\forall(k,j)\in IJ,k\not=i\}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\prod_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa_{i}}(x_{i,j})\right]\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\alpha^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)

\displaystyle+\mathbf{1}_{\{{\bm{x}}_{IJ}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{\eta_{i}}\Bigg[\prod_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}e_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}(x_{i,j})\Bigg]l(a_{0},b_{0})\mathrm{d}a_{0}\mathrm{d}b_{0},

since for every non-zero
\eta
we have
\int_{0}^{\infty}\eta([0,s])\mathrm{d}s=\infty
and
g(\infty)=\infty\delta_{\infty}
.

Conditional density of the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
:

By Lemma
5.4
, the distribution of the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
is proportional to

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{L(\tilde{\Theta})}\int_{\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{IJ\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}>{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{IJ\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}\}}f^{(IJ)}\left(({\bm{X}_{IJ}},{\bm{y}}_{IJ})(\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\right)\left(\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{|IJ|-|\tilde{\Theta}_{l}|}\right)\mathrm{d}\left({\underline{\bm{y}}}_{IJ\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}\right),

recalling the notation
({\bm{X}_{IJ}},{\bm{y}}_{IJ})(\tilde{\Theta}_{l}):=\left(\mathbf{1}_{\{(i,j)\in\tilde{\Theta}_{l}\}}X_{i,j}+y_{i,j}\mathbf{1}_{\{(i,j)\not\in\tilde{\Theta}_{l}\}}\right)_{(i,j)\in IJ}
. Next, (
32
) from Supplementary Material
9.2
gives that the conditional distribution of the conditional hitting scenario without index
(i,j)

P(\tilde{\Theta}=\theta^{\star}\mid\tilde{\Theta}_{\setminus(i,j)}=\tilde{\theta}_{\setminus(i,j)})
is proportional to

\mathbf{1}_{\{\theta^{\star}_{\setminus(i,j)}=\tilde{\theta}_{\setminus(i,j)}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{L(\theta^{\star})}v(\theta^{\star}_{l},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})\propto\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\{\theta^{\star}_{\setminus(i,j)}=\tilde{\theta}_{\setminus(i,j)}\}}\prod_{l=1}^{L(\tilde{\theta}^{\star})}v(\theta^{\star}_{l},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{L(\tilde{\theta})}v(\tilde{\theta}_{l},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})}

where the factors
v(\theta_{l},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})
are given by

\displaystyle v(\theta_{l},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{1}_{\{\theta_{l}\subset i\times n\}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\prod_{j:(i,j)\in\theta_{l}}e_{a\delta_{b}}(X_{i,j})\prod_{j:(i,j)\in IJ\setminus\theta_{l}}E_{a\delta_{b}}(X_{i,j})\right]\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{\eta_{i}}\Bigg[\prod_{j:(i,j)\in\theta_{l}}e_{\eta_{i}}(X_{i,j})\prod_{j:(i,j)\in IJ\setminus\theta_{l}}E_{\eta_{i}}(X_{i,j})\Bigg]l(a_{0},b_{0})\mathrm{d}a_{0}\mathrm{d}b_{0}.\

(34)

Density of
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}
:

Next, we determine the density of
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}
from Lemma
5.3
, up to a normalizing constant.

Lemma 10.2
.

In the settting of Lemma
5.3
and conditionally on
({\bm{X}_{IJ}},\tilde{\Theta})
the density of
\left(\bm{Z}^{(n,l)}_{j}\right)_{1\leq j\leq k}=:{\underline{\bm{Z}}}_{k}^{(n,l)}
is proportional to

\displaystyle p_{{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k},\tilde{\Theta}_{l},{\bm{X}_{IJ}}):=

(35)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{1}_{\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k\setminus i\times k}={\bm{\infty}},\tilde{\Theta}_{l}\subset i\times n\}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa_{i}}(x_{i,j})\prod_{j:(i,j)\in\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa_{i}}(X_{i,j})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\prod_{j:(i,j)\in IJ\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}E_{a\delta_{b},\kappa_{i}}(X_{i,j})\Bigg)\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}\Bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}e_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}(x_{i,j})\prod_{j:(i,j)\in\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}e_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}(X_{i,j})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\prod_{j:(i,j)\in IJ\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}E_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}(X_{i,j})\Bigg]l(a_{0},b_{0})\mathrm{d}a_{0}\mathrm{d}b_{0}

for every
1\leq l\leq L
.

Density of the exponent measure of
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}
:

From Supplementary Material
8
and (
12
) it follows that, by definition of the conditional distribution of the exponent measure, for an exchangeable min-id sequence
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}
with exponent measure
\bar{\lambda}
we can represent

\bar{\lambda}(A)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bar{K}_{i,j}\left(y_{i,j},\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}\in A\}\right)\bar{\lambda}^{(i,j)}(\mathrm{d}y_{i,j})

for every
(i,j)\in d\times\mathbb{N}
, where
\bar{K}_{i,j}
denotes the conditional distribution of the exponent measure
\bar{\lambda}
at location
(i,j)
and
\bar{\lambda}_{i,j}
is the exponent measure of
{\bm{Y}}^{(n)}_{i,j}

Thus, given a PRM
N_{i,j}:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{y^{(k)}_{i,j}}
with intensity
\bar{\lambda}^{(i,j)}
we can draw independent sequences
{\underline{\bm{y}}}^{(k)}\sim\bar{K}_{i,j}(y^{(k)}_{i,j},\cdot)
and obtain that

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{{\underline{\bm{y}}}^{(k)}}

is a PRM with intensity
\bar{\lambda}
. Simulation of the univariate PRM
N_{i,j}
is simple when
\bar{\lambda}^{(i,j)}((\cdot,\infty))
can be evaluated, which in turn can be derived from (
16
) and (
17
). Further, due to partial exchangeability,
\bar{K}_{i,j}(y,\cdot)=\bar{K}_{i,1}(y,\cdot)
.
Thus, we can simulate
\bar{\lambda}\left(\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}\mid y_{i,j}>a\}\right)
exactly by first simulating the finitely many atoms
y_{i,j}^{(k)}>a
and then, for each
y_{i,j}^{(k)}
drawing independent realizations
{\underline{\bm{y}}}^{(k)}\sim K_{i,1}(y_{i,j}^{(k)},\cdot)
, see Appendices
9
and
8.3
for more details.

In our case, the exponent measure of
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k}
has conditional distributions
\bar{K}_{i,1}(y,{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k\setminus(i,1)}\in\cdot)
whose density is proportional to

\displaystyle p_{{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k},i}(y,{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k\setminus(i,1)},{\bm{X}_{IJ}}):=

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{y\setminus i\times k}={\bm{\infty}}\}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{j=1}^{k}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa_{i}}(y_{i,j})\Bigg(\prod_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}E_{a\delta_{b},\kappa_{i}}(X_{i,j})\Bigg)\rho_{i}(a)\mathrm{d}a\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}\Bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}e_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}(y_{i,j})\prod_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}E_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}(X_{i,j})\Bigg]l(a_{0},b_{0})\mathrm{d}a_{0}\mathrm{d}b_{0},

(36)

where we set
y_{i,1}:=y
to keep the notation concise.

Remark 10.3
.

From a practical perspective, it is convenient to observe that the density
p_{{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k},i}(y,{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k\setminus(i,1)},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})
from (
36
) is of the same type as the density of
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
. In fact, we can construct a “fictive” min-id sequence with corresponding “fictive” hitting scenario and show that we can simulate from
\bar{K}_{i,1}(y,{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k\setminus(i,1)}\in\cdot)
by simulating the corresponding random vector of the type
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}_{k}^{(n,l)}
. This allows us to only implement the simulation of random vectors of the type
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}_{k}^{(n,l)}
to also simulate from
\bar{K}_{i,1}(y,{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k\setminus(i,1)}\in\cdot)
(resp.
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k}
).

To understand how to construct the “fictive” min-id sequence, hitting scenario and random vector of type
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
let
n
such that
IJ\subset d\times(n-1)
and define
n_{+}:=\{n,n+1,\ldots\}
. Further, define the “reduced” min-id sequence
\underline{\bm{A}}
with index set
IJ\cup d\times n_{+}
via

\underline{\bm{A}}:=\left(X_{i,j}\right)_{(i,j)\in IJ\cup d\times n_{+}}.

Then, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, define a “fictive” hitting scenario of
IJ\cup(i,n)
by

\tilde{\Theta}^{(i)}:=\left(\tilde{\Theta}^{(i)}_{1},\tilde{\Theta}^{(i)}_{2}\right):=\big((i,n),IJ\big).

Denote
\mathbf{K}_{i,n}(y,\cdot)
as the conditional distribution of the exponent measure of
\underline{\bm{A}}_{IJ\cup d\times n_{+}}
given
A_{i,n}=y
. Let
\underline{\bm{\mathsf{Z}}}^{(\tilde{\Theta}^{(i)}_{1})}\sim\mathbf{K}_{i,n}(y,\cdot)
, which has index set
IJ\cup d\times n_{+}
and coincides with
A_{i,n}=y
on
(i,n)
.
Similarly to (
18
), define

\underline{\bm{\mathsf{Z}}}^{(n,1)}\sim\left(\bm{\mathsf{Z}}^{(\Theta^{(i)}_{1})}_{n+j}\right)_{j\geq 1}\mid\underline{\bm{\mathsf{Z}}}^{(\tilde{\Theta}^{(i)}_{1})}_{IJ}>{\bm{X}_{IJ}}.

Then, the density of
\underline{\bm{\mathsf{Z}}}^{(n,1)}_{k\setminus(i,1)}
is given by
\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{\underline{\bm{\mathsf{Z}}}^{(n,1)}_{k}}({\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k},(i,n),\bm{A}_{IJ\cup(i,n)})\mathrm{d}y_{i,1}
due to Lemma
10.2
and it is easily seen that this coincides with
p_{{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k},i}(y,{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k\setminus(i,1)},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})
.

Thus, to simulate from
\bar{K}_{i,1}(y,{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k\setminus(i,1)}\in\cdot)
we can simulate
\underline{\bm{\mathsf{Z}}}^{(n,1)}_{k}
for the fictive min-id sequence
\underline{\bm{A}}
with fictive conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}^{(i)}
and set component
(i,1)
of
\underline{\bm{\mathsf{Z}}}^{(n,1)}_{k}
equal to
y
, exploiting partial exchangeability.

Summary:
To conclude this subsection, let us summarize the main message. It is possible to simulate
\tilde{\Theta}
,
\left({\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}\right)
and
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}
via standard MCMC algorithms, but it requires fast evaluation of the densities (
34
), (
35
) and (
36
). Their efficient evaluation largely depends on the efficient evaluation of terms of the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\eta}\Bigg[\prod_{j\in J_{1}}e_{\eta,\kappa}(y_{j})\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(y_{j})\Bigg].

(37)

Due to Lemma
10.1
, these terms may be efficiently evaluated via automatic differentiation of terms of the form
L_{\kappa,\gamma(\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}b)}({\bm{x}}_{J_{1}},{\bm{x}}_{J_{2}})
, which is the strategy that we will employ in the following.

10.2
A concrete example

In this section we want to illustrate that the above-mentioned computational tricks are indeed applicable in practice. To this purpose we focus on the concrete example of a generalized gamma CRM
\mu_{0}
on
(0,\infty)
with intensity

l(a,b)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}b=a^{-1-\sigma}\exp\left(-a\right)\Gamma(1-\sigma)^{-1}\mathrm{d}aq(b)\mathrm{d}b,

where
q(b)
denotes a non-negative function such that
\int_{0}^{t}q(b)\mathrm{d}b<\infty
and
\sigma\in(0,1)
is a parameter which determines the deviation from the Gamma CRM which is obtained by choosing
\sigma=0
. For
\kappa_{0}
we choose the rectangular kernel
\kappa_{0}(s,y)=(2\tau_{0})^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{\{|s-y|\leq\tau_{0}\}}
for some
\tau_{0}>0
. Further, the marginal CRMs
\mu_{i}
are also based on generalized Gamma CRMs with identical
\sigma
and Dykstra-Laudt kernel, i.e. we choose
\rho_{i}(a):=\rho(a):=a^{-1-\sigma}\exp\left(-a\right)\Gamma(1-\sigma)^{-1}
and
\kappa_{i}(s,y):=\kappa(s,y)=\tau_{1}\mathbf{1}_{\{s\geq y\}}
.

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
\eta
denote a CRM with intensity
\rho(a)\mathrm{d}aq(b)\mathrm{d}b
and let
x_{0}:=0<x_{1}<\ldots<x_{m}<\infty
. Then

\displaystyle L_{\kappa,\rho(a)\mathrm{d}aq(b)\mathrm{d}b}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{x_{j-1}}^{x_{j}}\frac{\left(\tau_{1}\sum_{k=j}^{m}(x_{k}-b)_{+}+1\right)^{\sigma}-1}{\sigma}q(b)\mathrm{d}b\right).

For simplicity, let us set
q(b)=1
and
\alpha_{0}=\Lambda(\cdot\cap(0,\infty))
in the following. Then,
\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}\mathrm{d}b=\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa_{0}(b,y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}b
and
(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)=a_{0}\kappa_{0}(b,b_{0})\mathrm{d}b
and we obtain that for every
