a_{0},b_{0}>0

\displaystyle L_{\kappa,\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m})

(38)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-a_{0}(2\tau_{0})^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{x_{j-1}}^{x_{j}}\frac{\left(\tau_{1}\sum_{k=j}^{m}(x_{k}-b)+1\right)^{\sigma}-1}{\sigma}\mathbf{1}_{\{|b-b_{0}|\leq\tau_{0}\}}\mathrm{d}b\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigg(-a_{0}(2\tau_{0})^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\frac{\left(\tau_{1}\sum_{k=j}^{m}(x_{k}-b)+1\right)^{\sigma+1}}{-(m-j+1)\tau_{1}(\sigma+1)\sigma}\Bigg|_{b=\min\{\max\{x_{j-1},(b_{0}-\tau_{0})_{+}\};b_{0}+\tau_{0}\}}^{b=\max\{\min\{x_{j},b_{0}+\tau_{0}\};(b_{0}-\tau_{0})_{+}\}}\right)

\displaystyle+a_{0}(2\tau_{0})^{-1}\sigma^{-1}\left(\min\{x_{m};b_{0}+\tau_{0}\}-(b_{0}-\tau_{0})_{+}\right)\Bigg)

and

\displaystyle-\log\left(L_{\kappa,\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}\mathrm{d}b}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m})\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\left(\tau_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(x_{j}-b)_{+}+1\right)^{\sigma}-1}{\sigma}(2\tau_{0})^{-1}\min\{b+\tau_{0},2\tau_{0}\}\big)\mathrm{d}b,

(39)

where
(b_{0}-\tau_{0})_{+}:=\max\{b_{0}-\tau_{0};0\}
.

Therefore, applying the results from the previous subsection, the densities
p_{{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k},i}(y,{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k\setminus(i,1)},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})
,
p_{\bm{Z}^{(n,l)}}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k},\tilde{\Theta}_{l},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})
and the factors
v(\theta_{l},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})
can be evaluated via automatic differentiation of (
38
) and (
39
) in conjunction with standard numerical integration methods as the expectations of the form (
37
) appearing in (
34
), (
35
) and (
36
) can be evaluated.

It remains to describe the marginal distributions for
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}
in an implementable form. The following Lemma provides this characterizes via the marginal exponent measure
\bar{\lambda}^{(i,j)}
of
Y^{(n)}_{i,j}
in terms of expressions that have already been calculated in closed-form above.

Lemma 10.5
.

Conditionally on
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
, the exponent measure of
Y^{(n)}_{i,j}
is defined via

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}^{(i,j)}\left((x,\infty]^{\complement}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\left(\tau_{1}(x-b)_{+}+\tau_{1}\sum_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}(X_{i,j}-b)_{+}+1\right)^{\sigma}-1}{\sigma}\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\left(\tau_{1}\sum_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}(X_{i,j}-b)_{+}+1\right)^{\sigma}-1}{\sigma}\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\zeta_{k}\left(x,(X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)l(a_{0},b_{0})\mathrm{d}a_{0}\mathrm{d}b_{0},

where

\displaystyle\zeta_{k}\left(x,(X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\{i=k\}}\bigg(L_{\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}\left((X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)-L_{\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}\left(x,(X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle\ \ +\mathbf{1}_{\{i\neq k\}}L_{\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}\left((X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)

and we define
L_{\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}\left(\emptyset\right):=1
.

Thus, we may simulate a PRM
N
with intensity
\bar{\lambda}^{(i,j)}
by evaluating the integrals appearing in Lemma
10.5
, which require essentially the evaluation of the same objects appearing in the densities
p_{{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k},i}(y,{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k\setminus(i,1)},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})
and
p_{\bm{Z}^{(n,l)}}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k},\tilde{\Theta}_{l},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})
.
To summarize, we have now obtained representations of the densities
p_{{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}_{k},i}(y,{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k\setminus(i,1)},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})
,
p_{\bm{Z}^{(n,l)}}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k},\tilde{\Theta}_{l},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})
,
v(\theta_{l},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})
and the exponent measure
\bar{\lambda}^{(i,j)}
which are implementable in closed form, since all remaining expectations can be calculated via automatic differentiation.

Remark 10.6

(Proposal distributions and transition densities in Metropolis Hastings)
.

As
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}
and
{\bm{Z}}^{(n,l)}
may take infinite values in their components when
\tilde{\Theta}_{l}\subset i\times\mathbb{N}
for some
i
it is not obvious how the respective proposal distribution in an MCMC simulation scheme should look like. Thus, we here report our specific choices for the MCMC scheme that has been implemented by us.
For the Gibbs sampler of
\tilde{\Theta}
we follow the elaborations in Supplementary Material
9.2
. For the simulation of
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}
and
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}^{(n)}
we opted for the classical Metropolis-Hastings algorithm. We denote the current state by
{\bm{x}}
(resp.
\theta
) and the state that we transition to by
{\bm{x}}\prime
(resp.
\theta^{\prime}
).
Our transition distribution is based on lognormal random variables, which have density

g_{s}(x)=\frac{1}{x\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\left(\log(s)-\log(x)\right)^{2}/2\right),

where
s>0
is a scale parameter.
If
\tilde{\Theta}_{l}
contains indices from more than one row
i

{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
concentrates on
\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
and we choose i.i.d. lognormal random variables with marginal scale parameter equal to the current value as proposals, which translates to the transition density

g_{1}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k}^{\prime},{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k})=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\prod_{j=1}^{k}g_{x_{i,j}}(x^{\prime}_{i,j})\mathbf{1}_{\{x^{\prime}_{i,j}<\infty\}}\text{ w.r.t. }\Lambda_{dk}^{(\infty)}.

In the case
\tilde{\Theta}_{l}
contains indices from only one row
\underline{i}
the distribution of
{\bm{Z}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
either takes values in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
or
A_{\underline{i}}=\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k}\in(0,\infty]^{d\times k}\mid x_{\underline{i},j}\in\mathbb{R}\ \forall 1\leq j\leq d\text{ and }x_{i,j}=\infty\ \forall i\not=\underline{i}\}
and we choose i.i.d. lognormal random variables with scale parameter equal to the current value as proposals, which translates to the transition density

\displaystyle g_{2,\underline{i}}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k}^{\prime},{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}\}}}{2}\Bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{d}\prod_{j=1}^{k}g_{x_{i,j}}(x^{\prime}_{i,j})\mathbf{1}_{\{x^{\prime}_{i,j}<\infty\}}+\prod_{j=1}^{k}g_{x_{i,j}}(x^{\prime}_{i,j})\mathbf{1}_{\{x_{\underline{i},j}<\infty\}}\prod_{i=1,i\not=\underline{i}}^{d}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{\{x^{\prime}_{i,j}=\infty\}}\bigg)

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k}\in A_{\underline{i}}\}}}{2}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}g_{x_{\underline{i},j}}(x^{\prime}_{\underline{i},j})\mathbf{1}_{\{x^{\prime}_{\underline{i},j}<\infty\}}\prod_{i=1,i\not=\underline{i}}^{d}\prod_{j=1}^{k}g_{1}(x^{\prime}_{i,j})\mathbf{1}_{\{x^{\prime}_{i,j}<\infty\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\prod_{j=1}^{k}g_{x_{\underline{i},j}}(x^{\prime}_{\underline{i},j})\mathbf{1}_{\{x^{\prime}_{\underline{i},j}<\infty\}}\prod_{i=1,i\not=\underline{i}}^{d}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{\{x^{\prime}_{i,j}=\infty\}}\bigg),

w.r.t.
\Lambda_{dk}^{(\infty)}
, where we default to a scale parameter
1
when the current value is infinite.

A valid proposal distribution for
{\underline{\bm{Z}}}^{(n,l)}_{k}
is then given by
g_{2,i}({\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k}^{\prime},{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k})
, where for concise notation we have set
y_{i,1}=y
. Due to Remark
10.3
above, the same type of proposal can be used to simulate
{\underline{\bm{Y}}}_{k}^{(n)}
.

10.3
Additional simulation results

We present the detailed parametrization and additional simulation results for our model for partially exchangeable data by continuing Section
6.1
from the main text, which already contains the plot of the posterior survival function in Figure
1
. The simulation algorithm is implemented by following the roadmap laid out in the previous subsections. Recall that the observations are given by
\{0.47,0.16,3.01,1.32,0.91,0.17\}
and
\{0.24,1.42,0.96,1.11,0.14,1.87\}
. The underlying CRMs
(\mu_{i})_{0\leq i\leq 2}
were chosen as generalized gamma CRMs with identical jump intensities. More specifically, we chose
\mu_{0}
as a CRM on
(0,\infty)
with base measure
\alpha_{0}([0,t])=t
and intensity

\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}b
,
where
\rho(a):=a^{-1-\sigma}\exp\left(-a\right)\Gamma(1-\sigma)^{-1}
is the jump intensity of a generalized Gamma CRM and
\sigma\in(0,1)
is a parameter which determines the deviation from the Gamma CRM, which is obtained by choosing
\sigma=0
. As the kernel
\kappa_{0}
at the root we chose the rectangular kernel
\kappa_{0}(s,y)=(2\tau_{0})^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{\{|s-y|\leq\tau_{0}\}}
where for the margins we chose the Dykstra-Laudt kernel
\kappa(s,y)=\tau_{1}\mathbf{1}_{\{s\geq y\}}
.
To create the plots we fixed
\tau_{0}=1
,
\tau_{1}=2/3
and
\sigma=1/2
to illustrate the implied prior and posterior distributions.

Since the posterior simulation procedure is computationally demanding, the purpose of the experiment is not to explore many different simulation scenarios, but rather to provide a transparent illustration of the prior-to-posterior update in a representative small-sample setting. Let us first discuss the posterior predictive survival function in Figure
1
. The posterior predictive survival function is estimated by the empirical survival function of the posterior predictive samples, immediately providing a corresponding estimate of the posterior predictive cumulative hazard, which is provided in Figure
3
below. The Dvoretzky-Kiefer-Wolfowitz confidence bands are computed as function-valued confidence bands for the empirical survival function and then transformed to confidence bands for the cumulative hazard via a simple
\log
transformation. The survival function and the hazard rate plots both show that the posterior predictive distribution is able to learn from the data, since it shrinks the posterior towards smaller event times, reflecting the fact that our data mostly contains small event times. The effect is quite strongly visible, even though we only have
6
prior observations in each group, which suggests that the posterior quickly adapts to the observations.

Fig 3:
Prior and posterior predictive hazard rate. Colors (blue,red) distinguish the two groups and the opaque vertical lines mark the observed event times within the groups.

Another natural theoretical object of interest associated with the model is the posterior behavior of hazard-rate related functionals. Recall that one of the motivations for our model for partially exchangeable data was that an observations should trigger an increase in the hazard rate around the observations, which was theoretically verified in the discussion after Lemma
6.2
. In practice, direct estimation of hazard rates from posterior predictive samples is numerically unstable, since it requires density estimation and division by an estimated survival function. We therefore avoid using estimated hazard rates as the primary numerical diagnostic. Instead, we focus on more stable predictive summaries that can be estimated directly from the samples and visualize the outcome of a local hazard rate increase around observations.
To visualize the local effect of the observations without estimating a hazard rate, we introduce two sample-based diagnostics. The first is the average local predictive mass around the observed event times,

L_{i}(r)=\frac{1}{6}\sum_{j=1}^{6}P\left(|X_{i,n+1}-t_{i,j}^{\mathrm{obs}}|\leq r\mid{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{6}\right),

where
t_{i,1}^{\mathrm{obs}},\ldots,t_{i,6}^{\mathrm{obs}}
denote the observed event times in group
i
. The second is the probability that a new predictive draw falls close to at least one observed event time,

M_{i}(r)=P\left(\min_{1\leq j\leq 6}|X_{i,1}-t_{i,j}^{\mathrm{obs}}|\leq r\mid{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{6}\right).

In contrast to
L_{i}(r)
, which averages local probabilities over the observed event times,
M_{i}(r)
treats the union of all neighborhoods around the observed times as a single event. Both diagnostics are computed under the prior and posterior predictive distributions. If the posterior predictive curve lies above the prior predictive curve for small or moderate values of
r
, this indicates that conditioning on the data increases the predictive mass near the observed event times.
The resulting plots should therefore be interpreted as posterior predictive diagnostics rather than as direct estimates of the posterior hazard rates. They provide a stable visualization of the effect of conditioning on the data in a small-sample partially exchangeable survival model. In particular, they show whether the posterior predictive distribution moves mass toward the observed event times and how the marginal survival behavior differs from the prior distribution.

Fig 4:
The local predictive mass diagnostics
L_{i}(r)
for the prior (dotted,blue) and posterior predictive (solid, orange) distribution.

Fig 5:
The local predictive nearest-observation probability diagnostics
M_{i}
for the prior (dotted,blue) and posterior predictive (solid, orange) distribution.

Figure
4
and Figure
5
illustrate that the posterior places significantly more mass close to the observations than the prior, where the majority of the increase is already reached for a small window radius of
0.4
, even though our choice of
\tau_{0}=1
theoretically forces the hazard rate increase to be distributed in a window of radius
1
around each observation. Even though the local predictive mass increase is less pronounced in group
2
, the local predictive nearest-observation probability is only slightly less pronounced. This could be due to the fact that the observations in group
2
are less dispersed in group
2
compared to group
1
, requiring a smaller adaption in the posterior to match the observations. However, a theoretical verification of this claim is lacking. To sum up, similarly to the comments above, the plots again suggest that the posterior adapts rather quickly to the observations.

Furthermore, let us illustrate the dispersion of the posterior predictive draws via a scatter plot of the samples. A concentration of the samples around the
45
-degree reference line would indicate a strong dependence of the posterior samples, indicating that the learned common structure dominates posterior and vice versa if the observations are scattered. Figure
6
below shows a strong dispersion of the posterior samples and thus indicates that the marginal effects dominate the posterior. This is in line with results in the literature, see e.g. the related model from
[
6
]
, where the authors essentially show that posterior consistency is driven be the marginal specifications.

Fig 6:
Joint posterior predictive draws for
(X_{1},X_{2})
together with the
45
-degree reference line.

Finally, let us comment on the runtime of the simulation algorithm. In the present implementation, the main bottleneck is not the simulation logic itself but the repeated evaluation of expectations that enter the conditional densities and the probabilities of the conditional hitting scenarios. As explained above, these expectations are computed by automatic differentiation of closed-form Laplace transforms, since a standard Monte-Carlo approach was unpractically slow as it required too many draws from the corresponding additive process. However, it is still numerically expensive because the MCMC scheme has to recompute many such quantities repeatedly over the course of the Gibbs updates for the hitting scenario and in the simulation of the min-id distribution.
Moreover, for each expectation which requires differentiation to be evaluated the computational cost increases rather quickly with the number of derivatives. As a consequence, adding an observation enlarges both the dimension of the mixed-derivative calculations and the combinatorial complexity of the conditional hitting scenarios, so that more candidate states and more expensive expectation evaluations have to be handled in each sweep of the algorithm. On a standard desktop computer, this growth was already severe enough that we could not push the experiment far beyond six observations in each margin within a reasonable runtime. To give a rough intuition, a derivative calculation of order
>10
took about
500
ms on average. Considering that in each step of the MCMC schemes several such derivatives might need to be calculated, depending on the current draw of the conditional hitting scenario, it becomes apparent that a group size of
10
would lead to a long runtime for the MCMC algorithm. We want to stress that this is solely a computational issue and not a theoretical one. A natural direction for future work would therefore be to parallelize these evaluations, for example by using distributed computing on a cluster or GPU-based acceleration for the automatic-differentiation workload. Developing such a optimizations is beyond the scope of the present paper and beyond the computational resources and implementation capabilities available to the author.

11
Additional properties of min-id priors: prior moments, dependence and moments of mean functionals

Let us shortly discuss some of the properties which are implied by an IDEM prior. We express these properties in terms of the Lévy characteristics, since they correspond to the natural description of IDRMs. First, we start by characterizing the prior moments of the random survival function and the cumulative hazard rate. To keep the notation concise, for every
{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m}\in[-\infty,\infty)^{d\times m},\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, denote the
m
-dimensional Laplace transform of
\mu\left((\cdot,{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)
as

\displaystyle L_{(\alpha,\nu)}(\bm{z},{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m}):=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}\mu\left(({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(-\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}\alpha\left(({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)-\int_{M_{d}^{0}}1-\exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}\eta\left(({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)\right)\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)\bigg),

implicitly assuming that the expectations are well-defined whenever we allow for negative
z_{i}
. This allows to express the prior moments of the random survival function as well as the moments of the cumulative hazard.

Proposition 11.1
.

Let
\mu
denote an IDEM with Lévy characteristics
(\alpha,\nu)
, where we denote the survival function associated with
\minid(\mu)
as
S_{\mu}(\cdot)
. Then, upon well-definedness of the stated expectations, for every
{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m}\in[-\infty,\infty)^{d\times m},\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
(j_{k})_{1\leq k\leq m}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m}S_{\mu}({\bm{x}}_{i})^{z_{i}}\right]=L_{(\alpha,\nu)}(\bm{z},{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m})\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mu\left(({\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)^{j_{i}}\right]=(-1)^{\sum_{i=1}^{m}j_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\prod_{i=1}^{m}(\partial z_{i})^{j_{i}}}L_{(\alpha,\nu)}(\bm{z},{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m})\Big|_{\bm{z}=\bm{0}}.

Example 9
.

Proposition
11.1
can be combined with Proposition
3.2
to obtain moments of functionals of the form
m_{k,g}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{E_{d}^{\prime}}g({\bm{x}})\mu(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})\right)^{k}\right]
. Proposition
3.2
yields that
m_{k,g}
is the
k
-th moment of an infinitely divisible random variable with tractable (univariate) Lévy measure
\upsilon(A):=\nu\left(\{\eta\mid\int_{E_{d}^{\prime}}g({\bm{x}})\eta(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})\in A\}\right)
and (univariate) drift
a=\int_{E_{d}^{\prime}}g({\bm{x}})\alpha(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})
. Thus, one can differentiate the Laplace transform of
\int_{E_{d}^{\prime}}g({\bm{x}})\mu(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})

k
-times at
0
to obtain
m_{k,g}
.

Up to this point, it is not clear whether a min-id prior implies positive or negative dependence of
{\bm{X}}
or
S_{\mu}(\cdot)
. The next proposition will show that a min-id prior implies positive dependence of many statistical functionals, which is due to association. A random vector
\bm{Z}
is called associated if
\cov\left(g_{1}(\bm{Z}),g_{2}(\bm{Z})\right)\geq 0
for all functions
g_{1},g_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
which are monotone increasing in each coordinate and for which the covariance is finite. Association is a strong positive dependence property and it is well known that every min-id random vector is associated
[
31
, Section 5.6]
, which implies that
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
is associated for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, conditionally on
\mu
as well as unconditionally. Here, we will also show that real-valued functionals of
\mu
are associated and the results are summarized in the following proposition.

Proposition 11.2
.

Consider a collection of non-negative continuous functions
(f_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}
with compact support on
E_{d}^{\prime}
. Then, for every IDEM
\mu

\left(\int_{E_{d}^{\prime}}f_{1}({\bm{x}})\mu(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}),\ldots,\int_{E_{d}^{\prime}}f_{m}({\bm{x}})\mu(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})\right)

is associated. Moreover,
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
is associated for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, conditionally on
\mu
and unconditionally.

An immediate consequence of Proposition
11.2
is that
\cov\left(\mu\left(({\bm{x}}_{2},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right),\mu\left(({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)\right)

\geq 0
and
\cov(S_{\mu}({\bm{x}}_{1}),S_{\mu}({\bm{x}}_{2}))\geq 0
for all
{\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{x}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. An abstract implication of Proposition
11.2
which might be of independent interest beyond the scope of this paper is that non-negative infinitely divisible random vectors are associated, which follows by an appropriate choice of
(f_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}
and
\mu
, exploiting that association is preserved under weak convergence
[
31
, Lemma 5.32 iii]
.

Next, we turn to moments of mean functionals of the form

I(f,\mu):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x)\minid(\mu)(\mathrm{d}x),

where we assume that
d=1
and that
f:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
is monotone. Further, it is easy to see that we can focus on monotone increasing
f
, since we can derive results about monotone decreasing
f
from the distribution of the monotone increasing function
-f
.

Proposition 11.3
.

Let
\mu
denote an IDEM on
(-\infty,\infty]
with Lévy characteristics
(\alpha,\nu)
. Then, for every monotone increasing
f:(-\infty,\infty]\to[0,\infty]
with
f(\infty)=\infty
we have
I(f,\mu)\sim I(\textit{id},\mu_{f})
, where
\mu_{f}
is an IDEM on
[0,\infty]
with Lévy characteristics
(\alpha_{f},\nu_{f})
as defined in Proposition
3.2

(i)
and
id
denotes the identity function. Moreover, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[I(f,\mu)^{m}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\ldots\int_{0}^{\infty}L_{\alpha_{f},\nu_{f}}\big(\bm{1},(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})\big)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}t_{m}.

12
Discrete priors: the multivariate NTR prior

Providing explicit and general formulas for the posterior of an IDEM is difficult for priors that concentrate on discrete distributions. The technical reason for this is that the counting measure, the natural dominating measure of discrete distributions, is not
\sigma
-finite and one cannot easily find an analog of Condition
3
for discrete priors. However, as Condition
2
is routinely satisfied even by priors concentrating on discrete distributions, the results of Section
4
are applicable and one can aim to find explicit formulas for families of discrete priors that are sufficiently regular. Here, we exemplarily show how to obtain explicit formulas for the posterior in the case of the multivariate NTR (mNTR) prior, i.e. CRM priors
\mu
of the form

\displaystyle\mu(A)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}a_{k}\delta_{{\bm{b}}_{k}}(A)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{(0,\infty)^{d}}\mathbf{1}_{\{{\bm{b}}\in A\}}N(\mathrm{d}(a,{\bm{b}})),

(40)

where
N
is a PRM on
(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)^{d}
with atoms
\left((a_{k},{\bm{b}}_{k})\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. Obviously, each margin of
\mu
is a univariate CRM and thus is a univariate NTR prior.
The mNTR prior is of special interest since it is the natural multivariate extension of univariate NTR priors introduced by
[
16
,
9
]
. The univariate NTR prior has been used as a fundamental building block in many multivariate Bayesian models. Thus, the following closes a long standing gap in the literature and the results may open the door to more refined multivariate applications of (m)NTR priors.

To further simplify our developments we will assume that
\mu
has no base measure and that the Lévy measure of
\mu
is infinite and given as the image measure of

\displaystyle\left((0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)^{d},\rho(a,{\bm{b}})\mathrm{d}(a,{\bm{b}})\right);\ (a,{\bm{b}})\mapsto a\delta_{{\bm{b}}},

(41)

where
\rho(a,{\bm{b}})
is chosen such that
\int_{(\bm{t},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\min\{a,1\}\rho(a,{\bm{b}})\mathrm{d}(a,{\bm{b}})<\infty
for all
\bm{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This ensures that
\mu
is a well-defined IDEM which satisfies Condition
2
.

Before we derive the technical results underlying the posterior of an mNTR prior we present the main result of this section: the posterior of an mNTR prior. To this purpose, for any index set
IJ\subset d\times\mathbb{N}
, define
I(IJ):=\{i\mid\exists\ j\text{ s.t.\ }(i,j)\in IJ\}
with
I(IJ)^{\complement}=\{1,\ldots,d\}\setminus I(IJ)
. Similarly, define
J(IJ):=\{j\mid\ \exists{1\leq i\leq d}\text{ s.t. }(i,j)\in IJ\}
. We will show below that for every conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}=\left(\tilde{\Theta}_{l}\right)_{1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})}
with positive probability we have that for all
{1\leq i\leq d}
and
1\leq l\leq L(\tilde{\Theta})
the vector
{\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}\cap(\{i\}\times\mathbb{N})}
is a collection of ties, i.e. a vector consisting of identical values. Let us denote these unique values as
{\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}(i)
and define the
d
-dimensional vector

({\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}},\bm{B})(I(\tilde{\Theta}_{l}))=\left({\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}(i)\mathbf{1}_{\{i\in I(\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\}}+B_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{i\in I(\tilde{\Theta}_{l}^{\complement})\}}\right)_{1\leq i\leq d}

for an arbitrary vector
\bm{B}
such that
(B_{i})_{i\in I(\tilde{\Theta}_{l})^{\complement}}
is uniquely defined. To keep the notation concise, let us also denote
\times_{i=1}^{k}\int_{A_{i}}=\int_{A_{1}}\ldots\int_{A_{k}}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Theorem 12.1

(Posterior of mNTR prior)
.

Let
\mu
denote a CRM without base measure whose intensity measure is given by (
41
) and let
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
denote the corresponding exchangeable min-id sequence such that
({\bm{X}}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are conditionally i.i.d. with distribution
\minid(\mu)
. Further, let
IJ\subset d\times\mathbb{N}
denote a finite index set and choose
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
IJ\subset d\times n
. Then, conditionally on
{\bm{X}_{IJ}}
, the posterior of
\mu
is given by

\mu\mid{\bm{X}_{IJ}}\sim\bar{\mu}_{IJ}+\sum_{l=1}^{L(\tilde{\Theta})}\bar{\mu}_{IJ}^{(l)}

where

(i)

\bar{\mu}_{IJ}
is a CRM with intensity
\rho(a,{\bm{b}})\exp\left(-a\sum_{j\in J(IJ)}\mathbf{1}_{\{b_{i}\leq X_{i,j}\text{ for some }(i,j)\in IJ\}}\right)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}{\bm{b}}
.

(ii)

\tilde{\Theta}
is distributed according to

P(\tilde{\Theta}=\theta)=\tau(\theta,{\bm{X}_{IJ}})=\frac{q_{\theta}}{\sum_{\overset{\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{P}_{IJ}}{\Psi(\theta)=\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}})}}q_{\tilde{\theta}}}\left({\bm{X}_{IJ}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\Psi(\theta)=\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}})\}}

where
\Psi(\theta)
,
\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}})
and
q_{\theta}(\cdot)
are defined in (
44
),(
47
) and (
46
) below.

(iii)

Conditionally on
\tilde{\Theta}
,
\bar{\mu}_{IJ}^{(l)}\sim A\delta_{({\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}},\bm{B})(I(\tilde{\Theta}_{l}))}

where
(A,\bm{B}):=\left(A,\left((B_{i})_{i\in I(\tilde{\Theta}_{l})^{\complement}}\right)\right)
has probability density

C(\tilde{\Theta}_{l},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})^{-1}\rho_{IJ}^{(l)}\left(a,\left(b_{i}\right)_{i\in I(\tilde{\Theta}_{l})^{\complement}},{\bm{X}_{IJ}}\right)\rho\left(a,({\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}},{\bm{b}})(I(\tilde{\Theta}_{l}))\right)

on
(0,\infty)\times(-\infty,\infty)^{|I(\tilde{\Theta}_{l}))^{\complement}|}
with

\displaystyle\rho_{IJ}^{(l)}\left(a,\left(b_{i}\right)_{i\in I(\tilde{\Theta}_{l})^{\complement}},{\bm{X}_{IJ}}\right)

\displaystyle:=\otimes_{j=1}^{n}\left(\minid\left(a\delta_{({\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}},{\bm{b}})(I(\tilde{\Theta}_{l}))}\right)\right)\left(\big\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n}\ \big|\ {\bm{y}}_{IJ\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}>{\bm{X}}_{IJ\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}};\ {\bm{y}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}={\bm{X}}_{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}\big\}\right)

and
C(\tilde{\Theta}_{l},{\bm{X}_{IJ}})
denoting the corresponding normalizing constant.

Similarly to the posterior of a univariate NTR prior, the posterior of an mNTR prior
\mu
is the sum of a CRM
\bar{\mu}_{IJ}
plus atoms with random weights given by
\bar{\mu}_{IJ}^{(l)}
. However, in contrast to the univariate case, the number of atoms is random and the atoms do not necessarily coincide with the observed locations
\left({\bm{X}}_{IJ\cap(d\times\{j\})}\right)_{1\leq j\leq n}
, since the observations in the
i
-margins of
{\bm{X}_{IJ}}
only function as building blocks for the atoms of the posterior. This is due to the fact that, contrary to the univariate case, there are several conditional hitting scenarios with positive probability as it cannot be known by solely observing
{\bm{X}_{IJ}}
whether or not the observations
X_{i,j}
and
X_{\underline{i},j}
are induced by the same underlying extremal sequence, see the technical results below for more details. If
d=1
, one recovers the classical result for univariate NTR priors, i.e that the atoms in the posterior appear at each distinct entry of
\left(X_{(1,j)}\right)_{1\leq j\leq n}
.

Let us mention that the inclusion of a diffuse base measure
\alpha
into the prior
\mu
is feasible by going through the same technical steps as below. However, the formulation of the results would then require even heavier notation, which is why we refrained from treating this more general case. Furthermore, the same underlying ideas as below can be applied to CRMs that do not have an intensity of the form (
41
). However, the formulation of the results is then highly dependent on the exact form of the intensity measure and must be adapted case-by-case.

12.1
The conditional distributions of the exponent measure and hitting scenario of an mNTR prior

To derive the posterior of an mNTR prior, it is essential to understand the stochastic representation of the corresponding exchangeable min-id sequence
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
. The same arguments as in Example
4
yield that a stochastic representation of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\minid(\mu)
is given by

\displaystyle{\underline{\bm{X}}}\sim\min_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\bm{x}}^{(k)},

(42)

where, conditionally on a PRM
\tilde{N}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(a_{k},{\bm{b}}_{k})}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the
{\underline{\bm{x}}}^{(k)}
are independent i.i.d. sequences with marginal distribution
(1-\exp(-a_{k}))\delta_{{\bm{b}}_{k}}+\exp(-a_{k})\delta_{\bm{\infty}}
. Thus, the components of
{\underline{\bm{x}}}^{(k)}
concentrate on the two points
\{{\bm{b}}_{k},{\bm{\infty}}\}

As a consequence, the
i
-margins
(X_{i,j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
can have ties, resembling the familiar tie structure in the observations from a univariate NTR prior. The multivariate analog of this tie structure is encoded by the hitting scenario of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
. In contrast to the univariate case, this tie structure cannot be directly read from the data as for
i\neq\underline{i}
the observations
X_{i,j}
and
X_{\underline{i},j}
may originate from the same underlying extremal sequence
{\underline{\bm{x}}}^{(k)}
even though
X_{i,j}\neq X_{\underline{i},j}
. However, as one might expect, the tie structure in the
i
-margins of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
will also play a critical role in the posterior of an mNTR prior, since it directly determines those conditional hitting scenarios that have positive probability.

Before determining the distribution of the conditional hitting scenario, we must first derive the conditional distribution of the exponent measure
\lambda
of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
. From the stochastic representation (
42
) it directly follows that
\lambda
is given by

\displaystyle\lambda(A)=\int_{(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)^{d}}\int_{E_{d\times\mathbb{N}}}\mathbf{1}_{\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}\in A\}}\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid(a\delta_{\bm{b}})\left(\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{x}}}\right)\rho(a,{\bm{b}})\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}{\bm{b}},

(43)

for every measurable
A\subset E_{d\times\mathbb{N}}
. From (
43
) it immediately follows that
\lambda
has no mass on sets of the form

\big\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}\ \big|\ \exists\ j\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }i\neq\underline{i}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\text{ s.t.\ }x_{i,j}<\infty\text{ and }x_{\underline{i},j}=\infty\big\}

and

\big\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}\ \big|\ \exists\ j\neq\underline{j}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\text{ s.t.\ }x_{i,j},x_{i,\underline{j}}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }x_{i,j}\neq x_{i,\underline{j}}<\infty\big\}.

In other words, the
j
-th vector
{\bm{x}}_{j}
in
{\underline{\bm{x}}}
must either be finite in every component or be equal to
{\bm{\infty}}
and all finite values in
(x_{i,j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
must be identical. Therefore, when deriving the conditional distribution
K_{IJ}\left({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\cdot\right)
of
\lambda
, we focus on
{\bm{x}_{IJ}}
that comply with these restrictions a priori. More precisely, we only need to derive
K_{IJ}\left({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\cdot\right)
for

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}_{IJ}\in\mathcal{U}:=

\displaystyle\big\{{\bm{y}}_{IJ}\in E^{\prime}_{IJ}\mid\forall\ j\in J(IJ):\text{Either }{\bm{y}}_{IJ\cap(d\times\{j\})}={\bm{\infty}}\text{ or }{\bm{y}}_{IJ\cap(d\times\{j\})}\in\mathbb{R}^{|IJ\cap(d\times\{j\})|}\}

\displaystyle\cap\{{\bm{y}}_{IJ}\in E^{\prime}_{IJ}\mid\forall\ i\in I(IJ):\text{ All finite components of }{\bm{y}}_{IJ\cap(\{i\}\times\mathbb{N})}\text{ are identical}\},

recalling the notation
J(IJ)=\{j\mid\exists\ {1\leq i\leq d}\text{ s.t.\ }(i,j)\in IJ\}
.

To ease the notation in the following denote the finite values in the
i
-margins of
{\bm{x}_{IJ}}\in\mathcal{U}
as
{\bm{x}_{IJ}}(i)
, if they exist. Further, denote

I({\bm{x}}_{IJ}):=\big\{i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\mid x_{i,j}<\infty\text{ for some }(i,j)\in IJ\}
,

I({\bm{x}}_{IJ})^{\complement}=\{1,\ldots,d\}\setminus I({\bm{x}_{IJ}})
and for any
{\bm{b}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
let

({\bm{x}_{IJ}},{\bm{b}})(I({\bm{x}_{IJ}})):=\left({\bm{x}_{IJ}}(i)\mathbf{1}_{\{i\in I({\bm{x}_{IJ}})\}}+b_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{i\in I({\bm{x}_{IJ}})^{\complement}\}}\right)_{{1\leq i\leq d}}.

Note that in case
{\bm{x}}_{IJ}\in\mathbb{R}^{|IJ|}
we have that
I({\bm{x}}_{IJ})=I(IJ)=\{i\mid\exists\ j\in\mathbb{N}\text{ such that }(i,j)\in IJ\}
is simply the collection of
i
-margins that are present in
IJ
, whereas
I(IJ)^{\complement}=\{i\mid(i,j)\not\in IJ\ \forall j\in\mathbb{N}\}
is the collection of
i
-margins that are not present in
IJ
.

Proposition 12.2
.

Let
\mu
denote a CRM without base measure whose Lévy measure has intensity (
41
). Let
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
denote the corresponding exchangeable min-id sequence with exponent measure
\lambda
. Then, for any finite
IJ\subset d\times\mathbb{N}
,
{\bm{x}_{IJ}}\in\mathcal{U}
and measurable
A\subset E^{\prime}_{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}
, the conditional probability distribution of
\lambda
given
{\bm{x}_{IJ}}\in\mathcal{U}
is given by

\displaystyle K_{IJ}\left({\bm{x}}_{IJ},A\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\times_{i=1}^{|I({\bm{x}_{IJ}})^{\complement}|}\int_{0}^{\infty}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a\delta_{({\bm{x}_{IJ}},{\bm{b}})(I({\bm{x}_{IJ}}))}\right)\left(\big\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}\mid{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{\setminus IJ}\in A,{\bm{y}}_{IJ}={\bm{x}}_{IJ}\big\}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\rho\left(a,({\bm{x}_{IJ}},{\bm{b}})(I({\bm{x}_{IJ}})\right)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}\left((b_{i})_{i\in I({\bm{x}_{IJ}})^{\complement}}\right)C({\bm{x}_{IJ}})^{-1}

where
C({\bm{x}_{IJ}})
denotes the corresponding normalizing constant.

To derive the conditional hitting scenario of an mNTR prior we recall the family of measures

\left(\beta_{\theta}\right)_{\theta\in\mathcal{P}_{IJ}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{|IJ|}
given by

\displaystyle\beta_{\theta}(A):=P\left({\bm{X}_{IJ}}\in A,\Theta=\theta\right),

which were already defined in (
30
), recalling that
\mathcal{P_{IJ}}
denotes all possible partitions of
IJ
. Moreover, we recall that
\beta_{\theta}
has the representation

\displaystyle\beta_{\theta}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}_{IJ}})=\exp\left(-\lambda^{(IJ)}\left(({\bm{x}_{IJ}},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)\right)\prod_{l=1}^{L(\theta)}K_{\theta_{l}}\left({\bm{x}}_{\theta_{l}},\big\{{\bm{y}}_{IJ\setminus\theta_{l}}>{\bm{x}}_{IJ\setminus\theta_{l}}\big\}\right)\otimes_{l=1}^{L(\theta)}\lambda^{(\theta_{l})}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}_{\theta_{l}}),

where
\lambda^{(\theta_{l})}
denotes the distribution of the
\theta_{l}
margin of
\lambda
given by

\lambda^{(\theta_{l})}(\cdot):=\lambda\left(\{{\underline{\bm{x}}}\mid{\bm{x}}_{\theta_{l}}\in\cdot\}\right).

Intuitively,
\beta_{\theta}
can be viewed as the (non-normalized) distribution of
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
when the hitting scenario is fixed to
\theta
. Each
\beta_{\theta}
is absolutely continuous w.r.t. to the distribution of
{\bm{X}_{IJ}}
given by

\minid(\lambda^{(IJ)})=\sum_{\theta\in\mathcal{P}_{IJ}}\beta_{\theta}

with a density
\mathrm{d}\beta_{\theta}/\mathrm{d}\minid(\lambda^{(IJ)})
on
\mathbb{R}^{|IJ|}
. By
[
12
, Theorem 3.2.1]
the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
follows the distribution

\tau({\bm{X}_{IJ}},\theta)=P\left(\Theta=\theta\mid{\bm{X}}_{IJ}\right)=\frac{\mathrm{d}\beta_{\theta}}{\mathrm{d}\minid(\lambda^{(IJ)})}({\bm{X}_{IJ}}).

Thus, to derive the distribution of the conditional hitting scenario we have to find
\mathrm{d}\beta_{\theta}/\mathrm{d}\minid(\lambda^{(IJ)})
. Before turning to this task, we need to introduce further notation. For a fixed partition
\theta
of
IJ
define a partition
\Psi^{(i)}(\theta)
of
IJ\cap(\{i\}\times\mathbb{N})
via

\Psi^{(i)}(\theta):=\left(\Psi^{(i)}_{l}(\theta)\right)_{1\leq l\leq L(\Psi^{(i)})}:=\big\{\theta_{l}\cap(\{i\}\times\mathbb{N}))\mid 1\leq l\leq L(\theta);\theta_{l}\cap(\{i\}\times\mathbb{N})\neq\emptyset\big\},

which is the partition of the
i
-margin of
IJ
that is induced by
\theta
. Further, let

\displaystyle\Psi(\theta):=\left(\Psi^{(i)}(\theta)\right)_{i\in I(IJ)}

(44)

denote the collection of the partitions of the
i
-margins of
IJ
that are induced by
\theta
. To illustrate the notation consider
IJ=2\times 2
with
\theta=\big\{\{(1,1)\},\{(1,2),(2,1),(2,2)\}\big\}
. Then
\Psi^{(1)}(\theta)=\big\{\{(1,1)\},\{(1,2)\}\big\}
and
\Psi^{(2)}(\theta)=\big\{\{(2,1),(2,2)\}\big\}
.

From the stochastic representation (
42
) and by a similar reasoning as for deriving that
\lambda^{(IJ)}
concentrates on
\mathcal{U}
one can derive that

\beta_{\theta}(A)
concentrates on
d_{\Psi(\theta)}:=\left(\sum_{i\in I(IJ)}L(\Psi^{(i)})(\theta)\right)
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{|IJ|}
given by

\displaystyle G^{\Psi(\theta)}:=

\displaystyle\big\{{\bm{y}}_{IJ}\in\mathbb{R}^{|IJ|}\ \big|\ \text{ For all }\ i\in I(IJ)\text{ and }1\leq l\neq\underline{l}\leq L(\Psi^{(i)}(\theta)):\ y_{i,j}=y_{i,\underline{j}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\forall\ (i,j),(i,\underline{j})\in\Psi^{(i)}_{l}(\theta)\text{ and }y_{i,j}\neq y_{i,\underline{j}}\ \forall\ (i,j)\in\Psi^{(i)}_{l}(\theta),(i,\underline{j})\in\Psi^{(i)}_{\underline{l}}(\theta)\big\},

This can be seen as follows. Fixing the hitting scenario
\theta
implies that all indices
(i,j)
corresponding to the same subset
\theta_{l}
must stem from the same extremal sequence
{\underline{\bm{x}}}^{(k)}
whose only finite atom is
{\bm{b}}_{k}=(b_{k,i})_{{1\leq i\leq d}}
. Thus,
X_{i,j}=X_{i,\underline{j}}
for all
(i,j),(i,\underline{j})\in\theta_{l}
. By the same argument one obtains that
X_{i,j}\neq X_{i,\underline{j}}=b_{k,i}
whenever
(i,j)
and
(i,\underline{j})
are in distinct subsets of
\theta
.

The discussion above implies that the support of
\beta_{\theta}
is fully determined by
\Psi(\theta)
and all partitions
\theta
and
\tilde{\theta}
with
\Psi(\theta)=\Psi(\tilde{\theta})
induce the same support of
\beta_{\theta}
and
\beta_{\tilde{\theta}}
.
The following lemma formally proves these claims and also shows that
\beta_{\theta}
is singular to
\beta_{\tilde{\theta}}
whenever
\Psi(\theta)\neq\Psi(\tilde{\theta})

Lemma 12.3
.

Let
\theta
and
\tilde{\theta}
denote two partitions of
IJ
. We have that
\beta_{\theta}
is concentrated on
G^{\Psi(\theta)}
. Further,
\beta_{\theta}
is singular to
\beta_{\tilde{\theta}}
whenever
\Psi(\theta)\neq\Psi(\tilde{\theta})
.

Somewhat conversely, we will now show that
\beta_{\theta}
and
\beta_{\tilde{\theta}}
are absolutely continuous w.r.t. a common dominating measure whenever
\Psi(\theta)=\Psi(\tilde{\theta})
.

Proposition 12.4
.

Let
\theta=(\theta_{l})_{1\leq l\leq L(\theta)}
denote a partition of
IJ
with corresponding induced partitions
\Psi(\theta)=\left(\Psi^{(i)}(\theta)\right)_{i\in I(IJ)}=\left(\left(\Psi^{(i)}_{l}(\theta)\right)_{1\leq l\leq L(\Psi^{(i)}(\theta))}\right)_{i\in I(IJ)}

Then,

\beta_{\theta}(A)=\int_{A}q_{\theta}({\bm{b}}_{IJ})\mathrm{d}\left(\left(b_{i}^{(k)}\right)_{1\leq k\leq L(\Psi^{(i)}(\theta))}\right)_{i\in I(IJ)}

where

\displaystyle q_{\theta}({\bm{b}}_{IJ}):=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\lambda^{(IJ)}\left(({\bm{b}}_{IJ},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)\right)

(45)

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{L(\theta)}\Bigg(\int_{(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)^{|I(\theta_{l})^{\complement}|}}\left(\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a^{(l)}\delta_{({\bm{b}}_{\theta_{l}},{\bm{b}}^{(l)})(I(\theta_{l}))}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\Big(\{{\bm{x}}_{\theta_{l}}={\bm{b}}_{\theta_{l}},{\bm{x}}_{IJ\setminus\theta_{l}}>{\bm{b}}_{IJ\setminus\theta_{l}}\}\Big)\rho(a^{(l)},{\bm{b}}^{(l)})\mathrm{d}\left(a^{(l)},(b_{i}^{(l)})_{i\in I(\theta_{l})^{\complement}}\right)\Bigg)

(46)

with
{\bm{b}}_{IJ}:=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{L(\Psi^{(i)}(\theta))}b_{i}^{(k)}\mathbf{1}_{\{(i,j)\in\Psi^{(i)}_{k}\}}\right)_{(i,j)\in IJ}
and
{\bm{b}}_{\theta_{l}}:={\bm{b}}_{IJ\cap\theta_{l}}
.

With these tools at hand, we are ready to determine the conditional hitting scenarios with positive probability. It is crucial to observe that each observation of
{\bm{X}_{IJ}}
uniquely corresponds to a tie structure (partition)
\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}})
, which can be directly read from the data by grouping the indices of all observations in an
i
-margin that take identical values.
Formally,
\Psi^{(i)}({\bm{X}_{IJ}})
is the partition of
(\{i\}\times\mathbb{N})\cap IJ
induced by the equivalence relation

\displaystyle(i,j)\sim(i,\underline{j})\Leftrightarrow X_{i,j}=X_{i,\underline{j}}.

(47)

For example, when
IJ=2\times 2
and
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}=\left((1.5,2.3)^{\intercal},(3.7,2.3)^{\intercal}\right)
the corresponding tie structure
\Psi({\bm{X}}_{IJ})
is given by
\Psi^{(1)}({\bm{x}}_{IJ})=\big\{\{(1,1)\},\{(1,2)\}\big\}
and
\Psi^{(2)}({\bm{x}}_{IJ})=\big\{\{(2,1),(2,2)\}\big\}
.

It now follows from Lemma
12.3
that

\frac{\mathrm{d}\beta_{\theta}}{\mathrm{d}\minid\left(\lambda^{(IJ)}\right)}({\bm{X}_{IJ}})=0\ \minid\left(\lambda^{(IJ)}\right)\text{-almost-surely }\forall\ \theta\text{ with }\Psi(\theta)\neq\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}}).

Moreover, as all remaining conditional hitting scenarios
\tilde{\Theta}
with positive probability correspond to the same support
G^{\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}})}
, one can exploit the singularity again to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\beta_{\theta}}{\mathrm{d}\minid\left(\lambda^{(IJ)}\right)}({\bm{X}}_{IJ})=\frac{d\beta_{\theta}}{d\left(\sum_{\underset{\Psi(\tilde{\theta})=\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}})}{\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{P}_{IJ}}}\beta_{\tilde{\theta}}\right)}({\bm{X}}_{IJ})\mathbf{1}_{\{\Psi(\theta)=\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}})\}}.

(48)

Proposition
12.4
now implies that all
\beta_{\theta}
with
\Psi(\theta)=\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}})
have the
d_{\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}})}
-dimensional Lebesgue measure as their common dominating measure. This allows to determine

\mathrm{d}\beta_{\theta}/\mathrm{d}\minid\left(\lambda^{(IJ)}\right)
explicitly.

Corollary 12.5
.

Let
IJ\subset d\times\mathbb{N}
denote a finite set of indices and let
\mu
denote a CRM without base measure whose intensity measure is given by (
41
). Further, let
{\underline{\bm{X}}}
denote the corresponding exchangeable min-id sequence such that
({\bm{X}}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are conditionally i.i.d. with distribution
\minid(\mu)
. Then, the distribution of the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
of
IJ
of an mNTR prior with intensity (
41
) is given by

\displaystyle\tau({\bm{X}_{IJ}},\theta)=\frac{q_{\theta}}{\sum_{\overset{\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{P}_{IJ}}{\Psi(\theta)=\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}})}}q_{\tilde{\theta}}}\left({\bm{X}_{IJ}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\Psi(\theta)=\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}})\}}.

In practice, one needs to compute
|\{\theta\in\mathcal{P}_{IJ}\mid\Psi(\theta)=\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}})\}|
many non-zero probabilities of a conditional hitting scenarios, which is usually much smaller than the number of possible partitions
2^{|IJ|}
. In the univariate case
d=1
, there is only one conditional hitting scenario with positive probability since
\theta=\Psi({\bm{X}_{IJ}})
is unique, which explains why there is no analog of the conditional hitting scenario appearing in the classical posterior representations for univariate NTR priors in
[
16
,
9
]
.

Combining the results above one can deduce the posterior of an mNTR prior as stated in Theorem
12.1
at the beginning of this section.

13
Additional example of IDEM prior

Example 10

(Dependent NTR priors
[
14
,
29
]
)
.

Extending the framework of NTR priors to the multivariate setting,
[
14
,
29
]
couple vectors of cumulative hazards
\left(\left(H_{i}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}\right)_{{1\leq i\leq d}}
with the help of Lévy copulas, see e.g.
[
34
]
for a review on Lévy copulas. Essentially, they define marginal non-negative and non-decreasing driftless Lévy processes
\left(\left(\tilde{H}_{i}(t)\right)_{{1\leq i\leq d}}\right)_{t\geq 0}
, couple them with a suitable Lévy copula to a multivariate Lévy process, and then apply a uniform deterministic time-change
\gamma(\cdot)
to the multivariate Lévy process to obtain a multivariate additive process
\left(\left(H_{i}(t)\right)_{{1\leq i\leq d}}\right)_{t\geq 0}:=\left(\left(\tilde{H}_{i}(\gamma(t)))\right)_{{1\leq i\leq d}}\right)_{t\geq 0}
. Then, they define a prior on the random vector
{\bm{X}}\in(0,\infty)^{d}
in terms of its survival functions
S(t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}):=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\exp\left(-H_{i}(t_{i})\right)
.
Thus,
S
defines a min-id survival function, with exponent measure
\mu_{\mu_{1}^{(H_{1})},\ldots,\mu_{d}^{(H_{d})}}
, where
\mu_{i}^{(H_{i})}(-\infty,t]:=H_{i}(t)
. Moreover, it is easy to see that
\mu
is an IDEM prior without base measure.

To obtain the Lévy characteristics of
\mu
, consider a PRM
N
on
(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)^{d}
with intensity
\tilde{\nu}\mathrm{d}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d},b)=\gamma^{\prime}(b)\rho(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})\mathrm{d}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d},b)
, where
\rho
is the multivariate Lévy density that corresponds to the choice of Lévy copula and marginal Lévy processes. Then, the Lévy measure of
\mu
is given as the image measure of the map
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d},b)\mapsto\mu_{a_{1}\delta_{b},\ldots,a_{d}\delta_{b}}
under
\tilde{\nu}
. Thus, conditionally on
\mu
, each component of
{\bm{X}}
has the same set of atoms, but the probabilities associated with these atoms are different.

A similar argument to Example
7
shows that the conditional hitting scenarios of
[
14
,
29
]
can also be simply read off from the realizations of
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
by partitioning
d\times n
by the ties in the margins of the data.

14
Proofs of the results from the supplementary material

Proof of Lemma
10.1

Proof.

We start by proving the first statement.
Note that for a deterministic discrete measure
\eta
we have that
E_{\eta,\kappa}:\ x\mapsto\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa(s,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s\right)
is not differentiable everywhere. But as it contains a Lebesgue integral it is Lebesgue-almost-surely differentiable with Lebesgue-almost-sure derivative

-e_{\eta,\kappa}(x):=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa(x,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa(s,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s\right).

Unfortunately, in general this is not enough to apply Leibniz’ rule to interchange differentiation and expectation and we have to argue more rigorously. First, observe that for every
\eta
the function
E_{\eta,\kappa}
is absolutely continuous as a composition of the Lipschitz function
\exp(-\cdot)\mathbf{1}_{\{\cdot\geq 0\}}
and the absolute continuous function
\int_{0}^{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa(s,y)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s
. Thus, the only candidate for its density is
-e_{\eta,\kappa}(x)
and therefore we have for all
x\in[0,\infty]

1-\int_{0}^{x}e_{\eta,\kappa}(z)\mathrm{d}z=E_{\eta,\kappa}(x).

W.l.o.g. assuming that
J_{1}=\{1,\ldots,|J_{1}|\}
we can rewrite

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{j=1}^{|J_{1}|}-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)L_{\kappa,\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)}({\bm{x}}_{J_{1}},{\bm{x}}_{J_{2}})

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{|J_{1}|}-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\int_{\Omega}\prod_{j=1}^{|J_{1}|}\left(1-\int_{0}^{x_{j}}e_{\eta,\kappa}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{j}\right)\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\crm_{\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)}(\mathrm{d}\eta)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{|J_{1}|}-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\Bigg(\int_{\Omega}\prod_{j=2}^{|J_{1}|}\left(1-\int_{0}^{x_{j}}e_{\eta,\kappa}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{j}\right)\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\crm_{\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)}(\mathrm{d}\eta)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\int_{\Omega}e_{\eta,\kappa}(z_{1})\prod_{j=2}^{|J_{1}|}\left(1-\int_{0}^{x_{j}}e_{\eta,\kappa}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{j}\right)\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\crm_{\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\mathrm{d}z_{1}\Bigg),

where we could apply Fubini due to
e_{\eta,\kappa}\geq 0
and
1-\int_{0}^{x_{j}}e_{\eta,\kappa}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{j}\geq 0
. By the Lebesgue differentiation theorem the resulting function is now once Lebesgue-almost-surely differentiable w.r.t.
x_{1}
and we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{-\partial}{\partial x_{1}}\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\int_{\Omega}e_{\eta,\kappa}(z_{1})\prod_{j=2}^{|J_{1}|}\left(1-\int_{0}^{x_{j}}e_{\eta,\kappa}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{j}\right)\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\crm_{\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)}(\mathrm{d}\eta)\mathrm{d}z_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}e_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{1})\prod_{j=2}^{|J_{1}|}\left(1-\int_{0}^{x_{j}}e_{\eta,\kappa}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{j}\right)\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\crm_{\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)}(\mathrm{d}\eta)

as the
x_{1}
-Lebesgue-almost sure representation of the derivative. Now, we argue iteratively. Fixing an
x_{1}
such that the representation holds we repeat the argument to obtain

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{j=1}^{|J_{1}|}-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)L_{\kappa,l(a,b)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}b}({\bm{x}}_{J_{1}},{\bm{x}}_{J_{2}})=\int_{\Omega}\prod_{j=1}^{|J_{1}|}e_{\eta,\kappa}(z_{j})\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{\eta,\kappa}(x_{j})\crm_{\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)}(\mathrm{d}\eta)

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
-Lebesgue-almost everywhere, where we use that a Cartesian product of sets has Lebesgue measure
0
as soon as one of the sets in the product has Lebesgue measure
0
.

Let us turn to the second statement. Using the same arguments as above and using Proposition
3.2
, we get

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{j=1}^{|J_{1}|}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)-\log\left(L_{\kappa_{1},\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)}\left({\bm{x}}_{J_{1}},{\bm{x}}_{J_{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{|J_{1}|}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(1-\prod_{j=1}^{|J_{1}|}\left(1-\int_{0}^{x_{j}}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{j}\right)\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(x_{j})\right)\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{|J_{1}|}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{x_{1}}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(z_{1})\prod_{j=2}^{|J_{1}|}\left(1-\int_{0}^{x_{j}}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{j}\right)\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(x_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{1}\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)

\displaystyle+\left(\prod_{j=1}^{|J_{1}|}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(1-\prod_{j=2}^{|J_{1}|}\left(1-\int_{0}^{x_{j}}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{j}\right)\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(x_{j})\right)\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b),

where we could split the integral into two integrals because
|e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(z)|\leq C_{1}\min\{a,C_{2}\}
for all
z_{1}
in a neighborhood of
x_{1}
for some constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
and
\lim_{a\to 0}(1-e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(z))=1
by our assumptions on
\kappa
. This allows to apply Fubini and the Lebesgue differentiation theorem to obtain the partial derivative w.r.t.
x_{1}
given by

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{j=2}^{|J_{1}|}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(x_{1})\prod_{j=2}^{|J_{1}|}\left(1-\int_{0}^{x_{j}}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{j}\right)\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(x_{j})\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)

x_{1}
-Lebesgue-almost everywhere. Now, we can iterate the argument above to obtain that

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{j\in J_{1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)-\log\left(L_{\kappa_{1},\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)}\left(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|J_{1}|+1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{j=1}^{|J_{1}|}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(x_{j})\prod_{j\in J_{2}}E_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(x_{j})\gamma(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)

(49)

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
-Lebesgue-almost everywhere.

To conclude, we want to mention that for
x\in[0,\infty]
we can have
E_{\eta,\kappa}(x)=0
with positive probability. In this case the integral vanishes as well as all its partial derivatives. Thus, we can ignore this case in the calculations above and only focus on those cases where
E_{\eta,\kappa}(x)>0
almost surely. Moreover, the formulas are also correct when
\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa_{1}(s,y)\mathrm{d}s\eta(\mathrm{d}y)=\infty
with positive probability or
\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa_{1}(s,b)\mathrm{d}s=\infty
on a set of non-zero Lebesgue measure.
∎

Proof of Lemma
10.2

Proof.

Take
n
large enough s.t.
IJ\subset n\times d
.
By Lemma
5.3
and
6.1
, conditionally on the conditional hitting scenario
\tilde{\Theta}
and
{\bm{X}_{IJ}}
, the survival function of
\left({\bm{Z}}^{(n,l)}_{j}\right)_{1\leq j\leq k}
takes the form

\displaystyle P\left(\bm{Z}^{(n,l)}_{j}>{\bm{x}}_{j}\text{ for all }1\leq j\leq k\mid\tilde{\Theta},{\bm{X}_{IJ}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\underset{1\leq j\leq k}{\otimes}\minid(\eta)\left(\big\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k}\mid{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k}>{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k}\big\}\right)h(\eta,{\bm{X}_{IJ}},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)}{C({\bm{X}_{IJ}},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})},

where

h(\eta,{\bm{X}_{IJ}},\tilde{\Theta}_{l}):=\int_{\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{IJ\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}>{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{IJ\setminus\Theta_{l}}\}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{\eta}\left(({\bm{X}_{IJ}},{\bm{y}}_{IJ})(\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\right)\left(\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{nd-|\tilde{\Theta}_{l}|}\right)\left(\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{n\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}\right),

with
({\bm{X}_{IJ}},{\bm{y}}_{IJ})(\tilde{\Theta}_{l}):=\left(\mathbf{1}_{\{(i,j)\in\tilde{\Theta}_{l}\}}X_{i,j}+y_{i,j}\mathbf{1}_{\{(i,j)\not\in\tilde{\Theta}_{l}\}}\right)_{(i,j)\in IJ}

and

C({\bm{X}_{IJ}},\tilde{\Theta}_{l}):=\int_{M_{d}^{0}}h(\eta,{\bm{X}_{IJ}},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta).

Note that
h(\eta,{\bm{X}_{IJ}},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})
can take two forms given by

\displaystyle h(\otimes_{j=1}^{i-1}\delta_{\infty}\times(a\delta_{b})^{(\kappa_{i})}\otimes_{j=i+1}^{d}\delta_{\infty},{\bm{X}_{IJ}},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\{\tilde{\Theta}_{l}\subset i\times n\}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}e_{a\delta_{b},\kappa_{i}}(X_{i,j})\prod_{j:(i,j)\in IJ\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}E_{a\delta_{b},\kappa_{i}}(X_{i,j})

and

\displaystyle h(\mu_{\eta^{(\kappa_{1})}_{1},\ldots,\eta^{(\kappa_{d})}_{d}},{\bm{X}_{IJ}},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})=\prod_{(i,j)\in\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}e_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}(X_{i,j})\prod_{(i,j)\in IJ\setminus\tilde{\Theta}_{l}}E_{\eta_{i},\kappa_{i}}(X_{i,j}).

Thus, noticing that

\displaystyle\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\underset{1\leq j\leq k}{\otimes}\minid(\eta)\left(\big\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k}\mid{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k}>{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k}\big\}\right)h(\eta,{\bm{X}_{IJ}},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k}\mid{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k}>{\underline{\bm{x}}}_{k}\big\}}\int_{M_{d}^{0}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}f_{\eta}({\bm{y}}_{j})h(\eta,{\bm{X}_{IJ}},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})\nu(\mathrm{d}\eta)\Lambda^{(\infty)}_{kd}(\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{y}}}_{k})

and plugging in
f_{\eta}
and
h(\eta,{\bm{X}_{IJ}},\tilde{\Theta}_{l})
yields the result via a simple calculation of the integral.
∎

Proof of Lemma
10.4

Proof.

Proposition
3.2
implies

\displaystyle L_{\kappa,\rho(a)\mathrm{d}aq(b)\mathrm{d}b}({\underline{\bm{x}}}_{m})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(1-\exp\left(-a\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{0}^{x_{j}}\kappa(s,b)\mathrm{d}s\right)\right)\rho(a)q(b)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}b\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{x_{j-1}}^{x_{j}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(1-\exp\left(-a\tau_{1}\sum_{k=j}^{m}(x_{k}-b)\right)\right)\rho(a)q(b)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}b\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{x_{j-1}}^{x_{j}}\frac{\left(\tau_{1}\sum_{k=j}^{m}(x_{k}-b)+1\right)^{\sigma}-1}{\sigma}q(b)\mathrm{d}b\right)

where we have used that for
\sigma\in(0,1)
the following identity holds

\int_{0}^{\infty}(1-\exp(-\lambda x))x^{-1-\sigma}\exp\left(-\beta x\right)\mathrm{d}x=\Gamma(1-\sigma)\frac{(\beta+\lambda)^{\sigma}-\beta^{\sigma}}{\sigma}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
10.5

Lemma 14.1
.

Let
IJ\subset d\times\mathbb{N}
and
\tilde{IJ}\subset d\times\mathbb{N}
denote arbitrary index sets. Then, conditionally on
{\bm{X}}_{IJ}
, the exponent measure of
{\bm{Y}}^{(n)}_{\tilde{IJ}}
is defined via

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}^{(IJ)}_{n}\left(({\bm{x}}_{\tilde{IJ}},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\left(\tau_{1}\sum_{j:(i,j)\in\tilde{IJ}}(x_{j}-b)_{+}+\tau_{1}\sum_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}(X_{i,j}-b)_{+}+1\right)^{\sigma}-1}{\sigma}\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\left(\tau_{1}\sum_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}(X_{i,j}-b)_{+}+1\right)^{\sigma}-1}{\sigma}\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}\mathrm{d}b\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\zeta_{i}\left((x_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in\tilde{IJ}},(X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)l(a_{0},b_{0})\mathrm{d}a_{0}\mathrm{d}b_{0},

where

\displaystyle\zeta_{i}\left((x_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in\tilde{IJ}},(X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\{\tilde{IJ}\cap i\times\mathbb{N}\neq\emptyset\}}\bigg(L_{\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}\left((X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-L_{\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}\left((x_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in\tilde{IJ}},(X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle\ \ +\mathbf{1}_{\{\tilde{IJ}\cap i\times\mathbb{N}=\emptyset\}}L_{\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}\left((X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)

and we define
L_{\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}\left(\emptyset\right):=1
.

Proof of Lemma
14.1
.

Define

\displaystyle\zeta_{i}\left((x_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in\tilde{IJ}},(X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{1}_{\{\tilde{IJ}\cap i\times\mathbb{N}\neq\emptyset\}}\bigg(L_{\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}\left((X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-L_{\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}\left((x_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in\tilde{IJ}},(X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle\ \ +\mathbf{1}_{\{\tilde{IJ}\cap i\times\mathbb{N}=\emptyset\}}L_{\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}\left((X_{i,j})_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}\right)

and
L_{\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a(a_{0}\delta_{b_{0}})^{(\kappa_{0})}(\mathrm{d}b)}\left(\emptyset\right):=1
.
The exponent measure of
{\bm{Y}}^{(n)}_{\tilde{IJ}}
is uniquely defined by expressions of the form

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}^{(IJ)}_{n}\left(({\bm{x}}_{\tilde{IJ}},{\bm{\infty}}]^{\complement}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{defpostexpmeasure})}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(1-\mathbf{1}_{\{\tilde{IJ}\cap i\times\mathbb{N}\neq\emptyset\}}\prod_{j:(i,j)\in\tilde{IJ}}E_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(x_{i,j})\right)\prod_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}E_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(X_{i,j})\rho(a)\mathrm{d}a\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{1\leq i\leq d}\mathbb{E}_{\eta_{i}}\left[\left(1-\mathbf{1}_{\{\tilde{IJ}\cap i\times\mathbb{N}\neq\emptyset\}}\prod_{j:(i,j)\in\tilde{IJ}}E_{\eta_{i},\kappa}(x_{i,j})\right)\prod_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}E_{\eta_{i},\kappa}(X_{i,j})\right]l(a_{0},b_{0})\mathrm{d}a_{0}\mathrm{d}b_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(1-\mathbf{1}_{\{\tilde{IJ}\cap i\times\mathbb{N}\neq\emptyset\}}\prod_{j:(i,j)\in\tilde{IJ}}E_{a\delta_{b},\kappa}(x_{i,j})\right)\exp\left(-a\tau_{1}\sum_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}(X_{i,j}-b)_{+}\right)

\displaystyle a^{-1-\sigma}\exp(-a)\Gamma(1-\sigma)^{-1}\mathrm{d}a\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\zeta_{i}\left((x_{i,j})_{j:j\in\tilde{IJ}},(X_{i,j})_{j:j\in IJ}\right)l(a_{0},b_{0})\mathrm{d}a_{0}\mathrm{d}b_{0}

\displaystyle\overset{\star}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\left(\tau_{1}\sum_{j:j\in\tilde{IJ}}(x_{j}-b)_{+}+\tau_{1}\sum_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}(X_{i,j}-b)_{+}+1\right)^{\sigma}-1}{\sigma}\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\left(\tau_{1}\sum_{j:(i,j)\in IJ}(X_{i,j}-b)_{+}+1\right)^{\sigma}-1}{\sigma}\alpha_{0}^{(\kappa_{0})}\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\zeta_{i}\left((x_{i,j})_{j:j\in\tilde{IJ}},(X_{i,j})_{j:j\in IJ}\right)l(a_{0},b_{0})\mathrm{d}a_{0}\mathrm{d}b_{0},

where in
\star
we have again used that

\int_{0}^{\infty}(1-\exp(-\lambda x))x^{-1-\sigma}\exp\left(-\beta x\right)\mathrm{d}x=\Gamma(1-\sigma)\frac{(\beta+\lambda)^{\sigma}-\beta^{\sigma}}{\sigma}.

∎

Proof of Lemma

Proof of Proposition
11.2

Proof.

Since max-id random vectors are associated by
[
31
, Proposition 5.29]
and association is preserved under multiplication with
-1
we immediately obtain that the min-id random vector
{\underline{\bm{X}}}_{n}
is associated, conditionally on
\mu
and unconditionally.

To prove the remaining claim, recall that according to
[
31
, Proposition 5.30]
PRMs
N
on a complete and separable metric space
\mathbb{Y}
are associated, which is equivalent to the fact that for every collection of continuous functionals with compact support
\left(g_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq m}
we have
\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}g_{1}(y)N(\mathrm{d}y),\ldots,\int_{\mathbb{Y}}g_{m}(y)N(\mathrm{d}y)\right)
is an associated random vector. First, we reduce the problem to finite random measures on compact sets, since this will allow us to later invoke simpler arguments about continuous functionals on finite measures on compact sets. Thus, we w.l.o.g. consider finite IDRMs on
E_{K}:=E_{d}\cap K
, where
K
denotes the union of the compact supports of the
\left(f_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq m}
. Note that
E_{K}
is a complete and separable compact metric space and that
\mu(\cdot\cap K)
can be viewed as a finite IDRM on
E_{K}
. Therefore, it is enough to focus on finite random measure on
K
, denoted as
M_{d}(E_{K}):=\{\eta(\cdot\cap K)\mid\eta\in M_{d}\}
, which is again a separable and complete metric space when equipped with the vague topology. Moreover, as
\eta\mapsto\int_{E_{K}}f_{i}({\bm{x}})\eta(\mathrm{d}x)
is a continuous functional w.r.t. the vague topology on
M_{d}(E_{K})
, see
[
23
, p. 111]
, and we have that

\displaystyle\int_{E_{K}}f_{i}({\bm{x}})\mu(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\int_{E_{K}}f_{i}({\bm{x}})\alpha(\mathrm{d}x)+\int_{M_{d}(E_{K})}\int_{E_{K}}f_{i}({\bm{x}})\eta(\mathrm{d}x)N(\mathrm{d}\eta)

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}_{i}(\alpha)+\int_{M_{d}(E_{K})}\tilde{f}_{i}(\eta)N(\mathrm{d}\eta),

where
\tilde{f}_{i}(\eta):=\int_{E_{K}}f_{i}({\bm{x}})\eta(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})
and
N
denotes the PRM on
M_{d}(E_{K})
such that
\mu(\cdot\cap K)=\alpha(\cdot\cap K)+\int_{M^{0}_{d}}\eta(\cdot\cap E_{K})N(\mathrm{d}\eta)
.
Thus, we can write
\int_{E_{K}}f_{i}({\bm{x}})\mu(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})
as an integral of the PRM
N
over a non-negative continuous functional
\tilde{f}_{i}(\eta)=\int_{E_{K}}f_{i}({\bm{x}})\eta(\mathrm{d}x)
on
M_{d}(E_{K})
and we can ignore the influence of
\tilde{f}_{i}(\alpha)
when considering association, since association of a random vector is preserved under translation.

The remaining issue is that
\tilde{f}_{i}
does not necessarily have compact support on
M_{d}(E_{K})
, which is why have to introduce another approximation. Since
K
is compact the set
M_{a}:=\{\mu\mid\mu\left(K\right)<a\}
is an open set in the vague topology on
M_{d}(E_{K})
, see
[
31
, Exercise 3.4.11]
, and we have

\int_{M_{d}(E_{K})}\tilde{f}_{i}(\eta)N(\mathrm{d}\eta)=\lim_{a\to\infty}\int_{M_{d}(E_{K})}\tilde{f}_{i}(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\{\eta\in M_{a}\}}N(\mathrm{d}\eta)

by monotone convergence, using that
\mu(K)
is finite and that
N
is
\sigma
-finite. Moreover,
M_{a}
is relatively compact w.r.t. the vague topology on
M_{d}(E_{K})
, since every continuous function
h
on
K
assumes a finite maximum
m_{h}
on the compact set
K
. Thus
\sup_{\eta\in M_{a}}h(\eta)<am_{h}
for every continuous function
h
in
K
and by
[
31
, Proposition 3.16]

M_{a}
is relatively compact. Moreover,
[
31
, Proposition 3.11]
shows that
\mathbf{1}_{\{\eta\in M_{a}\}}
can be approximated from below by a sequence of monotone increasing continuous functions
h_{n,a}\in[0,1]
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}h_{n,a}(\eta)=\mathbf{1}_{\{\eta\in M_{a}\}}
. Thus, by monotone convergence, we have

\int_{M_{d}(E_{K})}\tilde{f}_{i}(\eta)N(\mathrm{d}\eta)=\lim_{a\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{M_{d}(E_{K})}\tilde{f}_{i}(\eta)h_{n,a}(\eta)N(\mathrm{d}\eta).

Now, the function
\tilde{f}_{i}(\eta)h_{n,a}(\eta)
is continuous with support contained in the relatively compact set
M_{a}
, thus has compact support on
M_{d}(E_{K})
. Therefore, by
[
31
, Proposition 5.30 iii]
,

G_{n,a}:=\left(\int_{M_{d}(E_{K})}\tilde{f}_{i}(\eta)h_{a}(\eta)N(\mathrm{d}\eta)\right)_{1\leq i\leq m}

is associated for every
(n,a)
. Since association is preserved under weak convergence by
[
31
, Lemma 5.32 iii]
, we obtain

G:=\left(\int_{E^{\prime}_{d}}f_{i}({\bm{x}})\mu(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}})\right)_{1\leq i\leq m}=\left(\tilde{f}_{i}(\alpha)+\int_{M_{d}(E_{K})}\tilde{f}_{i}(\eta)N(\mathrm{d}\eta)\right)_{1\leq i\leq m}

is associated if we can show that
G
can be expressed as a weak limit of a sequence of associated random vectors. First, observe that

\lim_{n\to\infty}G_{n,a}=\left(\tilde{f}_{i}(\alpha)+\int_{M_{d}(E_{K})}\tilde{f}_{i}(\eta)\mathbf{1}_{\{\eta\in M_{a}\}}N(\mathrm{d}\eta)\right)_{1\leq i\leq m}=:G_{\infty,a}.

Thus, an application of the dominated convergence theorem implies that the limit of Laplace transform of
\lim_{n\to\infty}G_{n,a}
is the Laplace transform of
G_{\infty,a}
. Thus,
G_{\infty,a}
is the weak limit of
G_{n,a}
and
G_{\infty,a}
is associated. Next, since
G=\lim_{a\to\infty}G_{\infty,a}
, another application of the dominated convergence theorem implies that the Laplace transform of
G
is the limit of the Laplace transform of
\lim_{a\to\infty}G_{\infty,a}
. Thus,
G_{a}
is the weak limit of
G_{\infty,a}
and
G
is associated, which proves the claim.
∎

Proof of Proposition
11.3

Proof.

Recall that every min-id random variable
X\sim\minid(\mu)
has the stochastic representation
X\sim\min_{i\in\mathbb{N}}x_{i}
for some PRM
N=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{x_{i}}
with intensity
\mu
. Thus,
I(f,\mu)
has the stochastic representation

\displaystyle I(f,\mu)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\minid(\mu)}\left[f(X)\right]\sim\mathbb{E}_{N\sim PRM(\mu)}\left[f\left(\min_{i\in\mathbb{N}}x_{i}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{N\sim PRM(\mu)}\left[\min_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f\left(x_{i}\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{N\sim PRM(\mu_{f})}\left[\min_{i\in\mathbb{N}}x_{i}\right]

\displaystyle\sim\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\minid(\mu_{f})}\left[X\right]=I(\text{id},\mu_{f}),

where we have used that
f
is monotone increasing and
\mu_{f}(\cdot)=\mu\left(\{x\in(-\infty,\infty]\mid f(x)\in\cdot\}\right)
denotes the IDEM obtained from the image measure of
\mu
under the transformation
f
. Further, due to Proposition
3.2
the Lévy characteristics of
\mu_{f}
are
(\alpha_{f},\nu_{f})
.

Next, let
\eta
denote an exponent measure on
[0,\infty]
. Then,

\displaystyle I(\text{id},\eta)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}x\minid(\eta)(\mathrm{d}x)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\minid(\eta)}\left[X\right]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\Big(-\eta\big((-\infty,t]\big)\Big)\mathrm{d}t,

where we have used that
\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]=\int_{0}^{\infty}1-P\left(X\leq t\right)\mathrm{d}t
for
X\in[0,\infty]
. Therefore, letting
m\in\mathbb{N}
we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[I(f,\mu)^{m}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(I\left(\text{id},\mu_{f}\right)\right)^{m}\right]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\Big(-\mu_{f}\big((-\infty,t]\big)\Big)\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{m}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}\ldots\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{f}\big((-\infty,t_{i}]\big)\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}t_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\ldots\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{f}\big((-\infty,t_{i}]\big)\right)\right]\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}t_{m}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\ldots\int_{0}^{\infty}L_{\alpha_{f},\nu_{f}}\big(\bm{1},(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})\big)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}t_{m}.

∎

Proof of Proposition
12.2

Proof.

Note that, even though for the posterior distribution we only need to consider
{\bm{x}}_{IJ}\in\mathbb{R}^{|IJ|}
, we must allows for
{\bm{x}}_{IJ}\in E^{\prime}_{|IJ|}
to verify that
K_{IJ}({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\cdot)
is the claimed conditional distribution of
\lambda^{(IJ)}
. Denote
J(IJ)=\{j\mid(i,j)\in IJ\text{ for some }{1\leq i\leq d}\}
and for some
J\subset J(IJ)
denote
J^{\complement}=J(IJ)\setminus J
. Define the set

\displaystyle H(J)

\displaystyle:=\{{\bm{y}}_{IJ}\in E_{|IJ|}^{\prime}\mid\forall{1\leq i\leq d}:\infty>y_{i,j}=y_{i,\underline{j}}\text{ for all }(i,j),(i,\underline{j})\in IJ\text{ with }j,\underline{j}\in J

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\text{ and }y_{i,j}=\infty\ \text{ for all }(i,j)\in IJ\text{ with }j\in J^{\complement}\},

which encodes that
{\bm{y}}_{IJ\cap(d\times J)}
is finite with identical values in its
i
-margins and that
{\bm{y}}_{IJ\cap(d\times J^{\complement})}={\bm{\infty}}
.
It is easy to see from (
43
) that the exponent measure
\lambda^{(IJ)}
concentrates on
\cup_{J\subset J(IJ)}H(J)
and that
H(J)\cap H(J^{\prime})=\emptyset
whenever
J\neq J^{\prime}
.

We need to verify (
12
) for the claimed representation of
K_{IJ}({\bm{x}_{IJ}},\cdot)
.
Let
\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}}:={\underline{\bm{x}}}_{\setminus IJ}
and consider an arbitrary
\mathsf{G}:(-\infty,\infty]^{|IJ|}\times(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}\to[0,\infty)
that vanishes on
\{{\bm{\infty}}\}\times(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}
. Using that
J=\emptyset
is not relevant since
\mathsf{G}\big({\bm{\infty}},\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}}\big)=0
we observe

\displaystyle\int_{E_{|IJ|}^{\prime}}\int_{(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}}\mathsf{G}({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})K_{IJ}({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\mathrm{d}\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})\lambda^{(IJ)}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}_{IJ})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq J\subset J(IJ)}\int_{H(J)}\int_{(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}}\mathsf{G}({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})K_{IJ}({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\mathrm{d}\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})\lambda^{(IJ)}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}_{IJ})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq J\subset J(IJ)}\int_{(0,\infty)\times(-\infty,\infty)^{d}}\int_{H(J)}\int_{(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}}\mathsf{G}({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})K_{IJ}({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\mathrm{d}\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\left(\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a\delta_{{\bm{b}}}\right)\right)^{(IJ)}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}_{IJ})\rho(a,{\bm{b}})\mathrm{d}(a,{\bm{b}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq J\subset J(IJ)}\int_{(0,\infty)\times(-\infty,\infty)^{d}}\int_{\{{\bm{y}}_{IJ}={\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J)\}}\int_{(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}}\mathsf{G}({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})K_{IJ}({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),\mathrm{d}\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\left(\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a\delta_{{\bm{b}}}\right)\right)^{(IJ)}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}_{IJ})\rho(a,{\bm{b}})\mathrm{d}(a,{\bm{b}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq J\subset J(IJ)}\int_{(0,\infty)\times(-\infty,\infty)^{d}}\int_{(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}}\mathsf{G}({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})K_{IJ}({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),\mathrm{d}\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})

\displaystyle\left(\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a\delta_{{\bm{b}}}\right)\right)^{(IJ)}\left(\{{\bm{y}}_{IJ}={\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J)\}\right)\rho(a,{\bm{b}})\mathrm{d}(a,{\bm{b}})=:\#_{1}

where
{\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J):=\left(b_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{j\in J\}}+\infty\mathbf{1}_{\{j\in J^{\complement}\}}\right)_{(i,j)\in IJ}
and

\displaystyle\left(\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a\delta_{{\bm{b}}}\right)\right)^{(IJ)}

(50)

denotes the distribution of the
IJ
-margin of an i.i.d. sequence with law
\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a\delta_{{\bm{b}}}\right)
. One should note that those
b_{i}
such that

i\not\in I(IJ,J):=\{i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\mid(i,j)\in IJ\text{ for some }j\in J\}

are integrated out because they do not appear in the function that is integrated. We thus obtain

\displaystyle\#_{1}=\sum_{\emptyset\neq J\subset J(IJ)}\int_{(-\infty,\infty)^{|I(IJ,J)|}}\ \int_{(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}}\mathsf{G}({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})K_{IJ}({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),\mathrm{d}\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\times_{i\in I(IJ,J)^{\complement}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a\delta_{{\bm{b}}}\right)\right)^{(IJ)}\left(\{{\bm{y}}_{IJ}={\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J)\}\right)\rho(a,{\bm{b}})\mathrm{d}(a,\left(b_{i}\right)_{i\in I(IJ,J)^{\complement}})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\mathrm{d}\left(\left(b_{i}\right)_{i\in I(IJ,J)}\right)=:\#_{2}

It remains to observe that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\times_{i\in I(IJ,J)^{\complement}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a\delta_{{\bm{b}}}\right)\right)^{(IJ)}\left(\{{\bm{y}}_{IJ}={\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J)\}\right)\rho(a,{\bm{b}})\mathrm{d}(a,\left(b_{i}\right)_{i\in I(IJ,J)^{\complement}})

is exactly equal to the denominator appearing in
K_{IJ}({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),\mathrm{d}\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})
since
I({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J))=\{i\mid(i,j)\in IJ\text{ for some }j\in J\}=I(IJ,J)
. Thus, the denominator cancels and we get

\displaystyle\#_{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\emptyset\neq J\subset J(IJ)}\int_{(-\infty,\infty)^{|I(IJ,J)|}}\ \int_{(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}}\mathsf{G}({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})

\displaystyle\left(\times_{i\in I({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J))^{\complement}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a\delta_{({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),{\bm{b}})(I({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J))}\right)\left(\{\tilde{\underline{\bm{y}}}\in\mathrm{d}\tilde{\underline{\bm{x}}},{\bm{y}}_{IJ}={\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J)\}\right)

\displaystyle\rho(a,({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),{\bm{b}})(I({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J)))\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}\left((b_{i})_{i\in I({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J))^{\complement}}\right)\mathrm{d}\left(\left(b_{i}\right)_{i\in I({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J))}\right)=:\#_{3},

where
\minid\left(a\delta_{({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),{\bm{b}})(I({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J))}\right)\left(\{\tilde{\underline{\bm{y}}}\in\mathrm{d}\tilde{\underline{\bm{x}}},{\bm{y}}_{IJ}={\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J)\}\right)
denotes that
\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}}
is integrated w.r.t. the (non-probability) measure
\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a\delta_{({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),{\bm{b}})(I({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J))}\right)\left(\{{\underline{\bm{y}}}\mid\tilde{{\underline{\bm{y}}}}\in\cdot\ ,{\bm{y}}_{IJ}={\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J)\}\right)

Now, we also note that we simply have
({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),{\bm{b}})(I({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J))={\bm{b}}
, which gives

\displaystyle\#_{3}=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{\emptyset\neq J\subset J(IJ)}\int_{(0,\infty)\times(-\infty,\infty)^{d}}\ \int_{(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}}\mathsf{G}({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a\delta_{{\bm{b}}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\left(\{\tilde{\underline{\bm{y}}}\in\mathrm{d}\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}},{\bm{y}}_{IJ}={\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J)\}\right)\rho(a,{\bm{b}})\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}{\bm{b}}

\displaystyle=\int_{(0,\infty)\times(-\infty,\infty)^{d}}\ \int_{(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}\setminus IJ}}\sum_{\emptyset\neq J\subset J(IJ)}\mathsf{G}({\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J),\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a\delta_{{\bm{b}}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\left(\{\tilde{\underline{\bm{y}}}\in\mathrm{d}\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}},{\bm{y}}_{IJ}={\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J)\}\right)\rho(a,{\bm{b}})\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}{\bm{b}}

\displaystyle\overset{\star}{=}\int_{(0,\infty)\times(-\infty,\infty)^{d}}\ \int_{(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}}}\mathsf{G}({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid\left(a\delta_{{\bm{b}}}\right)\left(\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{x}}}\right)\rho(a,{\bm{b}})\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}{\bm{b}}

\displaystyle=\int_{(-\infty,\infty]^{d\times\mathbb{N}}}\mathsf{G}({\bm{x}}_{IJ},\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}})\lambda(\mathrm{d}{\underline{\bm{x}}})

which proves the claim, where in
\star
we used the
\mathsf{G}\big({\bm{\infty}},\tilde{{\underline{\bm{x}}}}\big)=0
and that

\displaystyle\sum_{\emptyset\neq J\subset J(IJ)}\left(\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid(a\delta_{{\bm{b}}})\right)\left(A\cap\{{\bm{y}}_{IJ}={\bm{b}}_{IJ}(J)\}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\otimes_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\minid(a\delta_{{\bm{b}}})\right)\left(A\setminus\{{\bm{y}}_{IJ}={\bm{\infty}}\}\right).

∎

Proof of Lemma
12.3

Proof.

The central observation for the proof of the claims is that for a Poisson point process
\tilde{N}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(a_{k},{\bm{b}}_{k})}
whose intensity is given by (
41
) we have

P\left(\exists\ C\in(0,\infty)\text{ and }{1\leq i\leq d}\text{ such that }\tilde{N}\left((0,\infty)\times\{{\bm{b}}\in(0,\infty)^{d}\mid b_{i}=C\}\right)>1\right)=0.

To see this note that
\tilde{N}^{(i)}:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{b_{k,i}}
is a Poisson random measure on
(0,\infty)
with diffuse intensity
